Hilbert’s Entscheidungsproblem asked whether there could be a general mechanical procedure for deciding the truth or falsity of every mathematical statement in a specified formal system. A decision problem is a question with a finite set of possible answers, commonly yes or no. A problem is decidable if an algorithm is guaranteed to return the correct answer for every valid input and terminate after a finite number of steps. Turing showed that some well-defined decision problems have no such general procedure. His result did not merely say that a problem was too slow for available machines; it established a formal limit on what algorithms can decide. The chapter uses primality testing to illustrate a problem that does have a precise, terminating procedure.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between answering one mathematical question and proving that a single algorithm can decide every instance of a whole class of questions.
Key point:
- Decidability requires a correct procedure that terminates for every valid input.
- Turing demonstrated that some decision problems are not algorithmically decidable.
Source links:
- Turing (1937): On Computable Numbers
5. Q: What is a Turing machine, and why was it a major conceptual breakthrough?
A: Show answer
A Turing machine is an abstract mathematical model of a mechanical procedure. In its standard form it has a tape divided into cells, a read-and-write head, a finite set of internal states, and transition rules that determine what to write, how to move, and which state to enter next. The model is intentionally simple: it captures the logic of following exact instructions rather than the physical construction of a modern processor. Turing’s insight was to make the informal idea of a “recipe” precise enough to reason about all possible computations. Once computation could be described formally, it became possible to prove both what machines can calculate and what they cannot calculate.
Study instruction:
- Draw a small Turing machine and trace its state, tape symbol, and head movement for a short input sequence.
Key point:
- A Turing machine formalises computation as rule-governed state transitions.
- The model makes it possible to reason rigorously about the limits of algorithms.
Source links:
- Turing (1937): On Computable Numbers
6. Q: What are a universal Turing machine and the halting problem, and how are they related?
A: Show answer
A universal Turing machine can simulate any other Turing machine when given an encoded description of that machine and its input. This means one general-purpose machine can execute many different procedures by changing the program rather than changing the hardware. Turing also considered whether a machine could always determine if another machine would eventually halt. He showed that no general algorithm can correctly decide this for every program-input pair. The universal-machine idea explains the flexibility of programmable computers, while the halting result establishes a boundary on what such computers can decide. A particular program may still be analysed; the impossibility concerns a universal decision procedure for all cases.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a universal machine simulates another program, then state precisely what the halting-problem theorem rules out.
Key point:
- Universality explains why one computer can run many different programs.
- The halting problem shows that universal programmability does not imply universal solvability.
Source links:
- Turing (1937): On Computable Numbers
- Turing (1950): Computing Machinery and Intelligence
7. Q: How should computability be distinguished from computational complexity?
A: Show answer
Computability asks whether an algorithm exists that can solve a problem in principle. Complexity asks how much time or memory the algorithm requires as the input grows. The halting problem is undecidable because no general algorithm can solve every instance. Exhaustive search in a complex board game is a different kind of difficulty: a procedure can be described, but the number of possibilities can make it infeasible with available resources. A faster computer may help with an expensive but decidable problem; it cannot remove an undecidability result. This distinction is essential when evaluating claims that more hardware alone will solve a difficult AI problem.
Study instruction:
- Classify examples as undecidable, decidable but potentially intractable, or practically manageable, and justify the classification.
Key point:
- Computability concerns whether a solution procedure exists.
- Complexity concerns the resources needed to execute that procedure.
Source links:
- Turing (1937): On Computable Numbers
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
8. Q: How did abstract computation become practical electronic computing?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes several parallel engineering developments. Konrad Zuse designed the Z3 in Germany during the Second World War; a team led by John Mauchly and J. Presper Eckert developed ENIAC in the United States; and Fred Williams and Tom Kilburn built the Manchester Baby in post-war Britain. The chapter also discusses Turing’s work at Manchester and the Ferranti Mark 1. These machines differed in purpose and architecture, but collectively demonstrated that abstract computational ideas could be realised in physical equipment. Historical “first computer” claims depend on the criteria used, such as electronic operation, programmability, stored-program design, or commercial availability, so comparisons should be made with care.
Study instruction:
- Compare the Z3, ENIAC, Manchester Baby, and Ferranti Mark 1 by purpose, historical setting, and contribution to programmable computing.
Key point:
- Modern computing emerged through several related engineering efforts.
- Claims about the first computer depend on the technical definition being applied.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
9. Q: What does it mean to say that a computer is a machine for following instructions?
A: Show answer
A conventional computer executes operations encoded in a program. At a low level, these operations include arithmetic, moving data, comparing values, branching when conditions are met, and repeating steps. Complex applications are built from combinations of such elementary operations. The user sees a word processor, game, search engine, or phone application, but the processor executes instructions and manipulates data rather than directly understanding the user’s purpose. This is not a dismissal of computing; it explains how general-purpose machinery can support sophisticated applications. The AI challenge is to determine how intelligent behaviour can be produced through programs, representations, and learned parameters that ultimately run on computational hardware.
Study instruction:
- Trace a familiar application action into the kinds of elementary operations a computer must perform.
Key point:
- Software translates complex tasks into executable operations.
- Executing instructions does not by itself establish human-like understanding.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
10. Q: Why do programming languages use abstraction, and why can programs still fail?
A: Show answer
Languages such as Python, Java, and C hide many low-level processor and memory details so people can express procedures more easily. This abstraction reduces the effort required to write and maintain software, but it does not remove the need for precise specifications. Programs can fail because requirements are ambiguous, algorithms are flawed, data are incorrect, components interact unexpectedly, or hardware and operating conditions are unreliable. A computer may execute its instructions consistently while producing an incorrect result because the instructions themselves do not represent the intended task. The chapter therefore distinguishes the reliability of the processor from the correctness of the program and the suitability of the underlying design.
Study instruction:
- Analyse a software failure by separating specification errors, coding defects, data problems, integration issues, hardware faults, and operational causes.
Key point:
- Programming languages simplify expression through abstraction.
- Reliable execution cannot compensate for a flawed specification, algorithm, or input.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
11. Q: What do computer speed, reliability, decision-making, and learning demonstrate about the nature of computation?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasises that computers can execute huge numbers of simple operations rapidly and consistently. A program can also make operational decisions by testing conditions and selecting branches. A learning system can change later behaviour by executing an algorithm that adjusts its internal parameters using data or feedback. These capabilities remain computational: the machine follows procedures that specify how to calculate, choose, or update. The chapter’s speed figures are historical illustrations rather than current hardware benchmarks. More importantly, speed and reliability do not guarantee that the task has been represented correctly or that the system will generalise beyond the conditions for which it was designed or trained.
Study instruction:
- Explain how an if-then rule differs from a learning update, and identify what each requires from its programmer or designer.
Key point:
- Computers can make choices and learn while remaining computational systems.
- Fast, reliable execution does not guarantee sound decisions or broad competence.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
12. Q: Why are some AI tasks easy to program while others are difficult?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies two broad sources of difficulty. In the first, a procedure is known but too expensive to run at a useful scale; exhaustive search in board games is the example. In the second, there is no adequate explicit recipe for the task, as with recognising faces or understanding speech in varied real-world conditions. The first category motivates better algorithms, heuristics, and efficient computation. The second motivates learning methods that extract patterns from examples. Real applications can combine both categories: a system may learn to interpret an image and then search for an action that satisfies constraints. The distinction helps explain why no single technique solves every AI problem.
Study instruction:
- Classify arithmetic, sorting, chess search, face recognition, and story comprehension under the two categories, noting where a task combines them.
Key point:
- Some problems are difficult because computation is too expensive.
- Others are difficult because the required procedure or representation is not obvious.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
13. Q: Why are arithmetic and sorting useful examples of tasks that computers can handle well?
A: Show answer
Basic arithmetic has precise operations that can be expressed as algorithms, so computers can execute them quickly and consistently. Sorting also has a clear goal, such as arranging numbers in ascending order, but an obvious procedure may be too slow for large lists. The chapter uses Quicksort, developed by Tony Hoare in 1959, to illustrate how algorithm design can make a known task practical. These examples show that ease of specification and efficiency are separate issues. They also reveal a limit of the analogy: arithmetic execution is not the same as choosing an appropriate mathematical model or understanding the meaning of a problem in its real-world context.
Study instruction:
- Compare a simple sorting procedure with a partition-based method and explain why algorithmic efficiency matters as data volume increases.
Key point:
- Well-specified tasks can often be implemented directly as algorithms.
- Practical performance depends on the efficiency of the chosen algorithm.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
14. Q: Why does board-game search illustrate the difference between a simple recipe and a practical AI solution?
A: Show answer
A board game has explicit rules and legal moves, so a program can generate possible future positions and search for a favourable sequence. The problem is that the number of possible continuations grows rapidly with each additional move. Exhaustively exploring every possibility can require more time and memory than a practical computer can provide. AI methods therefore guide search, evaluate positions, and prioritise promising branches. These methods do not necessarily prove that a selected move is globally optimal; they make strong decisions possible within a resource budget. The chapter uses games to demonstrate that a problem can be well-defined and still be computationally formidable.
Study instruction:
- Draw a small game tree and compare exhaustive search with a strategy that explores only selected branches.
Key point:
- A clear set of rules does not make exhaustive search feasible.
- Heuristics and evaluation methods trade exhaustive certainty for practical performance.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
15. Q: Why does machine learning help with face recognition, translation, and speech recognition?
A: Show answer
These tasks involve highly varied inputs that are difficult to cover with a complete set of hand-written rules. Machine learning provides another route: a system is trained on examples to identify patterns that are useful for producing a result. A face model may learn visual features, a speech model may learn relationships between acoustic signals and words, and a translation model may learn statistical relationships between language sequences. The chapter presents these as areas where learning methods made substantial progress. This should not be interpreted as a claim that the tasks are solved perfectly. Performance can vary with data quality, accents, lighting, context, language variety, and unfamiliar conditions.
Study instruction:
- For each task, identify its training data, target output, likely sources of bias, and conditions that should be included in an independent test set.
Key point:
- Learning can capture patterns that are difficult to specify manually.
- Success on a benchmark does not guarantee reliable performance in every real-world setting.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
16. Q: Why does autonomous driving expose the difficulty of perception and scene understanding?
A: Show answer
A driverless vehicle must interpret a dynamic scene containing cars, cyclists, pedestrians, road markings, signs, construction, and uncertain weather or visibility. It needs to estimate where it is, identify objects, infer their movement, and understand how they relate to one another. Only then can its planning system choose whether to brake, accelerate, turn, or wait. The chapter argues that deciding what action to take may be simpler than correctly understanding the surrounding situation. In practice, safe operation requires perception, prediction, planning, control, redundancy, and extensive validation. A single object-recognition score cannot establish that an autonomous vehicle is safe in all relevant conditions.
Study instruction:
- Analyse a busy intersection and separate perception, prediction, planning, control, and safety requirements.
Key point:
- Autonomous driving is difficult because the environment is dynamic and safety-critical.
- Sound action depends on reliable scene interpretation as well as planning.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
17. Q: Why are story comprehension and picture interpretation harder than simple recognition?
A: Show answer
Recognising an object or a word is narrower than understanding what is happening. A story may require tracking events over time, inferring motives, resolving references, identifying unstated causes, and understanding what a character believes. Picture interpretation may require recognising relationships and actions rather than merely labelling objects. These tasks depend on background knowledge that people rarely state explicitly. The chapter’s examples show that a system may identify the people in an image yet fail to explain their activity, or translate individual words while missing the meaning of a narrative. Rich understanding therefore requires combining evidence across a context rather than treating each input element independently.
Study instruction:
- Take a short narrative or scene description and separate explicit facts from conclusions that depend on background knowledge or inference.
Key point:
- Recognition of components is not the same as understanding their relationships.
- Rich interpretation relies on context and common-sense knowledge.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
18. Q: What is the Turing test, and what does it evaluate?
A: Show answer
In the familiar text-based version, a human interrogator exchanges written questions and answers with an unseen participant that may be a person or a computer program. The interrogator does not know which is which and must judge from the responses. If the program cannot reliably be distinguished from a human under the test conditions, it has met the behavioural criterion associated with the test. Turing’s proposal reframed the vague question “Can machines think?” into a more operational question about observable conversational behaviour. The test therefore evaluates what a system does in interaction, not whether its internal code or architecture resembles a human mind.
Study instruction:
- Diagram the participants, communication channel, information available to the judge, and criterion used to decide whether the machine is distinguishable.
Key point:
- The Turing test operationalises intelligence through linguistic behaviour.
- It evaluates observable outputs rather than directly inspecting internal mental states.
Source links:
- Turing (1950): Computing Machinery and Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
19. Q: How does Turing’s original imitation game differ from the simplified modern account of the Turing test?
A: Show answer
Turing’s 1950 article begins with an imitation game involving a man, a woman, and an interrogator who tries to identify them through written answers. Turing then asks what happens when a machine takes the place of one participant. Later descriptions often simplify the arrangement to a judge distinguishing a human from a computer program. The enduring principle is the use of controlled interaction to assess whether a machine’s responses are distinguishable from human responses. The historical distinction matters because the simplified version is not a verbatim description of the original game. Accurate study should separate Turing’s initial thought experiment from the variants developed later.
Study instruction:
- Read the opening of Turing’s article and explain how the original imitation game is adapted to the machine question.
Key point:
- The modern Turing test derives from, but simplifies, the original imitation game.
- Historical accuracy requires distinguishing the original setup from later versions.
Source links:
- Turing (1950): Computing Machinery and Intelligence
20. Q: Why is the Turing test a black-box test, and what are its limitations?
A: Show answer
The interrogator observes the questions and answers but does not inspect the program’s source code, internal states, training data, or physical construction. The system is treated as a black box, and the evidence comes from its input-output behaviour. This avoids requiring agreement about consciousness before a behavioural experiment can be conducted. It also limits the conclusion: two systems with very different internal mechanisms may produce similar answers in one setting, while a system that performs well in a narrow conversation may fail on unfamiliar tasks. The test cannot, by itself, establish subjective experience, general reasoning, or broad competence beyond the interaction that was examined.
Study instruction:
- State what a successful black-box test supports and list three claims about internal mental properties that would require separate evidence.
Key point:
- Behavioural tests assess observable performance without inspecting internal mechanisms.
- Similar conversational outputs do not prove identical understanding or consciousness.
Source links:
- Turing (1950): Computing Machinery and Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
21. Q: How did ELIZA work, and why is it an important example in the chapter?
A: Show answer
ELIZA, developed by Joseph Weizenbaum in the 1960s, used scripts to identify keywords or patterns in a user’s text and generate replies through associated transformation rules. Its well-known therapist-style script returned questions that encouraged the user to continue speaking. The exchange could feel meaningful even though the program did not maintain a rich model of the user’s circumstances or understand the dialogue as a person would. The chapter uses ELIZA to show how a simple response strategy can create an impression of comprehension when users supply context and interpret generic replies generously. Weizenbaum’s paper describes keyword detection, decomposition rules, and response reassembly.
Study instruction:
- Trace an ELIZA-style exchange and identify the keyword, matching rule, and response pattern that produced the appearance of continuity.
Key point:
- ELIZA generated dialogue through scripted text transformations.
- A convincing conversational style can conceal limited underlying capability.
Source links:
- Weizenbaum: ELIZA—A Computer Program for Natural Language Communication
22. Q: What do the ELIZA effect and the Loebner Prize example reveal about conversational AI evaluation?
A: Show answer
The ELIZA effect is the tendency to attribute more understanding, intention, or empathy to a program than its mechanisms justify. The chapter also describes the Loebner Prize, launched in 1990, as a competition in which programs attempted to persuade judges that they were human. It became controversial because superficial strategies could exploit the format without demonstrating robust comprehension or common sense. Together, these examples show that people may overinterpret fluent output and that benchmarks can reward behaviour that manipulates the test rather than demonstrates the intended capability. Evaluation should therefore specify what is measured, use varied tasks, record failures, and guard against predictable tricks.
Study instruction:
- Identify how a chatbot might exploit a narrow judging protocol and propose controls that would make the evaluation more informative.
Key point:
- Users may project human understanding onto systems that produce fluent language.
- A benchmark can be gamed unless its construct and controls are carefully designed.
Source links:
- Weizenbaum: ELIZA—A Computer Program for Natural Language Communication
- Turing (1950): Computing Machinery and Intelligence
23. Q: What are Winograd schemas, and what aspect of intelligence do they probe?
A: Show answer
Winograd schemas are short pairs of sentences that differ by a small change in wording but require different interpretations of an ambiguous reference. The chapter’s examples ask whether “they” refers to councillors or demonstrators, and whether a suitcase or trophy is too small or too large. Correct interpretation requires more than matching words: the system must use grammar and ordinary background knowledge to infer the likely referent. The challenge was proposed as an alternative to long conversational tests that can be manipulated by superficial tricks. It probes a focused form of commonsense language reasoning, although success on a fixed set of schemas would not prove general intelligence.
Study instruction:
- Construct a minimal pair that changes one meaningful word and explain how that change reverses the correct interpretation.
Key point:
- Winograd schemas test context-sensitive language interpretation and common-sense inference.
- A targeted benchmark measures a specific capability, not intelligence in every domain.
Source links:
- Morgenstern, Davis, and Ortiz: Winograd Schema Challenge Evaluation
24. Q: What does the “I’m leaving you” and “Who is she?” dialogue show about social reasoning?
A: Show answer
The exchange depends on information that is not explicitly stated. A listener infers that Bob and Alice are probably in a relationship, that Bob’s announcement means a separation, and that Alice suspects another woman may be involved. The pronoun “she” has no explicit antecedent in the two lines, so the listener supplies a plausible social explanation from background knowledge about relationships and breakups. The inference is reasonable but not logically certain; other contexts could produce a different interpretation. The example demonstrates how people use beliefs, desires, motives, and social conventions to understand short conversations, abilities that a purely literal language parser may not reproduce.
Study instruction:
- Separate the dialogue’s explicit statements from the assumptions used to infer Alice’s likely meaning, and identify at least one alternative context.
Key point:
- Everyday dialogue often relies on unstated social knowledge.
- Understanding requires inference while retaining awareness of ambiguity.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
25. Q: What is the difference between strong AI and weak AI in the chapter?
A: Show answer
In the chapter’s terminology, strong AI aims at programs that genuinely possess understanding or other mental attributes in a human-like sense. Weak AI aims to produce systems that demonstrate relevant capabilities without claiming that they possess those attributes. The distinction separates outward performance from a stronger claim about what is happening internally. Terminology can vary across philosophical discussions, so the definition being used should always be stated. The Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy similarly associates strong AI with artificial persons and phenomenal consciousness, while weak AI concerns information-processing machines that display a human-like mental repertoire or behaviour. Neither label should be inferred merely from a system’s marketing description.
Study instruction:
- Define both terms using the chapter’s account, then compare them with the formulation in the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy.
Key point:
- Strong AI makes a claim about genuine mental properties.
- Weak AI focuses on functional or behavioural capability without settling consciousness.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
- Turing (1950): Computing Machinery and Intelligence
26. Q: How are artificial general intelligence and narrow AI different, and is AGI identical to strong AI?
A: Show answer
Artificial general intelligence refers to broad intellectual capability across many kinds of tasks, potentially including language, reasoning, problem-solving, and perception at a level comparable to a typical person. Narrow AI refers to systems designed for particular tasks or bounded task families, such as image recognition, translation, or game playing. A system can be highly capable in a narrow domain without transferring that competence to unrelated tasks. AGI is not identical to strong AI: AGI concerns the breadth and flexibility of capability, while strong AI concerns whether a machine genuinely has mental properties such as consciousness. A machine could display broad competence without resolving the philosophical question of subjective experience.
Study instruction:
- Compare a specialist game-playing system, a general-purpose assistant, and a hypothetical AGI by task breadth, transfer ability, and claims about consciousness.
Key point:
- AGI concerns generality; narrow AI concerns bounded task capability.
- Strong AI is a separate philosophical claim about mental properties.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
27. Q: Why is AGI not the main focus of much practical AI research?
A: Show answer
The chapter observes that researchers often work on specific problems whose objectives and performance can be measured, such as recognition, translation, planning, and game playing. Building a system with the full range of human intellectual abilities is a much broader and less clearly defined goal. It raises difficult questions about what counts as general intelligence and how it should be tested. This does not make AGI unimportant; it explains why much day-to-day research is organised around tractable applications. The distinction also prevents a strong result in one domain from being presented as proof that the broader AGI objective has been achieved.
Study instruction:
- Compare the evaluation requirements for one narrowly defined AI application with those for a system claimed to have general intelligence.
Key point:
- Specific tasks provide clearer engineering targets than the full ambition of AGI.
- Progress in one application is not proof of general-purpose intelligence.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
28. Q: What is symbolic AI, and why did it become influential?
A: Show answer
Symbolic AI represents objects, properties, relationships, goals, and actions with symbols that a system manipulates according to explicit rules. The chapter’s example uses a symbol for a room and another for the action of cleaning it, allowing a robot to reason about a proposed action. This approach attempts to model aspects of conscious reasoning and problem-solving by making representations and inference steps explicit. It was influential from the mid-1950s into the late 1980s because it offered a transparent way to represent knowledge and inspect decisions. Its apparent resemblance to deliberate human reasoning also made it intuitively attractive to early AI researchers.
Study instruction:
- Represent a simple planning task using symbols for objects, states, actions, preconditions, and expected outcomes.
Key point:
- Symbolic AI models reasoning through explicit representations and rules.
- Its transparency depends on whether the chosen symbols adequately describe the task.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
29. Q: What are the strengths and limitations of symbolic AI?
A: Show answer
Symbolic systems can be useful when knowledge is structured and rules can be specified clearly. Their explicit representations can make some reasoning paths easier to inspect, explain, debug, and audit. However, a system may fail when it encounters noisy perceptual inputs, incomplete information, or situations that its designers did not anticipate. A large rule base can also become difficult to maintain, and common-sense knowledge is hard to enumerate exhaustively. These limitations help explain why learning-based methods became important alongside symbolic approaches. The comparison is not that symbolic reasoning is useless, but that explicit rules alone may be insufficient for tasks involving uncertainty, perception, and flexible adaptation.
Study instruction:
- Assess whether a rule-based system, a learned model, or a combination is most appropriate for image recognition, compliance checking, and robot navigation.
Key point:
- Explicit rules support structured reasoning and inspection.
- Perception, uncertainty, and unforeseen cases can expose the limits of rule-only systems.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
30. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish modelling the mind from modelling the brain?
A: Show answer
Modelling the mind means attempting to represent processes such as conscious reasoning, planning, and problem-solving, which the chapter associates with symbolic AI. Modelling the brain means taking inspiration from the biological structures that support cognition, an approach associated with neural networks. A complete simulation of a human brain would be vastly more ambitious than building a simplified artificial network because it would require detailed knowledge of an extraordinarily complex biological system. The chapter argues that researchers can instead borrow selected principles from neural structures without reproducing the entire brain. The distinction is about the starting point for engineering, not a claim that mind and brain are unrelated.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between representing reasoning with symbols and building a simplified network inspired by neural processing.
Key point:
- Symbolic AI takes the mind and explicit reasoning as its model.
- Neural approaches take inspiration from brain structure without requiring a full brain replica.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
31. Q: What are artificial neural networks, and why are they not literal copies of human brains?
A: Show answer
Artificial neural networks consist of interconnected computational units that transform signals and adjust numerical parameters during learning. Their design is inspired in a broad sense by biological neurons and connections, but the units and learning procedures are simplified mathematical constructs. A network can learn a mapping from inputs to outputs by adjusting weights in response to data and an objective function. It may perform image or speech tasks without reproducing the full biological processes involved in human perception, memory, emotion, or consciousness. The chapter contrasts this approach with the impractical ambition of simulating an entire brain and stresses that neural networks are only one way to take inspiration from biology.
Study instruction:
- Draw a network with input, hidden, and output layers, and explain how changing weights alters its predictions.
Key point:
- Neural networks learn patterns through computations over interconnected units and adjustable parameters.
- Biological inspiration does not make an artificial network a human brain replica.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
32. Q: How should the chapter’s comparison of symbolic AI and neural networks guide the evaluation of AI systems?
A: Show answer
Symbolic methods emphasise explicit representations and rules, while neural networks learn distributed patterns from data. Symbolic approaches can make some reasoning paths easier to inspect; neural approaches can be effective with complex inputs such as images, audio, and text. Neither is universally superior. Practical systems may combine learned perception with explicit constraints, structured knowledge, or rule-based checks. Evaluation should therefore begin with the task, evidence, operating conditions, need for explanation, and likely failure modes rather than loyalty to one paradigm. The chapter’s overall lesson is to distinguish task-specific success from generality, and observable capability from claims about genuine understanding or consciousness.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate one AI application by recording its task boundary, method, evidence, generalisation tests, explanation needs, and failure modes.
Key point:
- Symbolic and neural approaches offer different strengths and can be combined.
- Assess demonstrated capability carefully; do not infer general intelligence or consciousness from a narrow result.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
- Turing (1950): Computing Machinery and Intelligence
Rows: 32
Table 3
The Golden Age of Artificial Intelligence: Early Systems, Search, and Complexity
1. Q: What defined the golden age of artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
The golden age was the first major period of organized AI research, approximately 1956 to 1974. It followed the emergence of AI as a named academic discipline and was characterized by optimism, rapid institution-building, experimental systems, and the belief that individual intelligent capabilities could soon be engineered. Researchers demonstrated early programs for language interaction, planning, problem solving, and robotics. The period was not a sustained success story: its achievements were often narrow, dependent on simplified environments, and difficult to scale. By the mid-1970s, technical limits and unmet expectations produced a severe loss of confidence and funding.
Study instruction:
- Place the golden age on a timeline from the 1956 Dartmouth meeting to the funding and confidence crisis of the mid-1970s.
Key point:
- The golden age established AI as a research discipline and produced influential methods.
- Its achievements were real but narrower than contemporary predictions suggested.
2. Q: Why was Alan Turing's early contribution initially isolated from an AI research community?
A: Show answer
Turing's work on machine intelligence, including the imitation-game proposal later known as the Turing test, appeared before AI existed as a recognized discipline. There was no shared field name, established research community, or body of working AI systems. His contribution was therefore a substantial conceptual and scientific starting point rather than part of a coordinated program. The chapter distinguishes this early intellectual foundation from the institutional formation that occurred about a decade later, when researchers began building systems intended to demonstrate components of intelligent behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the existence of an important research idea from the existence of an organized discipline with institutions, researchers, and experimental systems.
Key point:
- A field requires more than a pioneering idea.
- AI became an organized discipline only after a research community and experimental agenda emerged.
3. Q: Why is the 1956 Dartmouth summer project treated as a conventional starting point for modern AI?
A: Show answer
The 1955 funding proposal for a summer research meeting at Dartmouth used the term artificial intelligence, chosen by John McCarthy. The meeting brought together researchers interested in aspects of machine intelligence and helped establish a recognizable research identity. The participants did not achieve the sweeping progress McCarthy had hoped for during that summer, but the name persisted and the meeting became a focal point for the emerging discipline. Its historical importance lies more in naming and consolidating a field than in delivering a finished intelligent system.
Study instruction:
- Explain why an event can be historically foundational even when its immediate technical output is limited.
Key point:
- Dartmouth supplied a durable name and focal point for the field.
- The meeting's symbolic and institutional influence exceeded its immediate technical results.
4. Q: What role did John McCarthy play in the formation of artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
McCarthy proposed the name artificial intelligence for the Dartmouth summer research project and became one of the central figures in the new discipline. He also developed LISP, a programming language that became closely associated with AI research for decades. The chapter presents McCarthy as an example of a researcher whose contributions shaped both the intellectual identity of AI and the practical tools used to investigate it. His work helped create a continuing research tradition through laboratories, students, and academic descendants.
Study instruction:
- Connect McCarthy's naming of the discipline with his technical contribution to its research infrastructure.
Key point:
- McCarthy helped define AI as a field and supplied an influential programming language.
- The discipline developed through both ideas and enabling tools.
5. Q: Why was LISP important to early artificial intelligence research?
A: Show answer
LISP became a preferred programming language for AI researchers because it supported the kinds of symbolic and structured computation used in early experiments. McCarthy invented it in the mid-1950s, and the chapter notes its remarkable longevity. Its continued use illustrates how foundational technical choices can persist long after the computing environment that produced them has changed. The chapter also uses LISP's distinctive syntax to illustrate the unusual programming culture of early AI laboratories.
Study instruction:
- Review why programming languages matter to a research field, then explain why LISP became associated with early AI.
Key point:
- LISP was an important practical tool in the early AI research ecosystem.
- Long-lived tools can outlast the hardware and research conditions in which they originated.
6. Q: Which researchers and institutions shaped the early AI research landscape?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights four influential figures associated with the Dartmouth meeting and the subsequent growth of AI: John McCarthy, Marvin Minsky, Allen Newell, and Herbert Simon. McCarthy established an AI laboratory at Stanford, Minsky founded the AI laboratory at MIT, and Newell and Simon were associated with Carnegie Mellon University. Their laboratories, students, and academic descendants helped sustain research across decades. The development of AI was therefore not only a sequence of isolated inventions; it also depended on institutions capable of training researchers and maintaining long-running programs.
Study instruction:
- Associate each named researcher with the laboratory or institution identified in the chapter, and consider how research schools perpetuate methods.
Key point:
- Early AI was shaped by influential laboratories and research lineages.
- Institution-building helped turn isolated ideas into a continuing discipline.
7. Q: Why did early AI researchers adopt a divide-and-conquer strategy?
A: Show answer
General intelligence was too broad and poorly defined to attack as a single engineering problem. Researchers therefore separated it into component capabilities such as perception, learning, planning, problem solving, reasoning, and natural-language understanding. They built experimental systems to demonstrate individual capabilities, assuming that the components could eventually be assembled into a more general intelligent system. This strategy made research problems more manageable, but it also encouraged an assumption that integration would be easier than it proved to be.
Study instruction:
- List the component capabilities and explain the strategic reason for decomposing a broad research goal.
Key point:
- Decomposition made AI research experimentally manageable.
- Success on separate components did not guarantee successful integration into general intelligence.
8. Q: What major capabilities did golden-age researchers identify as components of intelligent behaviour?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies perception, learning, problem solving, planning, reasoning, and natural-language understanding as important capabilities. Perception turns sensory input into an interpretation of the environment. Learning uses data or experience to improve predictions or performance. Problem solving and planning seek action sequences that achieve goals. Reasoning derives new conclusions from known facts, while natural-language understanding interprets flexible human communication. These capabilities overlap in practical systems, but separating them helped researchers design focused experiments.
Study instruction:
- Create a capability map showing the input, purpose, and output of each component discussed in the chapter.
Key point:
- AI was decomposed into capabilities that could be studied separately.
- The components are interdependent when a system must act in a complex environment.
9. Q: Why did perception prove more difficult than simply installing sensors?
A: Show answer
Sensors can collect measurements, but measurements are not the same as meaningful understanding. A camera, for example, converts an image into numerical values representing colour and brightness. An intelligent system must interpret those values as objects, relationships, obstacles, or relevant events. The chapter emphasizes that building a sensor is only part of perception; extracting a reliable account of what is happening in the environment is a much harder problem. Better hardware does not automatically solve interpretation.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between acquiring raw sensory data and interpreting that data as meaningful information.
Key point:
- Perception requires interpretation, not merely sensing.
- A high-resolution sensor can still provide data that a system does not understand.
10. Q: How does the chapter distinguish machine learning from human learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter cautions that the term machine learning can create an exaggerated impression that a machine autonomously develops intelligence from nothing. In the examples discussed, machine learning involves using data to learn patterns and make predictions. A face-recognition system, for instance, may be trained with many images labelled with the identities of the people shown. Its later task is to predict an identity from an image without the label. This is a specific data-driven capability, not necessarily the broad, flexible learning associated with human development.
Study instruction:
- Use the face-recognition example to explain the role of labelled training examples and prediction on new inputs.
Key point:
- Machine learning in the chapter is data-based pattern learning and prediction.
- A learned predictive capability should not automatically be equated with human-like understanding.
11. Q: How are problem solving and planning related in the chapter's account of AI?
A: Show answer
Both involve reaching a goal through a sequence of available actions. In a board game, the goal may be to win and the actions are legal moves; the system must identify a sequence that leads toward the goal. Planning similarly requires choosing and ordering actions to transform an initial situation into a desired one. The difficulty is not merely describing a goal but finding a workable sequence among many possible alternatives. This becomes computationally challenging as the number of available actions grows.
Study instruction:
- Describe a practical task as an initial state, a goal state, and a set of actions that can transform one into the other.
Key point:
- Problem solving and planning are goal-directed action-selection tasks.
- The central challenge is choosing a feasible sequence from many alternatives.
12. Q: What does automated reasoning mean in the context of early AI?
A: Show answer
Automated reasoning is the attempt to give computers the ability to derive new knowledge from existing facts through logical rules. The chapter illustrates this with the inference that if all men are mortal and Michael is a man, then Michael is mortal. A system can use the newly derived fact as a premise for further conclusions. Reasoning is therefore more than retrieving stored information: it involves applying rules to produce conclusions that were not explicitly listed as initial facts.
Study instruction:
- Work through a simple syllogism and identify which conclusion is derived rather than directly supplied.
Key point:
- Automated reasoning derives conclusions from existing facts and rules.
- The ability to make valid inferences was treated as a central component of intelligence.
13. Q: Why did rule-based approaches to natural-language understanding encounter difficulties?
A: Show answer
Natural languages are flexible, context-dependent, ambiguous, and fluid. Early approaches tried to describe them through precise rules in the way programming languages are defined. The chapter explains that this approach could not adequately capture everyday language use. A natural-language system must interpret meaning in context rather than rely only on a fixed set of unambiguous grammatical rules. This helps explain why a seemingly simple capability, such as understanding an ordinary instruction, proved much harder than expected.
Study instruction:
- Compare the formal precision of a programming language with the flexibility and ambiguity of everyday language.
Key point:
- Natural language cannot be fully captured by a small, rigid rule set.
- Meaning depends on context and use as well as formal structure.
14. Q: What was SHRDLU designed to demonstrate?
A: Show answer
Terry Winograd developed SHRDLU in 1971 to demonstrate problem solving and natural-language understanding. The system accepted instructions in ordinary-looking English and manipulated objects in a simulated Blocks World. It could answer questions about the configuration of objects and resolve some references in a conversation. Its performance was impressive for its time because it connected language interaction with action planning inside a defined environment. The chapter also stresses that its apparent fluency depended on a tightly constrained world.
Study instruction:
- Identify the two principal capabilities SHRDLU was intended to demonstrate and explain how the Blocks World supported them.
Key point:
- SHRDLU combined language interaction with symbolic problem solving.
- Its success depended on a narrow, carefully controlled domain.
15. Q: What is the Blocks World and why was it useful in early AI research?
A: Show answer
The Blocks World is a simulated environment containing objects such as blocks, boxes, and pyramids that can be rearranged to reach a goal configuration. It reduces a complex physical task to a manageable set of objects, states, and permitted actions. Researchers could study planning and language interaction without first solving the engineering problems of a real robot. Its simplicity made it useful as an experimental test bed, but also meant that success in the simulation did not establish that a robot could manipulate objects in the physical world.
Study instruction:
- Draw an initial and goal configuration and specify the legal actions needed to transform one into the other.
Key point:
- The Blocks World isolates planning and action sequencing in a simple model.
- A controlled simulation is useful for research but may omit decisive real-world difficulties.
16. Q: What preconditions govern the basic Blocks World actions?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a small action repertoire: pick up an object from the table, place a carried object on the table, pick up an object from another object, and place an object on top of another object. Each action has preconditions. For example, picking up an object from the table requires that the object is on the table and the robot arm is empty. Picking an object from another object also requires that the target object is on top and unobstructed. Making these conditions explicit allows a planner to reject impossible action sequences.
Study instruction:
- For each Blocks World action, write down the conditions that must be true before it can occur and the state change it produces.
Key point:
- Planning requires explicit action preconditions and effects.
- A valid plan must respect the physical and logical constraints encoded in the model.
17. Q: What does the closed-world assumption mean in SHRDLU's Blocks World?
A: Show answer
The closed-world assumption means that changes in the simulated environment occur only when SHRDLU performs an action. If the system places one block on another, it can assume that the arrangement remains unchanged until it acts again. The chapter compares this with living alone: no other person is present to move an object unexpectedly. Real environments are open to independent actors and unforeseen events, so an AI system that assumes it is the only source of change may rely on an outdated model.
Study instruction:
- Contrast a closed simulated environment with a shared physical workspace in which other actors can change the state.
Key point:
- The closed-world assumption simplifies state tracking.
- Real systems must account for changes they did not cause.
18. Q: Why was SHRDLU's simulated manipulation different from real robotic manipulation?
A: Show answer
SHRDLU modelled the effects of actions rather than physically carrying them out. A command such as picking up an object could be treated as one indivisible operation. A real robot must locate the objects, perceive their positions, move an arm through space, approach the correct point, grasp the object, and confirm that the grasp succeeded. Each stage introduces uncertainty and engineering difficulty. The simulation therefore removed much of the complexity that makes physical manipulation challenging.
Study instruction:
- Break a simulated pick-up action into the perception, movement, grasping, and verification steps required by a physical robot.
Key point:
- A symbolic action can conceal substantial physical complexity.
- Successful planning in a simulation does not prove successful execution in the real world.
19. Q: Why did SHRDLU's language abilities fail to generalize broadly?
A: Show answer
SHRDLU's dialogue appeared sophisticated because users could issue instructions, ask questions, and refer to objects in ordinary-looking language. However, the dialogue concerned a limited set of objects and relationships in the Blocks World. The system's language competence was therefore supported by a constrained domain with a manageable vocabulary and a structured model. The chapter notes that the hoped-for route from this demonstration to general natural-language understanding did not materialize.
Study instruction:
- Identify the domain restrictions that make a language demonstration easier, then ask what changes when the subject matter is unrestricted.
Key point:
- SHRDLU demonstrated meaningful but domain-limited language interaction.
- Fluent performance in a narrow setting is not proof of general language understanding.
20. Q: What general lesson does SHRDLU offer about evaluating AI demonstrations?
A: Show answer
A demonstration should be evaluated against the range of conditions it actually handles, not the impression created by a polished example. SHRDLU's dialogue was impressive, but its apparent generality depended on a small simulated world. A sound evaluation asks which objects, actions, assumptions, and linguistic forms are supported, and what happens outside them. This lesson applies to modern demonstrations as well: a system may perform convincingly within a prepared setting while remaining fragile when the environment changes.
Study instruction:
- When reviewing an AI demonstration, list its supported inputs, assumptions, operating domain, and failure conditions.
Key point:
- A successful demonstration may prove a narrow capability rather than a general one.
- Always test transfer beyond the environment used to showcase a system.
21. Q: Why were physical robots a relatively small part of golden-age AI research?
A: Show answer
Building a robot was expensive, time-consuming, and technically demanding. A serious project required a research laboratory, engineering support, workshop facilities, sensors, and computers capable of running the software. Computers of the period were large and often unsuitable for installation on a mobile platform. It was therefore cheaper and more practical for many researchers to build programs that simulated interaction with the world than to construct machines that had to operate in its full complexity.
Study instruction:
- Compare the resource requirements of a simulated planning program with those of a mobile robot operating in a real environment.
Key point:
- Physical robotics required substantial hardware and engineering resources.
- Simulation lowered cost but also removed important sources of difficulty.
22. Q: What made the SHAKEY project historically significant?
A: Show answer
SHAKEY, developed at the Stanford Research Institute between 1966 and 1972, was a serious attempt to build a mobile robot that could receive tasks and determine how to perform them in a real environment. Unlike SHRDLU, it manipulated physical objects rather than only updating a simulated model. It needed to perceive surroundings, navigate, plan, execute actions, and monitor whether the task was proceeding as intended. The project was significant because it attempted to integrate several AI capabilities in one autonomous physical system.
Study instruction:
- Explain why SHAKEY represented a different level of challenge from a system that only manipulates a simulated world.
Key point:
- SHAKEY attempted autonomous task execution in a physical environment.
- It exposed the difficulty of integrating perception, planning, navigation, and action.
23. Q: Which sensors and engineering features did SHAKEY use?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes SHAKEY as using a television camera and laser range finders to obtain information about its surroundings, together with bump detectors known as cats' whiskers to detect obstacles. The robot also needed a mobile platform and mechanisms for navigating and moving objects. These components show that physical AI requires a coordinated sensing and engineering system, not merely a reasoning program. The sensors themselves were constrained by the technology and operating conditions of the period.
Study instruction:
- Match each sensor to the kind of environmental information it supplied and explain why several sensing mechanisms were needed.
Key point:
- SHAKEY combined camera, distance-sensing, and contact-detection equipment.
- A physical robot needs reliable environmental information to act safely.
24. Q: What limitations prevented SHAKEY from becoming a broadly useful robot?
A: Show answer
SHAKEY's perception was limited and depended on a specially prepared, carefully lit environment. Its camera was power-hungry and took time to become usable. Planning could take up to fifteen minutes, during which the robot remained inactive. The computers needed to run the software were too large and heavy to carry, so processing was performed remotely through a radio link. These constraints made the robot an important research achievement but unsuitable for substantial practical tasks.
Study instruction:
- Explain how sensing conditions, processing speed, computing hardware, and communication affected SHAKEY's autonomy.
Key point:
- SHAKEY demonstrated physical autonomy under heavily simplified conditions.
- Its limitations showed the gap between a research prototype and a practical robot.
25. Q: Why is integrating AI components harder than developing each component separately?
A: Show answer
A system may have workable perception, navigation, planning, and action modules individually, yet still fail when they must operate together. Each component depends on information from others, and delays or errors can propagate through the system. A robot must keep its internal model aligned with the changing environment while selecting and executing actions. The chapter describes this coordination problem as substantially harder than getting any one capability to work in isolation.
Study instruction:
- Trace a hypothetical robot task from sensing through planning to action, and identify where an error in one module could affect the next.
Key point:
- Component-level success does not guarantee system-level reliability.
- Integration requires coordination, timing, and consistent information across modules.
26. Q: What does the term search mean in artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
Search in AI is a systematic problem-solving technique that considers possible courses of action to find a route from an initial state to a goal state. It is not the same as searching the internet. A search algorithm represents possible configurations, applies available actions to generate new configurations, and explores the resulting alternatives. The chapter presents search as a cornerstone technique because many tasks, including games and navigation, require choosing among sequences of possible actions.
Study instruction:
- Explain AI search using an initial state, a goal state, possible actions, and the states those actions generate.
Key point:
- AI search explores action sequences through a problem's state space.
- The purpose is to find a path to a goal, not to retrieve web pages.
27. Q: What are a state, an action, and a goal state in a search problem?
A: Show answer
A state is a particular configuration of the problem at a moment in time. An action is an available operation that transforms one state into another. A goal state is a configuration that satisfies the objective. In the Towers of Hanoi, a state records which rings are on each column, an action moves one legal ring, and the goal state has all rings on the destination column. These definitions allow an informal puzzle to be represented as a formal search problem.
Study instruction:
- Represent a familiar task as a state description, a set of legal actions, and a precise goal condition.
Key point:
- Search depends on a clear representation of states and transitions.
- A vague goal or incomplete action model makes systematic search unreliable.
28. Q: How does the Towers of Hanoi illustrate a search problem?
A: Show answer
The puzzle begins with rings of different sizes on one column and requires moving them to another column. Only one ring may be moved at a time, and a larger ring may never rest on a smaller one. Each legal move creates a new state. A solver must find a sequence that reaches the goal without violating the constraints. The puzzle is useful because its rules are simple, yet the number of moves and possible configurations grows rapidly as the number of rings increases.
Study instruction:
- Solve the three-ring version and write each move as a transition between valid states.
Key point:
- The puzzle is a state-space problem governed by explicit action constraints.
- A simple rule set can still produce a large search problem.
29. Q: How does level-by-level exhaustive search explore a problem?
A: Show answer
The procedure begins at the initial state and applies every available action to generate successor states. It checks whether any successor is a goal; if not, it repeats the process for the newly generated states. Exploring level by level creates a search tree whose depth represents the number of actions taken. If a solution exists and the state space is finite, systematic exhaustive exploration can eventually find it. When all actions have equal cost, examining shallower levels first also ensures that the first solution found uses the fewest actions.
Study instruction:
- Draw the first few levels of a small search tree and label each node with the state reached.
Key point:
- Level-by-level search systematically explores paths in increasing depth.
- With equal action costs, the first goal found is a shortest-action solution.
30. Q: Why can exhaustive search find an optimal Towers of Hanoi solution?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's level-by-level method, all states reachable in one move are considered before states reachable in two moves, and so on. The first goal reached is therefore at the smallest depth in the search tree. For the three-ring Towers of Hanoi puzzle, the minimum is seven moves; a solution using more moves is possible but not optimal. This guarantee depends on the orderly exploration of levels and the assumption that each move has the same cost.
Study instruction:
- Verify the seven-move minimum for three rings and explain why exploring shallower levels first matters.
Key point:
- Exhaustive level-by-level search can guarantee a minimum-move solution.
- Optimality depends on the search order and the cost model.
31. Q: What is the main inefficiency of naive exhaustive search?
A: Show answer
Naive exhaustive search may revisit configurations that have already been explored. In the Towers of Hanoi example, a sequence of moves can return the puzzle to its initial state, wasting effort without making progress. A human solver may quickly recognize such a cycle, but a simple program will repeat it unless the algorithm records visited states or applies rules to avoid revisiting them. The inefficiency grows as the search tree expands.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a search tree for repeated states and mark where a visited-state check would prevent redundant exploration.
Key point:
- Repeated states create unnecessary work in exhaustive search.
- Cycle detection and state tracking can reduce wasted exploration.
32. Q: What is the branching factor of a search problem?
A: Show answer
The branching factor is the average number of successor choices available from a state. It measures how widely the search tree expands at each step. The chapter uses a branching factor of about three for the Towers of Hanoi example and about 250 for Go. Even a modest increase in branching factor can cause enormous growth as search depth increases, because each level multiplies the number of possible paths.
Study instruction:
- Estimate the number of nodes at successive depths for a problem with a fixed branching factor.
Key point:
- The branching factor measures the average number of choices at each state.
- Large branching factors make deep search rapidly expensive.
33. Q: What is combinatorial explosion and why is it important in AI?
A: Show answer
Combinatorial explosion occurs when successive choices multiply the number of possible outcomes so rapidly that exhaustive exploration becomes impractical. If each state offers many actions, the search tree grows exponentially with depth. The chapter identifies this as one of the central practical problems in AI because search appears in many tasks, from games to planning. A method may be correct in principle yet unusable because the number of alternatives exceeds available time or memory.
Study instruction:
- Use a branching-factor example to calculate how quickly a search tree expands and identify the depth at which resources become limiting.
Key point:
- Combinatorial explosion makes brute-force search impractical.
- The challenge is not always whether a solution exists, but whether it can be found with available resources.
34. Q: How does the Go example demonstrate the scale of search-tree growth?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses an average branching factor of approximately 250 for Go. At depth one there are about 250 states; at depth two there are 62,500; at depth three there are roughly 15.6 million; and at depth four there are about 3.9 billion. The example illustrates how quickly a tree becomes too large to store or explore. A typical game lasts far longer than four moves, so straightforward exhaustive search cannot handle the full problem.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate the first four levels using a branching factor of 250 and explain the practical memory implication.
Key point:
- A high branching factor produces explosive growth after only a few levels.
- A correct exhaustive method may exceed practical computing resources almost immediately.
35. Q: What is depth-first search and how does it differ from level-by-level search?
A: Show answer
Depth-first search follows one branch as far as possible before returning to explore another branch. If it reaches a dead end or detects a repeated state, it backtracks and selects a different branch. Unlike level-by-level exploration, it does not need to store the entire frontier of the search tree; it mainly stores the current path and the information needed to backtrack. Its efficiency in memory comes with a risk that the chosen branch may be unproductive or extremely deep.
Study instruction:
- Trace a small tree using depth-first order and compare the number of stored nodes with level-by-level exploration.
Key point:
- Depth-first search explores one path deeply before backtracking.
- It can reduce memory use but may spend a long time on a poor branch.
36. Q: What are the strengths and weaknesses of depth-first search?
A: Show answer
Its principal strength is memory efficiency because it stores the branch being explored rather than the whole search tree. Its weakness is that a poor branch can consume substantial effort without finding a solution. Depending on the problem, depth-first search may also fail to find the shallowest or least-cost solution first. It is therefore not automatically superior to level-by-level search; its suitability depends on the structure of the state space, the availability of cycle detection, and whether a useful depth bound exists.
Study instruction:
- Choose a search strategy for a small problem and justify the choice in terms of memory, solution depth, and risk of unproductive branches.
Key point:
- Depth-first search trades lower memory requirements for less predictable exploration.
- Search strategy must be selected according to the problem's structure and objective.
37. Q: What is heuristic search?
A: Show answer
Heuristic search uses rules of thumb to guide exploration toward states that appear promising. Rather than treating every branch as equally attractive, the system estimates which direction is more likely to lead to a useful result. A heuristic can make search much more efficient in a practical setting, but it is not generally guaranteed to identify the best path. The chapter presents heuristic search as a response to the limits of exhaustive exploration, not as a universal solution to computational complexity.
Study instruction:
- Identify a measurable feature that could help prioritize promising states in a search problem.
Key point:
- A heuristic guides search using an informed estimate.
- Heuristics can improve practical efficiency without guaranteeing an optimal result.
38. Q: Why can a heuristic be useful even when it is not guaranteed to be correct?
A: Show answer
A heuristic can reduce the number of alternatives explored by prioritizing states that appear promising. In many practical problems, a good approximation is more useful than an exhaustive search that cannot finish within available resources. However, a heuristic may misjudge a state, overlook a better branch, or become unreliable when the problem changes. The correct question is therefore not whether a heuristic is infallible, but whether its performance is useful for the task and whether its limitations are understood.
Study instruction:
- For a planning task, propose a heuristic and identify at least one situation in which it could mislead the search.
Key point:
- Heuristics trade certainty for focused exploration.
- Their quality depends on how well the estimate matches the actual problem.
39. Q: How did Arthur Samuel apply heuristic evaluation to checkers?
A: Show answer
Arthur Samuel's checkers program, developed at IBM in the mid-1950s, evaluated board positions using features that indicated how favourable a position might be. One example was the number of pieces a player retained. The program combined features into an overall assessment and used that assessment to choose promising moves. This allowed the program to play credibly despite severe limitations in the computer available at the time. The chapter presents the work as an early substantial application of heuristic search.
Study instruction:
- Identify the board features that might contribute to a position evaluation and explain how they could influence move selection.
Key point:
- Samuel used an evaluation function to estimate the quality of a board position.
- A heuristic can guide decisions without exploring every possible continuation.
40. Q: What is the difference between a heuristic evaluation and a guaranteed proof of a solution?
A: Show answer
A heuristic evaluation estimates how promising a state appears; it does not by itself prove that the state leads to a solution or that the selected path is optimal. A proof or guarantee requires reasoning that covers the relevant possibilities under the algorithm's assumptions. Samuel's checkers program could make credible choices using position features, but the chapter does not present those estimates as a universal guarantee. The distinction matters whenever an AI system ranks alternatives using approximate scores.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed heuristic score, separate what the score predicts from what can be logically guaranteed.
Key point:
- A heuristic is an estimate, not a proof.
- A strong practical result should not be confused with a formal optimality guarantee.
41. Q: What distinction does the chapter make between decidable and practically solvable problems?
A: Show answer
A problem is decidable if there is a computational procedure that can determine an answer in principle. That does not mean the procedure can finish within practical limits. Some decidable problems require enormous time or memory as their size increases. The chapter explains that AI researchers increasingly encountered problems that were theoretically solvable but computationally intractable for realistic instances. This distinction helped explain why early demonstrations did not scale into general solutions.
Study instruction:
- Classify a problem separately by whether an algorithm exists and whether it can run within realistic resource limits.
Key point:
- Decidability concerns whether a problem can be solved in principle.
- Practical solvability also depends on time, memory, and input size.
42. Q: What is the traveling salesman problem as presented in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a salesman who must visit a collection of cities and return to the starting point. The road network may not connect every pair of cities, and the salesman has a fixed fuel range. The decision question is whether a tour exists that visits all cities and can be completed on one tank of fuel. A brute-force approach lists possible tours and checks whether each satisfies the constraint. The problem illustrates how a simple statement can conceal a rapidly growing number of combinations.
Study instruction:
- Restate the problem as a decision question and identify the route constraints that must be checked.
Key point:
- The traveling salesman example asks whether a feasible complete tour exists.
- The number of candidate tours grows rapidly with the number of cities.
43. Q: Why does brute-force enumeration become impractical for the traveling salesman problem?
A: Show answer
A brute-force method must examine many possible orders in which cities can be visited. As the number of cities increases, the number of candidate tours grows extremely quickly. The chapter gives illustrative counts of up to 3.6 million tours for ten cities and about 40 million for eleven. Even though each candidate can be checked, generating and evaluating all candidates soon becomes too expensive. The difficulty is the growth in combinations, not an inability to describe the task.
Study instruction:
- Compare the candidate-tour counts for ten and eleven cities and explain why a small increase in input size matters.
Key point:
- Brute force scales poorly because the number of candidate tours grows rapidly.
- A one-city increase can multiply the computational burden.
44. Q: What does NP-complete mean in the chapter's intuitive explanation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes NP-complete problems as combinatorial problems for which finding a solution is hard because there are too many possibilities to examine, while checking a proposed solution is comparatively easy. In the traveling salesman example, verifying a candidate tour involves checking whether it visits the required cities and meets the fuel constraint. The chapter presents this as an intuitive explanation rather than a complete formal definition of complexity theory. The key distinction is between discovering a solution and verifying one already supplied.
Study instruction:
- Use a candidate tour to distinguish the work involved in finding a solution from the work involved in checking it.
Key point:
- NP-complete problems are presented as hard to solve but easier to verify.
- Verification efficiency does not imply that discovery is efficient.
45. Q: Why is the P-versus-NP question important?
A: Show answer
The P-versus-NP problem asks, in broad terms, whether problems whose proposed solutions can be checked efficiently can also be solved efficiently. The chapter identifies it as a major open problem and notes the Clay Mathematics Institute's recognition of it as a millennium problem with a substantial prize. The practical significance is enormous: if efficient methods existed for NP-complete problems, many tasks currently regarded as computationally hard could become tractable. The chapter cautions that the prevailing expectation is not a proof; the question remains unresolved.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between efficiently finding a solution and efficiently verifying one, and why proving a relationship between them matters.
Key point:
- P versus NP concerns the relationship between efficient verification and efficient solution.
- The chapter treats the question as unresolved, not as a settled theorem.
46. Q: What does it mean to say that a problem is tractable?
A: Show answer
A problem is tractable when it can be solved using resources that remain practical as the input grows. The chapter contrasts tractable problems with those that are decidable but require impractical amounts of time or memory. The term is important because theoretical solvability alone is insufficient for an AI system that must respond within operational limits. A method that eventually returns the correct answer after an unrealistic delay is not useful for many real tasks.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an algorithm using input size, runtime growth, memory demand, and the time available for the task.
Key point:
- Tractability is about practical resource requirements as a problem scales.
- An algorithm can be correct and still be unusable.
47. Q: What is combinatorial explosion in a sequence of choices beyond search trees?
A: Show answer
Combinatorial explosion appears whenever a problem involves successive choices whose combinations multiply. The chapter uses possible tours in the traveling salesman problem as an example: each additional city creates many new possible visit orders. The same principle applies to planning and other AI tasks where a system must choose among many combinations. The important diagnostic is whether the number of alternatives grows multiplicatively rather than linearly with the size of the task.
Study instruction:
- Identify the independent choices in a problem and estimate how the number of combined outcomes grows as choices are added.
Key point:
- Combinatorial explosion is a general property of multiplying choice sequences.
- It affects many AI problems, not only game-tree search.
48. Q: Why does the chapter say that better heuristics cannot remove every complexity barrier?
A: Show answer
Heuristics can direct effort toward promising alternatives and may make particular instances manageable. However, a heuristic is not guaranteed to find a valid or optimal solution in every case. The chapter argues that no simple search recipe can be assumed to eliminate the underlying combinatorial growth of NP-complete problems. A heuristic may improve practical performance, but it does not establish that the worst-case complexity has disappeared. This distinction prevents a useful engineering technique from being mistaken for a mathematical resolution.
Study instruction:
- For an optimization method, state what it improves and what it does not guarantee about worst-case performance.
