Table 5
Robots, Agents, and Rational Decision-Making in Artificial Intelligence
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 4, “Robots and Rationality”?
A: Show answer
The chapter traces a shift in artificial intelligence from systems centered on explicit symbolic knowledge and reasoning toward systems that interact directly with their environments, then toward integrated agents that combine perception, action, planning, cooperation, rational choice, and probabilistic belief revision. Rodney Brooks’s behavioral approach challenged the assumption that intelligence must begin with a detailed internal model of the world. Agent-based AI retained the importance of situated behavior while restoring reasoning where it is useful. The chapter then explains how agents can represent preferences with utilities, choose under uncertainty through expected utility, and update beliefs using Bayesian inference. Its historical lesson is that AI advances through changing paradigms rather than one universally sufficient technique.
Study instruction:
- Map the chapter’s progression from symbolic AI to behavioral AI, agent-based AI, rational choice, and Bayesian reasoning.
Key point:
- Intelligent systems must connect perception, action, reasoning, and uncertainty management.
- No single paradigm solves every AI problem.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
2. Q: Why did another crisis in artificial intelligence emerge by the late 1980s?
A: Show answer
The expert-systems boom had faded after the field had been criticized for exaggerated promises and results that did not match expectations. The crisis questioned more than the commercial enthusiasm surrounding expert systems: researchers began challenging the assumptions behind symbolic AI itself, especially the idea that intelligent behavior should be built around explicit knowledge representation and reasoning. Importantly, some of the strongest criticism came from AI researchers rather than external opponents. The chapter presents this as a crisis of scientific foundations: methods that worked in restricted settings did not adequately explain or reproduce intelligent behavior in the physical world. The resulting debate opened space for alternative approaches to robotics and situated intelligence.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the commercial disappointment of expert systems from the deeper challenge to symbolic AI assumptions.
Key point:
- Overpromising and limited real-world performance weakened confidence in the prevailing paradigm.
- Internal criticism helped drive a new research direction.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
3. Q: How does Thomas Kuhn’s idea of a scientific paradigm shift help explain the chapter’s account of AI history?
A: Show answer
Kuhn argued that established scientific frameworks can enter a crisis when repeated failures undermine their ability to explain important observations. A new framework may then replace the old one and change the questions researchers ask and the methods they consider legitimate. Wooldridge uses this idea to frame the late-1980s challenge to symbolic AI: the field began questioning not only particular systems but also the assumptions that had guided research. The analogy is explanatory rather than a claim that every AI transition follows a fixed Kuhnian sequence. It helps readers understand why a research community may need to reconsider its foundations when established techniques repeatedly fail outside controlled settings.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between improving an existing technique and replacing the assumptions that define a research paradigm.
Key point:
- A paradigm crisis concerns foundational assumptions, not merely an unsuccessful product.
- AI history includes repeated changes in what researchers regard as the right starting point.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
4. Q: What was Rodney Brooks’s principal criticism of knowledge-based AI?
A: Show answer
Brooks questioned the assumption that a robot must first encode extensive knowledge about the world and then use that representation as the basis for reasoning and decision-making. He argued that a useful robot must operate in the real world, where it continuously encounters changing conditions and must perceive and act. A central symbolic model can become an obstacle if the system reasons from information that is already out of date. Brooks therefore shifted attention toward direct interaction between an embodied system and its environment. His criticism did not establish that reasoning is always useless; it challenged the idea that explicit representation and logical reasoning should be the universal foundation of intelligent behavior.
Study instruction:
- Compare a robot that relies on a stored world model with one that responds directly to current sensor information.
Key point:
- Real-world intelligence requires timely perception and action, not only stored knowledge.
- Brooks challenged the priority given to symbolic reasoning.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
5. Q: Why did Brooks regard the Blocks World as an inadequate test of real-world intelligence?
A: Show answer
The Blocks World is a simulated tabletop environment in which a system rearranges objects according to specified goals. Its apparent similarity to a warehouse can be misleading because the simulation removes many of the difficult physical conditions that a real robot must handle. In an actual warehouse, objects may be positioned unpredictably, sensors may be imperfect, obstacles may move, and the robot must coordinate perception with physical movement. A program that solves a simplified symbolic puzzle therefore does not automatically demonstrate that it can manipulate objects reliably in a real environment. Brooks used the example to expose the gap between success in a carefully controlled model and competence in the world the model is supposed to represent.
Study instruction:
- List the real-world variables omitted by a simplified simulation and explain how each could affect a robot’s performance.
Key point:
- A simulated task may hide the very difficulties that determine real-world success.
- Evaluation should test the capabilities required in the intended environment.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
6. Q: What does it mean for an intelligent system to be situated in the real world?
A: Show answer
A situated system is directly embedded in an environment: it receives information from that environment and acts within it rather than operating only on an abstract description of the world. Its behavior is shaped by the conditions it currently encounters. A mobile robot, for example, must detect obstacles, respond to changes in its surroundings, and adjust its movement while carrying out a task. The environment is not merely an input supplied before reasoning begins; it is a continuing part of the system’s operation. This approach emphasizes the relationship between sensing, action, and changing circumstances as a foundation for practical intelligent behavior.
Study instruction:
- Describe a real task and identify the sensors, environmental changes, and actions that make the system situated.
Key point:
- Situated intelligence depends on continuous interaction with an environment.
- The system’s behavior must remain responsive to current conditions.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
7. Q: What does Brooks mean when he suggests that intelligence can emerge from interaction with the environment?
A: Show answer
The claim is that useful intelligent behavior need not always be produced by a central store of explicit facts followed by formal reasoning. It can arise from the coordinated operation of simpler behaviors that respond to conditions in the environment. A robot that avoids obstacles, follows a direction, and reacts to nearby objects may display coherent task-oriented behavior even when no single component contains a complete symbolic description of the world. The intelligence is found in the organized interaction among the robot, its behaviors, and its surroundings. This does not mean that every complex cognitive task can be solved without representation or reasoning; it identifies a different route to practical competence for suitable tasks.
Study instruction:
- Explain how several simple sensor-driven behaviors could combine to produce a coherent activity without a central world model.
Key point:
- Complex behavior can emerge from coordinated simpler interactions.
- The usefulness of this approach depends on the task and environment.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
8. Q: What was Brooks’s objection to the divide-and-conquer strategy in AI research?
A: Show answer
The divide-and-conquer strategy attempts to understand intelligence by separating it into components such as reasoning, learning, and perception, then developing each component independently. Brooks argued that this can miss the way these capabilities interact in an operating system. Perception affects which actions are possible; action changes what the system can perceive; learning may alter later responses; and reasoning may depend on the current physical situation. A collection of individually successful components may therefore fail when integrated if their interactions have not been considered. The chapter’s point is not that decomposition is always wrong, but that component-level progress cannot substitute for testing the complete system in the environment where it must work.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simple interaction diagram showing how perception, learning, reasoning, and action influence one another in a robot.
Key point:
- Intelligence cannot always be understood by studying isolated capabilities alone.
- Integration and interaction must be evaluated explicitly.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
9. Q: Why did Brooks criticize AI approaches that ignored computational effort?
A: Show answer
An intelligent system must produce behavior within the time and resource limits of its environment. If every activity is translated into elaborate logical reasoning, the computational cost may make the system too slow to respond to events that require immediate action. A mobile robot cannot safely postpone obstacle avoidance while it performs an expensive deduction. Brooks therefore challenged the assumption that intelligence should always be reduced to computationally demanding reasoning. Simple behavior mechanisms can sometimes deliver faster and more reliable responses. The appropriate design depends on the task: rapid reactive control may be essential for immediate movement, while more expensive reasoning may be valuable for planning or complex decisions.
Study instruction:
- Identify one task requiring an immediate response and one task where slower deliberate reasoning is acceptable.
Key point:
- Computational feasibility is part of intelligent system design.
- A theoretically sophisticated method may be unsuitable if it cannot respond in time.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
10. Q: What is the difference between a decoupled perceive-reason-act loop and close-coupled behavior?
A: Show answer
In a traditional knowledge-based loop, a system senses the environment, updates an internal description, reasons about what to do, performs an action, and then repeats the cycle. The concern is that the environment may change after sensing but before the selected action is completed. If the system continues to rely on its earlier description, it may act on stale information. Close-coupled behavior maintains a tighter relationship between current conditions and the response, allowing the system to react as the environment changes. The distinction is not simply whether a system has a loop; it concerns how directly and quickly its behavior remains connected to what is happening around it.
Study instruction:
- Create a timeline in which an obstacle moves after sensing and compare the responses of a delayed planner and a closely coupled controller.
Key point:
- Stale internal information can produce inappropriate actions.
- Close coupling improves responsiveness to environmental change.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
11. Q: Why is a close relationship between a robot’s situation and its behavior important?
A: Show answer
A robot’s surroundings can change continuously. A person may move into its path, an object may be displaced, or a sensor may detect a new condition while the robot is carrying out a task. If the robot’s behavior is tightly connected to current sensory information, it can adapt rather than blindly completing an action chosen from an outdated picture. This is especially important for navigation and physical interaction, where delayed responses can create collisions or task failures. Close coupling does not eliminate the need for planning, but it helps ensure that plans and actions remain grounded in the environment in which they are executed.
Study instruction:
- Identify three environmental changes that should trigger an immediate change in a mobile robot’s behavior.
Key point:
- Real-time responsiveness is central to reliable physical interaction.
- Plans must remain sensitive to new information.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
12. Q: What is behavioral AI?
A: Show answer
Behavioral AI is an approach that emphasizes the individual behaviors contributing to an intelligent system’s overall operation. Rather than requiring a comprehensive symbolic model and extensive reasoning before action, the designer identifies useful behaviors and organizes how they respond to environmental conditions. In Brooks’s work, the approach was embodied in the subsumption architecture, which prioritized certain behaviors over others. This made it possible to build robots that responded quickly to sensory input and carried out practical tasks. Behavioral AI was influential because it offered a working alternative to criticism of symbolic AI, not merely a theoretical objection. Its later limitations, particularly difficulty scaling to many interacting behaviors, led researchers to seek integrated approaches.
Study instruction:
- Define behavioral AI and contrast its starting point with a knowledge-centered symbolic system.
Key point:
- Behavioral AI builds competence from organized, environment-responsive behaviors.
- Its strengths are most visible in tasks suited to direct reaction.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
13. Q: How does the subsumption architecture develop an intelligent robot?
A: Show answer
The method begins by identifying the separate behaviors needed for the task. The designer first implements one behavior, then adds further behaviors and organizes their relationships so that the right response takes precedence in the right circumstances. The process is incremental, but the key design problem is not simply adding more rules: it is deciding how the behaviors interact and which should override others when conditions conflict. Extensive experimentation is needed to check whether the resulting hierarchy works in practice. In the chapter’s example, obstacle avoidance, docking, emptying the dirt container, vacuuming, and random movement are organized into a priority structure for a cleaning robot.
Study instruction:
- Design a small behavior list for a mobile robot and specify the conditions and priority of each behavior.
Key point:
- Subsumption develops a system by adding and organizing component behaviors.
- Priority relationships determine which behavior controls the robot at a given moment.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
14. Q: Which six behaviors does the chapter use to explain a subsumption-based vacuum-cleaning robot?
A: Show answer
The example uses six behaviors: avoid obstacles by changing direction when an obstacle is detected; shut down when the robot is at its dock and has a low battery; empty the dirt container when docked and carrying dirt; head to the dock when the battery is low or the container is full; vacuum when dirt is detected at the current location; and walk randomly by choosing a direction and moving. Each behavior has a relatively simple condition and response. Their combined operation allows the robot to search for dirt, clean, avoid obstacles, and return for servicing without relying on a single elaborate logical plan for every movement.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the six behaviors and write the condition that activates each one.
Key point:
- A practical robot can be built from a set of specific, task-related behaviors.
- The behaviors must be coordinated rather than treated as an unordered list.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
15. Q: What is a subsumption hierarchy and how does it control competing robot behaviors?
A: Show answer
A subsumption hierarchy establishes precedence among behaviors. In the chapter’s description, behaviors lower in the hierarchy take precedence over those higher in it. This means that an urgent behavior can override a routine behavior. Obstacle avoidance receives the highest priority in the vacuum example: even if the robot is moving toward dirt or following its usual movement pattern, it must change direction when it detects an obstacle. The hierarchy therefore resolves conflicts by specifying which response should dominate under particular conditions. It is a practical control arrangement, not merely a list of independent functions.
Study instruction:
- Draw the vacuum robot’s behavior hierarchy and explain what happens when obstacle detection conflicts with routine movement.
Key point:
- Priority rules determine which behavior takes control when conditions overlap.
- Safety-related responses can override routine task behavior.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
16. Q: Why must the interactions among subsumption behaviors be tested extensively?
A: Show answer
A behavior that works in isolation may produce unexpected results when another behavior is active at the same time. For example, random movement may conflict with a command to return to the docking station, while obstacle avoidance may interrupt either movement pattern. A full system must therefore be tested across combinations of conditions, not merely by confirming that each behavior works independently. Testing should examine priority, repeated switching, dead ends, incomplete task sequences, and responses to changing sensor data. The chapter emphasizes experimentation because the behavior system’s success depends on the way its components are organized and interact in the actual environment.
Study instruction:
- Prepare test cases covering overlapping triggers, priority conflicts, battery depletion, a full dirt container, and obstacles near the dock.
Key point:
- Component tests do not prove that the integrated behavior system works.
- Interaction testing is essential for reliable control.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
17. Q: Why could simple subsumption behaviors respond faster than elaborate logical reasoning?
A: Show answer
The behaviors in the vacuum example are comparatively simple condition-response mechanisms. The chapter notes that they could be implemented directly using simple electrical circuits, avoiding the computational work required for extensive logical deduction. Because the response path is short, the robot can react quickly to changing sensor information and remain closely connected to its environment. This does not mean that circuits automatically make every behavior correct or that reasoning is always too slow. It means that a task such as immediate obstacle avoidance may be handled more efficiently by a direct response than by a lengthy process of constructing and evaluating a symbolic proof.
Study instruction:
- Compare the processing steps required for an immediate obstacle response and for a complex route-planning decision.
Key point:
- Simple mechanisms can be effective when rapid response is the main requirement.
- Computational complexity should match the demands of the task.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
18. Q: What does the Genghis robot illustrate about the subsumption architecture?
A: Show answer
Genghis illustrates that the architecture could coordinate many behaviors in a physical robot. The chapter describes it as a six-legged mobile robot controlled by fifty-seven behaviors arranged in a subsumption architecture. The example demonstrates the potential of behavior-based control for complex locomotion, where many local responses must work together to produce useful movement. It also shows why the approach attracted attention: the same task would have been difficult to construct using only knowledge-based AI techniques. The example should not be interpreted as proof that adding behaviors can solve every form of intelligence; it demonstrates the value of layered behavior for a particular class of robotic problems.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a multi-legged robot needs coordinated behavior and identify possible interactions among movement and stability responses.
Key point:
- Genghis demonstrated the practical potential of many coordinated behaviors.
- The architecture was influential in bringing robotics back into mainstream AI research.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
19. Q: Why did behavioral AI prove difficult to scale beyond relatively limited tasks?
A: Show answer
As the number of behaviors increases, the number of possible interactions among them becomes harder to understand and control. A small robot may be manageable because its required responses are limited and its task is narrowly defined. A larger system may contain many overlapping triggers, competing priorities, and sequences that are difficult to predict. The chapter describes behavioral design at this scale as a kind of black art: engineers often had to experiment to discover whether the system would work, making development time-consuming, costly, and unpredictable. The limitation is not that behavior-based methods are useless, but that they do not automatically provide a scalable solution for every complex problem.
Study instruction:
- Identify how the number of potential behavior interactions grows as a robot’s task set becomes more complex.
Key point:
- Behavioral systems can become difficult to design and validate as behaviors multiply.
- Practical success in a narrow task does not guarantee general scalability.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Jordanous, Intelligence without Representation: A Historical Perspective
20. Q: Why does the chapter reject the idea that reasoning and knowledge representation have no role in AI?
A: Show answer
Brooks’s critique showed that reasoning and explicit representation are not always the best starting point for intelligent behavior, particularly in tasks requiring immediate physical responses. However, the chapter argues that some problems naturally require reasoning, planning, or structured knowledge. It would be as inappropriate to make a vacuum robot solve every movement by logical deduction as it would be to expect a reasoning-intensive system to handle every complex task through a small set of reactive behaviors. The lesson is to choose methods according to the demands of the problem. A blanket rejection of reasoning repeats the same kind of dogmatism that behavioral AI originally challenged.
Study instruction:
- Give one example where a reactive response is preferable and another where explicit reasoning is useful.
Key point:
- Neither symbolic reasoning nor simple behavior is a universal solution.
- Effective AI design selects and combines methods according to task requirements.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
21. Q: Why did a combined behavioral and reasoning approach become influential?
A: Show answer
A combined approach retained the strongest lesson of Brooks’s work: intelligent systems must remain responsive to their environments. At the same time, it recognized that planning, knowledge representation, and reasoning remain valuable for tasks that cannot be handled by a few simple reactions. The resulting design does not treat the two traditions as mutually exclusive. A system may use fast behavioral mechanisms for immediate responses while using reasoning to select goals, plan a sequence, or interpret a more complex situation. The chapter presents this more moderate position as the direction that prevailed in agent-based AI, because it integrated practical responsiveness with capabilities inherited from earlier AI research.
Study instruction:
- Design a simple system that uses reactive obstacle avoidance alongside a planner for reaching a destination.
Key point:
- Behavioral responsiveness and deliberative reasoning can complement one another.
- Integration is often more useful than insisting on one exclusive paradigm.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
22. Q: What is an agent in the agent-based AI paradigm?
A: Show answer
An agent is a self-contained, autonomous AI system situated in an environment and carrying out a task on behalf of a user. The concept emphasizes an integrated entity rather than an isolated capability such as logical reasoning or planning. An agent must perceive relevant conditions, decide what to do, take action, and continue working toward its assigned task. Depending on the application, it may also cooperate with other agents. This integrated view was intended to avoid developing components of intelligence in isolation from the overall behavior they are meant to support. The environment and the task are therefore central to the agent’s design.
Study instruction:
- Describe an agent by identifying its environment, user-assigned task, inputs, actions, and degree of autonomy.
Key point:
- An agent is an integrated autonomous system acting in an environment for a purpose.
- It combines capabilities rather than representing only one isolated AI function.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
23. Q: What three capabilities characterize agents in the chapter’s account of agent-based AI?
A: Show answer
Agents are reactive, proactive, and social. Reactivity means that an agent is attuned to its environment and can adapt when circumstances change. Proactivity means that it can work systematically toward its assigned task rather than merely waiting for each instruction. Social capability means that it can interact with other agents when required, including communicating, cooperating, coordinating, and negotiating. These capabilities combine lessons from earlier AI traditions: behavioral AI emphasized responsiveness, while the earlier planning tradition emphasized goal-directed activity. Agent-based AI added the importance of interaction among agents, creating a broader model of autonomous intelligent activity.
Study instruction:
- For a digital assistant, identify one example of reactive, proactive, and social behavior.
Key point:
- Reactivity handles change, proactivity pursues goals, and sociality enables cooperation.
- An agent may need all three capabilities to complete a task effectively.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
24. Q: How did behavioral AI influence agent-based AI without determining its entire design?
A: Show answer
Behavioral AI established that an intelligent system should be situated and responsive to its environment. Agent-based AI accepted this lesson but did not adopt the most extreme position that reasoning and representation should be discarded. It sought an integrated system that could react to changing circumstances, pursue a task through planning, and cooperate with other agents. The influence was therefore substantial but selective: agent research incorporated the importance of situated behavior while preserving useful capabilities from symbolic and planning-based AI. This development illustrates how a new paradigm can build on earlier work without accepting every claim made by its strongest advocates.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a comparison showing which ideas agent-based AI retained from behavioral AI and which capabilities it restored from earlier approaches.
Key point:
- Agent-based AI inherited reactivity but did not abandon reasoning.
- Later paradigms can synthesize useful elements from competing traditions.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
25. Q: Why is social capability important for an autonomous agent?
A: Show answer
Many tasks cannot be completed by one agent acting alone. An agent may need information, services, resources, or actions from other agents. Social capability therefore involves more than sending a message: agents may have to cooperate toward a shared objective, coordinate the timing and sequence of their actions, and negotiate when their goals or resources conflict. Without these capabilities, individually competent agents may interfere with one another or fail to complete a larger task. The chapter identifies this focus on interaction among agents as a distinctive contribution of the agent-based paradigm, extending AI beyond isolated systems that operate independently.
Study instruction:
- Model a task requiring two agents and specify the information, commitments, and coordination each must exchange.
Key point:
- Multi-agent work requires cooperation, coordination, and sometimes negotiation.
- Communication alone does not guarantee coordinated action.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
26. Q: What is HOMER and why is it a useful example of a complete agent?
A: Show answer
HOMER was a simulated robot submarine operating in a two-dimensional sea world. It accepted instructions in a limited subset of English, with a vocabulary of about eight hundred words, and could plan and carry out tasks such as collecting a package and moving it to another location. It could adapt its plan when circumstances required. HOMER is useful because it illustrates an integrated system that understands instructions, maintains relevant information, plans actions, and interacts with its simulated environment. It also shows that the agent concept was intended to bring together capabilities rather than demonstrate only a single isolated technique.
Study instruction:
- List the language, planning, memory, and action capabilities demonstrated by HOMER in the chapter.
Key point:
- HOMER combined instruction handling, planning, and action in one agent.
- Its environment was a simulation, which limits what can be inferred about physical-world competence.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
27. Q: What does HOMER’s missing-log dialogue demonstrate about common-sense reasoning?
A: Show answer
HOMER is shown a log, learns that it belongs to the user, turns away, and then turns back after the user has moved the log. HOMER responds that it has lost the log. The exchange demonstrates that the agent retained information about an object and formed an expectation about what it would encounter after turning around. When the expected object was no longer present, it recognized a discrepancy. This is a modest example of common-sense reasoning about object identity, persistence, and changes in the environment. It also illustrates why an agent needs to relate its previous observations to what it currently perceives.
Study instruction:
- Explain the sequence of observations, stored information, expectation, and unexpected change in the log example.
Key point:
- Common-sense behavior can require memory and expectations about the environment.
- New observations may contradict what an agent previously believed.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
28. Q: How does HOMER’s package-delivery example illustrate planning and temporal reasoning?
A: Show answer
HOMER receives an instruction to deliver a package to a barge at a specified future time. It generates a plan, identifies that it must visit the pier to collect the package, and determines that it should leave at 8:56 p.m. to reach the barge by 9:00 p.m. It can also answer where the package will be after delivery and whether it will remain at the pier. The example demonstrates that an agent must reason about the order and duration of actions, the location of objects, and the future consequences of its plan. Planning is therefore more than selecting an immediate movement; it involves coordinating actions over time.
Study instruction:
- Construct a short delivery plan with locations, action durations, deadlines, and the expected location of the object after each step.
Key point:
- Planning links actions to time, location, and expected future states.
- An agent must account for the time required to complete its actions.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
29. Q: What limitation does HOMER share with earlier simulated AI systems?
A: Show answer
HOMER operates in a two-dimensional simulated sea world rather than in a physical ocean. Its demonstrations show that an agent can combine language understanding, planning, memory, and action within a model, but they do not establish that it can cope with every difficulty of a real environment. Physical systems face imperfect sensors, unexpected obstacles, changing conditions, and constraints that a simulation may omit. The chapter explicitly notes this limitation while recognizing that HOMER was more sophisticated than earlier simulated systems such as SHRDLU. A simulation is valuable for controlled experimentation, but its success must not be confused with proof of real-world robustness.
Study instruction:
- Identify which aspects of a simulated delivery task would become harder if HOMER had to operate in a physical ocean.
Key point:
- Simulation can demonstrate integrated capabilities while leaving physical-world challenges unresolved.
- Real-world deployment requires testing beyond the model.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
30. Q: How do software agents differ from traditional passive software applications?
A: Show answer
A traditional application generally waits for a user to select a command or perform an action. In the chapter’s contrast, the user remains the active agent and the software responds to explicit instructions. A software agent takes a more active role: it can monitor information, make predictions, and initiate actions on the user’s behalf when its design and confidence permit. The distinction is therefore about initiative and delegated activity, not merely about whether software uses artificial intelligence. An agent is intended to behave more like an assistant that cooperates with a user, while a conventional application is primarily a tool that responds to direct commands.
Study instruction:
- Compare a passive email application with an assistant that classifies, prioritizes, and processes messages under defined permissions.
Key point:
- Software agents can take initiative rather than waiting for every individual command.
- Delegated action requires appropriate limits and user expectations.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
31. Q: What problem were Pattie Maes’s software-agent prototypes intended to address?
A: Show answer
The prototypes explored how agents could reduce routine work and information overload. The chapter describes examples involving email management, meeting scheduling, news filtering, and music recommendation. These tasks require software to observe patterns, interpret user preferences, and help manage repeated decisions. The aim was not to create general intelligence but to provide practical assistance in everyday digital environments. The concept represented a change in human-computer interaction: software would no longer remain entirely passive but could cooperate with the user by anticipating useful actions. The article’s title, “Agents That Reduce Work and Information Overload,” captured this ambition.
Study instruction:
- Choose a repetitive digital task and explain how an agent could reduce effort without taking control away from the user.
Key point:
- Software agents were designed to assist with routine decisions and information management.
- Their value depends on useful predictions and appropriate delegation.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
32. Q: How did the email assistant described in the chapter learn when to act?
A: Show answer
The prototype observed how a user handled incoming email, such as reading a message immediately, filing it, or deleting it. A machine-learning algorithm used this behavioral history to predict what the user would do with a new message. The assistant would begin taking the initiative only when it was sufficiently confident in its prediction. This illustrates a basic pattern for delegated software action: observe behavior, learn a model of the user’s preferences, estimate the likely appropriate response, and act when the confidence level meets the system’s threshold. The example also raises practical questions about mistakes, oversight, and how users can correct an agent’s assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Specify what behavior data an email assistant might observe and what safeguards should apply before it automatically files or deletes a message.
Key point:
- Learning from user behavior can support personalized predictions.
- Confidence thresholds and user oversight matter when actions have consequences.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
33. Q: Why did the chapter suggest that software agents became practical only after advances in computing hardware?
A: Show answer
The ideas behind software agents emerged in the 1990s, but the computing resources available at the time were not sufficient for the practical mass-market systems described in the chapter. Natural-language interaction, continual processing of information, and responsive assistance require hardware capable of handling substantial computation within acceptable time and power limits. The spread of more powerful mobile devices in the second decade of the twenty-first century helped make such systems viable for everyday use. The point is that an attractive AI concept may precede its practical deployment by many years because implementation depends on hardware, software, data, and user-facing infrastructure developing together.
Study instruction:
- Identify the computational and infrastructure requirements that a modern mobile assistant must satisfy to operate responsively.
Key point:
- A research concept may exist long before it becomes commercially practical.
- Hardware capability can determine whether an AI application is deployable at scale.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
34. Q: How does the chapter connect early software-agent research to modern digital assistants?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents Siri, launched for the iPhone in 2010, as a descendant of the software-agent work developed during the 1990s. Siri was designed for natural-language interaction and for carrying out simple tasks on behalf of a user. Amazon Alexa, Google Assistant, and Microsoft Cortana are described as later realizations of the same broad vision. These systems illustrate the movement from passive applications toward software that can interpret requests and perform delegated tasks. The chapter’s historical point is about continuity of ideas: practical assistants did not appear without antecedents, but developed from earlier research once the necessary computing capabilities became available.
Study instruction:
- Trace one capability of a modern assistant back to the agent concepts described in the chapter.
Key point:
- Modern assistants have intellectual roots in earlier agent-based AI research.
- Practical deployment depended on later improvements in computing hardware.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
35. Q: Why did the goal of AI shift from imitating human choices toward making rational choices?
A: Show answer
The Turing-test tradition emphasizes behavior that is indistinguishable from human behavior. The agent paradigm asks a different question: if an AI system is acting on behalf of a user, should it copy the choices a human would make or select the action that best serves the user’s preferences? The chapter argues that delegated agents should make good choices rather than merely human-like ones. This shifts attention from imitation to decision quality. It does not imply that human behavior is irrelevant; human preferences still determine what counts as a desirable outcome. The key distinction is between reproducing a person’s behavior and selecting actions that achieve the outcomes the person values.
Study instruction:
- Compare a system that imitates a user’s usual action with one that selects the action that best meets the user’s stated objective.
Key point:
- Agent performance is judged by how well it serves the user’s preferences, not only by human resemblance.
- Rational action requires a way to represent what the user values.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
36. Q: What is a preference relation and why does an agent need one?
A: Show answer
A preference relation describes how a user ranks alternative outcomes. If an agent is to act on someone’s behalf, it needs information about which outcomes the user prefers. In the chapter’s fruit example, oranges are preferred to pears and pears are preferred to apples. This ranking allows the agent to compare available outcomes and select one that better matches the user’s wishes. A preference relation is not itself a monetary amount; it expresses an ordering of alternatives. Without a sufficiently clear preference model, an agent may be able to calculate or plan actions but still lack a defensible basis for deciding which outcome is best for its user.
Study instruction:
- Create a preference ranking for three outcomes and explain how it would guide an agent choosing between them.
Key point:
- Preferences describe the outcomes a user values.
- An agent needs a preference model to act meaningfully on someone’s behalf.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
37. Q: Why must preferences be consistent for an agent to make reliable choices?
A: Show answer
The chapter illustrates inconsistency with a cycle: oranges are preferred to pears, pears to apples, and apples to oranges. The first two comparisons suggest that oranges should be preferred to apples, but the third states the opposite. Such a cycle prevents the agent from constructing a stable ranking that can guide its decisions. The chapter connects rational choice to consistency requirements on preference relations. In practical systems, inconsistent or contradictory preference data can lead to unstable recommendations or arbitrary choices. The agent may need to ask the user to clarify priorities, resolve conflicting rules, or represent context-dependent preferences more carefully rather than pretending that the input defines one coherent ordering.
Study instruction:
- Test a three-outcome preference list for cycles and explain how contradictory rankings could affect an automated recommendation.
Key point:
- Inconsistent preferences do not provide a stable basis for ranking outcomes.
- Preference conflicts should be identified rather than hidden by arbitrary scoring.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
38. Q: How do utility values represent preferences for an AI agent?
A: Show answer
Utility values assign numbers to possible outcomes so that more preferred outcomes receive higher values. In the chapter’s simple example, an orange may receive utility 3, a pear 2, and an apple 1. The same ranking could be represented by 10, 9, and 0. For that deterministic ranking, the numerical magnitudes are less important than the ordering they express. Utility is therefore a mathematical representation of preference, not automatically a measure of money, pleasure, or moral worth. When decisions involve uncertain outcomes, the numerical representation must be suitable for the expected-utility calculations being used; the simple ranking example should not be treated as a complete account of every utility model.
Study instruction:
- Assign utility scores to a set of outcomes and explain which features of the scores express preference rather than physical quantity.
Key point:
- Utility provides a numerical representation of what an agent’s user prefers.
- Utility values should not automatically be interpreted as money.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
39. Q: What is expected utility and how does it guide an agent’s choice under uncertainty?
A: Show answer
Expected utility is the probability-weighted average of the utilities associated with an action’s possible outcomes. It allows an agent to compare options when no action guarantees a single result. The agent considers what outcomes may occur, how likely each outcome is, and how much the user values each one. It then selects an action with the highest expected utility, subject to the assumptions and constraints of the decision model. The chapter’s coin-toss example shows why the option with the largest possible reward is not necessarily the best choice: probabilities and less favorable outcomes must also be considered.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the expected utility of two options with different outcomes and probabilities, then explain why the larger possible payoff may not be preferable.
Key point:
- Expected utility combines outcome value with the probability of each outcome.
- Rational choice considers the full set of possible consequences, not only the best-case result.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
40. Q: Why is utility theory not necessarily about money or selfishness?
A: Show answer
Utility is a numerical way of representing preferences, and the preferences may concern any outcomes the user values. The chapter explicitly warns against treating utility as if it were always money or as if maximizing utility necessarily meant acting selfishly. An agent can represent preferences that prioritize helping others, protecting the environment, fairness, or another non-financial objective. The theory is neutral about what a person ought to value; it provides a framework for representing and acting on the preferences supplied to it. The practical challenge is to elicit those preferences accurately and ensure that the model reflects the user’s actual priorities rather than a narrow proxy such as financial gain.
Study instruction:
- Construct a preference example in which the highest-utility outcome is not the one with the greatest monetary reward.
Key point:
- Utility represents preference and is not synonymous with wealth.
- A rational agent can act on altruistic or non-financial preferences.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
41. Q: Why do most practical AI decisions need to account for uncertainty?
A: Show answer
Real-world systems rarely have perfect information about their environment or the consequences of their actions. Sensors may produce noisy readings, observations may be incomplete, and the same action may lead to different outcomes depending on conditions that are not under the agent’s control. A driverless vehicle, for example, may receive a sensor reading that indicates no obstacle even though an object is present. The reading still contains useful information, but it should not be treated as infallible. The chapter explains that AI needs a principled way to reason with imperfect evidence rather than simply accepting every observation or discarding uncertain data.
Study instruction:
- Identify sources of uncertainty in a sensor-based system and distinguish uncertainty in the world from uncertainty in the measurement.
Key point:
- Realistic AI must make decisions with incomplete and potentially inaccurate information.
- Imperfect data can be useful when its reliability is assessed appropriately.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology
42. Q: What is Bayesian inference and what problem does it solve for an AI agent?
A: Show answer
Bayesian inference is a method for updating beliefs when new evidence becomes available. An agent begins with a prior degree of belief, considers how likely the new evidence would be under different hypotheses, and calculates a posterior belief in light of that evidence. In the chapter’s robotics example, the hypothesis concerns whether an obstacle is present and the evidence is a sensor reading. Bayesian reasoning provides a disciplined way to combine prior information with imperfect observations. It neither treats the new reading as certainly correct nor ignores it. Instead, the agent adjusts its belief according to the evidence and the reliability of the observation.
Study instruction:
- Identify a hypothesis, a prior belief, new evidence, and the resulting posterior belief in a simple sensor example.
Key point:
- Bayesian inference updates beliefs in response to evidence.
- The strength of an update depends on both prior belief and evidential reliability.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology
43. Q: What does the chapter’s rare-disease test example teach about base rates?
A: Show answer
The example assumes that a disease affects one person in a thousand and that a test is 99 percent accurate. A positive result may appear to imply a 99 percent chance of disease, but that conclusion ignores how rare the disease is in the population. Among one thousand people, approximately one person has the disease. If the test correctly identifies that person about 99 percent of the time, there will be roughly one true positive. Among the 999 people without the disease, a 1 percent false-positive rate produces about ten false positives. Thus, only about one of the ten or eleven positive results is a true positive. The prior prevalence strongly affects the meaning of the test result.
Study instruction:
- Recreate the one-thousand-person calculation and identify the true positives, false positives, and proportion of positive results that are genuine cases.
Key point:
- Test accuracy alone does not determine the probability of disease after a positive result.
- Base rates and false positives must be considered together.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology
44. Q: Why can a highly accurate test still produce many false positives when a condition is rare?
A: Show answer
When prevalence is very low, the population without the condition is much larger than the population with it. Even a small false-positive rate applied to that large group can generate more positive results than the number of true positives. In the chapter’s illustration, a 1 percent false-positive rate among 999 people without the disease yields about ten false positives, while the one person with the disease is likely to produce one true positive. The phrase “99 percent accurate” therefore needs clarification: sensitivity, specificity, prevalence, and the definition of accuracy matter. The result should be interpreted using the relevant conditional probabilities rather than by equating test accuracy with the probability that a positive individual has the disease.
Study instruction:
- Change the assumed prevalence while holding sensitivity and specificity constant, then observe how the proportion of true positive results changes.
Key point:
- A small false-positive rate can have a large effect in a low-prevalence population.
- Conditional probabilities answer different questions and should not be confused.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology
45. Q: How can Bayesian reasoning help a robot build a map using noisy sensors?
A: Show answer
A robot exploring an unfamiliar building or planet may use sensors that sometimes report an obstacle where none exists or fail to detect an obstacle that is present. Rather than treating each reading as certain, the robot can use the probability that the sensor is correct to update its belief about the relevant location. A single observation may change the belief only modestly; repeated observations can provide additional evidence and progressively refine the map. This is important because movement decisions based on incorrect assumptions can cause collisions or navigation failure. Bayesian reasoning makes uncertainty explicit and allows the map to improve as more information is collected.
Study instruction:
- Work through a sequence of noisy obstacle readings and show how each observation should affect the robot’s confidence in the map.
Key point:
- Sensor readings should be weighted by their reliability.
- Repeated evidence can improve an agent’s estimate of its environment.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology
46. Q: What is the practical difference between accepting sensor data and updating beliefs from sensor data?
A: Show answer
Accepting a sensor reading treats the observation as if it directly establishes what is true. Updating a belief treats the observation as evidence whose strength depends on sensor reliability and what was believed before the reading arrived. If a sensor reports an obstacle, the agent should increase its belief that an obstacle exists by an amount appropriate to the evidence, not necessarily set that belief to certainty. If later observations conflict, the agent can revise its estimate again. This approach is more robust because it acknowledges that measurements can be wrong while preserving the information they provide. It also supports a transparent distinction between observation and the agent’s current estimate of the world.
Study instruction:
- Write two versions of a robot’s response to a sensor reading: one that assumes certainty and one that records a probability-based belief.
Key point:
- Evidence changes confidence; it does not automatically establish certainty.
- Beliefs should remain revisable as new observations arrive.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology
47. Q: How do expected utility and Bayesian reasoning work together in the agent paradigm?
A: Show answer
Expected utility provides a way to compare actions according to the preferences of the user and the possible outcomes of each action. Bayesian reasoning helps the agent maintain and update beliefs about the state of the world that influences those outcomes. The two functions are complementary: the agent needs beliefs about what is likely to happen and a representation of how much the user values the possible results. An agent can then evaluate actions under uncertainty rather than choosing solely from an unverified assumption about the environment. The chapter presents this combination as part of the mature agent paradigm that emerged by the end of the 1990s.
Study instruction:
- Build a simple decision example in which an agent updates the probability of an obstacle and then chooses a route using outcome preferences.
Key point:
- Bayesian reasoning supports beliefs about the world.
- Expected utility supports choices based on those beliefs and the user’s preferences.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology
48. Q: What did Deep Blue’s matches against Garry Kasparov demonstrate about AI in the 1990s?
A: Show answer
Deep Blue demonstrated that a specialized AI system could reach extraordinary performance in a well-defined intellectual domain. In the 1996 six-game match, Deep Blue won one game, but Kasparov won the match overall by four games to two. An upgraded system played Kasparov again in 1997 and won the match overall. The achievement attracted worldwide attention because chess had long been regarded as a demanding test of intelligence. The chapter uses the event as a visible sign of AI’s growing maturity, while making clear that success in chess did not establish that machines had achieved general intelligence or human-like understanding across all domains.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the result of the 1996 match from the outcome of the 1997 rematch and explain why the distinction matters historically.
Key point:
- Deep Blue’s 1997 match victory was a landmark in specialized AI performance.
- Success in one domain is not proof of general intelligence.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
49. Q: Why were advances in SAT solvers an important but less publicized achievement in AI?
A: Show answer
SAT, the satisfiability problem, asks whether a logical expression can be made true under some assignment of truth values. It is the first problem shown to be NP-complete and is fundamental because an efficient general solution to SAT would provide efficient solutions to all NP-complete problems. By the end of the 1990s, SAT solvers had become powerful enough to address industrial-scale problems and were increasingly useful across computing. The achievement was less visible than Deep Blue’s chess victory but important because it showed that algorithms could make difficult classes of computational problems practically manageable in many cases. It did not mean that every NP-complete instance had become easy; difficult cases still challenge even strong solvers.
Study instruction:
- Explain what SAT asks, why NP-completeness matters, and why practical solver success does not remove worst-case difficulty.
Key point:
- SAT solvers made many difficult real-world instances tractable in practice.
- Practical progress does not mean all NP-complete problems are easy in every case.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
50. Q: What does Chapter 4 identify as the main achievement and limitation of AI by the end of the 1990s?
A: Show answer
By the end of the 1990s, agent-based AI had established a more coherent foundation by combining autonomous agents, user preferences, rational decision-making, expected utility, and Bayesian belief revision. Deep Blue and increasingly capable SAT solvers demonstrated important practical achievements, while behavioral robotics showed how direct interaction with the environment could produce useful behavior. Nevertheless, these advances did not solve the problem of general AI or provide a complete roadmap to it. The chapter also notes that some researchers disliked the increasingly mathematical character of the field, even though probability, statistics, and economics had proved useful. Its closing point is historical humility: a paradigm that seems settled can still be disrupted by later developments.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter’s major technical achievements and identify what they did not establish about general intelligence.
Key point:
- AI gained a stronger scientific basis through agents, decision theory, and probabilistic reasoning.
- Progress in specific domains does not mean the wider problem of general intelligence is solved.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge, A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Normative Theories of Rational Choice: Expected Utility
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, Bayesian Epistemology

Table 6
Deep Learning's Breakthroughs, Applications, and Limits
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 5, “Deep Breakthroughs,” about the recent AI boom?
A: Show answer
The chapter traces the renewed prominence of artificial intelligence to the rapid progress of machine learning, especially neural networks and deep learning. It shows how advances in model architecture, training methods, data availability, and computing power produced systems with capabilities that were difficult to achieve through hand-coded rules. The chapter also argues that impressive results should not be mistaken for a complete solution to intelligence: opacity, robustness, grounding, and generalization remain significant challenges.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter as a progression from machine learning foundations through neural-network revivals to DeepMind’s achievements and the limits of deep learning."
Key point:
- The recent AI surge was driven by several mutually reinforcing technical and economic developments.
- Deep learning is powerful but does not resolve every problem associated with intelligence.

2. Q: Why did Google’s acquisition of DeepMind in 2014 become such a prominent event?
A: Show answer
DeepMind was a relatively small, little-known AI company, yet Google reportedly acquired it for about $650 million. The transaction signaled that a major technology company was willing to invest heavily in a research organization whose value was not explained by a conventional portfolio of products or a visible business plan. The acquisition helped make AI a mainstream commercial and public topic and contributed to wider investment, competition for researchers, and government attention.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the acquisition attracted attention beyond its financial value, distinguishing a research capability investment from buying an established product line."
Key point:
- DeepMind’s acquisition helped signal AI’s strategic value to technology companies.
- Investment and public interest grew as research progress became commercially consequential.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
3. Q: How did the public and industry perception of AI change during the 2010s?
A: Show answer
AI moved from a comparatively specialized research area to a highly visible commercial and policy priority. Media coverage increased, governments began national AI initiatives, companies invested in research and talent, and AI became a strategic technology across sectors. The chapter links this change principally to demonstrated machine-learning progress rather than to a sudden resolution of AI’s foundational questions. Hype, investment, and real technical achievements grew together, making careful evaluation especially important.

- AI’s visibility and commercial importance expanded rapidly.
- Public enthusiasm can amplify genuine progress while also producing exaggerated expectations.

4. Q: What is machine learning, and how does it differ from programming a complete explicit recipe?
A: Show answer
Machine learning develops programs that infer a useful mapping from inputs to outputs through experience or data rather than requiring developers to specify every decision rule. A programmer still chooses the learning method, data, objective, representation, and evaluation process, but the model’s parameters or decision patterns are learned. This approach is useful when the task is difficult to describe precisely with hand-written rules, such as interpreting handwriting or recognizing objects in varied images.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a rule-based solution with a learned model for a task where inputs vary widely and a reliable explicit recipe is difficult to construct."
Key point:
- Machine learning derives patterns from experience rather than relying only on manually specified rules.
- Developers still define the learning setup, objective, data, and evaluation.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
5. Q: Why is handwritten-text recognition a useful example of machine learning?
A: Show answer
Handwriting varies in shape, size, spacing, ink quality, and context. A fixed set of rules that handles every possible variation is difficult to design. A model can instead learn patterns from labeled examples of handwritten characters or words and use those patterns to predict the text in new images. The task illustrates why learning from examples is valuable when the input space is diverse and the relevant patterns are hard to describe exhaustively.
Study instruction:
- "List the sources of variation in handwriting and explain how examples can help a model recognize unseen instances."
Key point:
- Recognition problems often involve variation that is difficult to capture through exhaustive rules.
- Learned patterns can support predictions on new inputs.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
6. Q: What is supervised learning?
A: Show answer
Supervised learning trains a model using examples that include an input and a target label or value. The model adjusts its parameters to reduce the difference between its predictions and the supplied targets. In handwriting recognition, an image is paired with the correct character or text; in credit-risk modeling, records may be paired with a historical outcome. The quality, representativeness, labeling, and suitability of those examples strongly influence what the model learns.
Study instruction:
- "Create a simple supervised-learning dataset with inputs, labels, and a stated prediction objective."
Key point:
- Supervised learning uses labeled examples to learn a predictive mapping.
- Labels and data quality influence the learned model.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
7. Q: Why does supervised machine learning require a substantial amount of training data?
A: Show answer
A model must infer patterns from a limited sample of the possible inputs. If examples are too few or narrow, the model may fail to learn the variation needed for new cases. Training data should capture meaningful differences, noise, edge cases, and relevant context. More data is not automatically better, however: duplicates, biased sampling, unreliable labels, or irrelevant variables can undermine the learning process even when the dataset is large.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the relationship between sample coverage, label quality, and performance on cases not present in training."
Key point:
- Training data must represent the task’s meaningful variation.
- Dataset size alone does not guarantee useful learning.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
8. Q: What is generalization in machine learning?
A: Show answer
Generalization is a model’s ability to perform appropriately on inputs it did not encounter during training. It is the reason learning from examples is useful: the model should infer patterns rather than merely reproduce its training records. Generalization is assessed using data separated from the training process and, where possible, additional tests that reflect the intended real-world setting. Poor generalization can result from overfitting, distribution shifts, insufficient coverage, or an inappropriate model.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish training performance from validation and test performance using a small illustrative dataset."
Key point:
- A useful learned model must work beyond its training examples.
- Generalization needs evaluation on appropriately separated data.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
9. Q: What is feature extraction in machine learning?
A: Show answer
Feature extraction identifies or constructs measurable attributes that help a model learn the relationship between inputs and outcomes. In a credit-risk example, features might include repayment history, income, or debt obligations, but not every recorded field necessarily helps. Feature selection and representation influence model performance, interpretability, data requirements, and the risks of using inappropriate information. Modern neural networks may learn representations automatically, but input design and data quality remain important.
Study instruction:
- "For a prediction task, propose candidate features, justify their relevance, and identify which ones should be excluded or scrutinized."
Key point:
- Features represent information used to learn a prediction.
- Feature choices affect accuracy, data requirements, fairness, and privacy.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Systematic Review: Bias and Unfairness in Machine Learning Models
10. Q: What is the curse of dimensionality, and why does it matter when many features are included?
A: Show answer
As the number of dimensions grows, the space of possible feature combinations expands rapidly. Data becomes sparse relative to that space, making it harder for a model to learn reliable patterns from a finite sample. Additional features may require more data and computation, complicate model training, and create opportunities for noise or spurious relationships. The issue is not that every high-dimensional model fails, but that dimensionality changes the demands on data and learning.
Study instruction:
- "Describe why a dataset with many possible feature combinations can be sparse even when it contains numerous records."
Key point:
- High dimensionality can make learning harder by expanding the space the data must cover.
- Feature quantity should be balanced against relevance and available evidence.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
11. Q: What trade-offs arise when selecting features for a machine-learning model?
A: Show answer
Including useful features can improve predictive information, but irrelevant, redundant, poorly measured, or sensitive features can increase complexity and risk. Excluding a feature that carries important information may reduce performance or create misleading patterns. Feature selection should therefore be informed by the task, data provenance, domain knowledge, validation results, and ethical or legal constraints. It should not be treated as a purely mechanical exercise based on correlation or convenience.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a proposed feature set by considering predictive value, redundancy, privacy, fairness, and measurement quality."
Key point:
- Feature selection is a judgment about information and risk, not just model performance.
- Both omission and inclusion can have consequences.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- Systematic Review: Bias and Unfairness in Machine Learning Models
12. Q: How can a feature choice introduce bias into a credit-risk model?
A: Show answer
A feature can act as a proxy for protected or socially disadvantaged characteristics even when those characteristics are not explicitly included. The chapter’s neighborhood-address example illustrates how a seemingly simple predictor may encode structural differences and lead to discriminatory outcomes. Bias can also enter through historical labels, unequal data collection, missing information, or decision processes that reflect past discrimination. Assessing fairness requires examining the context and effects of features, not merely removing a few obvious fields.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a credit-risk feature and trace how it could proxy for a sensitive characteristic; propose a review and mitigation process."
Key point:
- Proxy variables can reproduce disadvantage without directly using a protected attribute.
- Fairness assessment requires examining data, features, outcomes, and context.
