- Systematic Review: Bias and Unfairness in Machine Learning Models
- AI Fairness 360: Detecting, Understanding, and Mitigating Unwanted Algorithmic Bias
13. Q: Why is data curation an important part of machine-learning development?
A: Show answer
Curated data is collected, cleaned, labeled, organized, and checked so that it is relevant and usable for the intended task. Curation can reveal missing fields, mislabeled examples, inconsistent categories, duplicates, and distribution problems. The ImageNet story in the chapter illustrates the value of a shared, carefully organized dataset for research and comparison. Data preparation is therefore a substantive part of model development, not merely administrative work.
Study instruction:
- "Design a data-curation checklist covering provenance, labeling, duplicates, representativeness, privacy, and quality review."
Key point:
- Data preparation shapes the evidence from which a model learns.
- Curated datasets support reproducibility and more meaningful evaluation.
Source links:
- NeurIPS 2012: ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks
14. Q: What is reinforcement learning?
A: Show answer
Reinforcement learning trains an agent to act in an environment and receive feedback, often in the form of rewards or penalties. The agent uses experience to improve the choices it makes over time. Unlike supervised learning, it is not necessarily given a correct label for every action or state. It must explore, learn from outcomes, and balance immediate rewards with future consequences. The chapter uses game-playing systems to illustrate this approach.
Study instruction:
- "Map an example environment into states, actions, rewards, and a learning objective."
Key point:
- Reinforcement learning improves behavior through interaction and feedback.
- The reward structure influences what the agent learns to pursue.
Source links:
- Mnih et al.: Human-level control through deep reinforcement learning
- Google DeepMind: Deep Reinforcement Learning
15. Q: What is a reward in reinforcement learning, and what does it represent?
A: Show answer
A reward is a feedback signal used to evaluate the consequences of an agent’s action or experience. A positive or negative reward can influence the agent’s future behavior, but the reward is only a proxy for the goal designers intend. If the reward is poorly specified, the agent may exploit it in ways that do not match the desired outcome. Reward design, monitoring, and evaluation therefore matter as much as the learning algorithm.
Study instruction:
- "Create a reward definition for a simple task and identify a behavior that could exploit the reward while violating the intended goal."
Key point:
- Reward signals shape behavior but may not capture the full intended objective.
- Reward design creates risks of unintended optimization.
Source links:
- Mnih et al.: Human-level control through deep reinforcement learning
- Google DeepMind: Deep Reinforcement Learning
16. Q: What is the credit-assignment problem in reinforcement learning?
A: Show answer
The credit-assignment problem concerns how an agent should attribute a later reward or penalty to earlier actions. In a long game, a final loss may depend on a few poor decisions, but the agent must identify which decisions contributed to the outcome. Treating every action as equally responsible would be misleading. Learning methods use estimates, temporal information, and other techniques to help distribute feedback, but credit assignment remains difficult when consequences are delayed or indirect.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a multi-step scenario in which a final reward depends on only some of the earlier actions and explain the attribution challenge."
Key point:
- Delayed feedback makes it difficult to identify which actions caused an outcome.
- Credit assignment is central to learning from sequences of decisions.
Source links:
- Mnih et al.: Human-level control through deep reinforcement learning
- Google DeepMind: Deep Reinforcement Learning
17. Q: How are artificial neural networks inspired by biological neurons?
A: Show answer
Artificial neural networks draw a simplified analogy to neurons that receive signals, combine them, and produce outputs. In the artificial model, numeric inputs are weighted and passed through a computational function. The analogy is useful for understanding the basic structure, but artificial networks are mathematical systems rather than faithful models of the complexity of biological brains. The chapter emphasizes the conceptual inspiration while explaining how network parameters are learned.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a simplified artificial neuron with a biological neuron and identify where the analogy stops being literal."
Key point:
- Artificial neural networks use simplified computational units inspired by neurons.
- The analogy does not imply that artificial networks reproduce biological cognition.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
18. Q: What is a perceptron?
A: Show answer
A perceptron is a basic artificial-neuron model that combines inputs using weights and compares the result with an activation threshold. Depending on the input values and the learned weights, it produces an output. Rosenblatt’s perceptron was important because it demonstrated an implemented neural-network learning model. It also provided a simple foundation for exploring how numerical parameters could be adjusted from examples to perform classification.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a perceptron with inputs, weights, a threshold, and an output; calculate the result for a small set of values."
Key point:
- A perceptron combines weighted inputs and applies an activation rule.
- It is a foundational model for understanding neural-network learning.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
19. Q: How do weights and activation thresholds determine a perceptron’s output?
A: Show answer
Each input contributes according to its weight. The perceptron combines the active inputs and compares the total with a threshold or equivalent bias term. If the combined value satisfies the activation rule, the unit produces an active output; otherwise it produces an inactive output. Weights can strengthen, weaken, or reverse the contribution of an input. Training adjusts these numerical parameters so the network produces outputs closer to the desired results.
Study instruction:
- "Work through a numerical example and show how changing one weight or threshold changes the classification."
Key point:
- Weights control the influence of inputs.
- The activation rule converts a weighted combination into an output.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
20. Q: Why are multilayer perceptrons more capable than single-layer perceptrons?
A: Show answer
Multilayer perceptrons contain multiple layers of units, allowing intermediate representations to transform the input before the final output is produced. With appropriate nonlinear activation functions, layers can represent patterns that a single linear decision boundary cannot capture. A network’s capacity depends on its architecture, parameters, training, and data, so adding layers does not automatically improve performance. Multilayer models made it possible to represent more complex relationships than early single-layer systems.
Study instruction:
- "Illustrate how a nonlinear problem such as XOR requires a richer representation than a single linear separator."
Key point:
- Multiple layers and nonlinearities enable more complex representations.
- Architecture alone does not guarantee successful training or useful performance.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
21. Q: Why does training a neural network involve finding suitable weights?
A: Show answer
The network’s behavior is determined by its parameters, especially the weights and biases connecting its units. Training searches for parameter values that make the model’s outputs align with the learning objective across the training examples. The process usually uses an error or loss measure and an optimization procedure to update the parameters. For large networks, the parameter set can be enormous, making training a computational and statistical challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a loss function and parameter updates work together to improve predictions over successive training steps."
Key point:
- Learned parameters determine how a neural network transforms inputs into outputs.
- Training is an optimization process guided by a learning objective.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- NeurIPS 2012: ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks
22. Q: What limitation of single-layer perceptrons did Minsky and Papert highlight?
A: Show answer
Minsky and Papert demonstrated limitations in the class of single-layer perceptron models, including their inability to represent certain functions such as XOR using a single linear decision boundary. The result was important mathematically, but its influence was often interpreted more broadly than the specific models and limitations examined. Multilayer networks were not subject to the same representational limitation, although effective training of multilayer networks was not yet available at the time.
Study instruction:
- "State the XOR problem and explain why a single linear boundary cannot separate its positive and negative cases."
Key point:
- Single-layer perceptrons have specific representational limitations.
- Those limitations should not be generalized to all neural-network architectures.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
23. Q: Why is the historical impact of the 1969 Perceptrons book still debated?
A: Show answer
The book documented mathematical limits of certain perceptron models, but it was sometimes taken to imply that neural networks generally were limited or unpromising. The chapter describes how research attention shifted away from neural nets toward symbolic approaches, while noting that multilayer networks had different capabilities. The debate concerns both the technical scope of the results and how research communities interpreted and acted on them.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the mathematical result from the broader historical interpretation and identify why the two should not be conflated."
Key point:
- Technical limitations of one model class do not settle the prospects of every model class.
- Research trajectories can be influenced by interpretation, resources, and institutional priorities.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
24. Q: What contributed to the first neural-network winter?
A: Show answer
Neural-network research declined sharply in the late 1960s as attention moved toward symbolic AI, following influential critiques and the practical difficulty of building and training more capable networks. The loss of prominent advocates and the limits of available hardware and methods also affected the field. The chapter treats the decline as part of AI’s broader cycles of enthusiasm and disappointment rather than as proof that neural networks were permanently invalid.
Study instruction:
- "Create a timeline showing early neural models, the decline in research interest, and the later revival."
Key point:
- Research interest can decline when methods, expectations, and resources do not align.
- A research winter does not necessarily mean the underlying idea is permanently unworkable.

- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
25. Q: What was the connectionist revival associated with Parallel Distributed Processing?
A: Show answer
The 1980s revival broadened neural-network models and emphasized distributed representations and processing across interconnected units. The Parallel Distributed Processing work helped establish a more general view of networks and provided a research setting for advances in training multilayer systems. It renewed interest in neural approaches and enabled applications beyond the earlier single-layer demonstrations, although the revival also faced practical limitations.
Study instruction:
- "Describe two ideas associated with connectionism and explain how they differed from the earlier perceptron focus."
Key point:
- Connectionism renewed neural-network research through richer models and distributed representations.
- The revival expanded capabilities but remained dependent on training methods and resources.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
26. Q: What is backpropagation, and why was it important for multilayer networks?
A: Show answer
Backpropagation computes how changes in a network’s parameters affect the loss by propagating error-related information backward through the layers. It provides gradients that an optimizer can use to update weights. This made training multilayer neural networks practical in a way that earlier researchers had struggled to achieve. Backpropagation is not itself a complete learning system: it depends on the architecture, loss, data, optimization strategy, and other design choices.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the forward calculation, loss calculation, backward gradient calculation, and parameter update in a simple network."
Key point:
- Backpropagation supplies gradients for learning through multilayer networks.
- Training also depends on the loss, optimizer, data, and architecture.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- NeurIPS 2012: ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks
27. Q: Why did the connectionist revival lose momentum in the 1990s?
A: Show answer
The chapter attributes the decline in neural-network enthusiasm partly to limitations in computing power, which made the newer techniques difficult to scale. At the same time, other machine-learning approaches appeared to make faster practical progress. The setback was therefore not simply a discovery that neural networks were fundamentally defective; it also reflected resource constraints, research competition, and expectations about what the methods could deliver.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the technical promise of multilayer networks with the computing constraints researchers faced at the time."
Key point:
- Hardware limitations affected the practical pace of neural-network research.
- Research attention is influenced by competing methods and perceived progress.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
28. Q: What does the chapter mean by deep learning being deep in multiple senses?
A: Show answer
Deep learning involves networks with more layers, larger numbers of units, and often many more connections and parameters. Layer depth can allow the model to build representations from simpler patterns toward more abstract ones, while scale increases the capacity to learn complex mappings. These characteristics are not independent: architecture, data, training techniques, and computing resources work together. The term therefore refers to a family of techniques rather than a single isolated innovation.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the three scale-related meanings of deep learning described in the chapter and connect each to model capacity."
Key point:
- Deep learning combines depth, scale, and interconnected representations.
- The label describes a family of related approaches, not one magic technique.
Source links:
- Michael Wooldridge: A Brief History of Artificial Intelligence
- NeurIPS 2012: ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks
29. Q: How can layers in a deep neural network build progressively more abstract representations?
A: Show answer
In a vision network, early layers may respond to local patterns such as edges or textures, while later layers combine those patterns into more complex representations. This progression is a useful conceptual description, although actual learned features are not always neatly interpretable or organized in a simple hierarchy. The network learns representations through training rather than being explicitly programmed with each abstraction. The usefulness of the representation depends on the task and data.
Study instruction:
- "Use a visual-recognition example to describe possible low-level, intermediate, and higher-level representations without treating them as fixed rules."
Key point:
- Layers can transform inputs into progressively richer representations.
- Learned representations may be useful without being easily interpretable.

30. Q: Why did increasing network size become important for deep learning?
A: Show answer
Larger networks can represent more complex patterns by using more parameters and units, but this capacity creates greater demands for data, computing, optimization, and validation. Scale can improve performance when the training setup supports it, yet it can also lead to overfitting, cost, and difficulties in deployment. The chapter emphasizes that larger networks were one ingredient in deep learning’s success, not a sufficient explanation by themselves.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a small and a large model in terms of capacity, data needs, computational cost, and risks of overfitting."
Key point:
- Model scale can increase capacity but also increases resource and evaluation demands.
- Bigger models require a supporting data and training system.

31. Q: What role did training techniques play in the rise of deep learning?
A: Show answer
Deeper networks required effective methods for optimizing large parameter sets. Research on training procedures, including backpropagation and later innovations, helped make increasingly complex models workable. A useful model also needs an objective, stable optimization, appropriate regularization, and careful validation. The chapter portrays progress as a combination of methods and enabling conditions rather than a single breakthrough.

32. Q: Why were data and computing power crucial alongside neural-network architecture?
A: Show answer
Deep learning requires many examples and substantial computation to train large networks. Data supports learning patterns and variation, while computing power makes repeated calculations and parameter updates feasible at scale. The architecture and learning method may be promising, but without suitable data and processing resources, training can be too slow or inadequate. The chapter presents these factors as complementary ingredients in the deep-learning breakthrough.

33. Q: What was the ImageNet project, and why did it matter?
A: Show answer
ImageNet was developed as a large, organized image dataset with category labels that could support training and evaluation of visual-recognition systems. The chapter describes Fei-Fei Li’s vision of providing the research community with a shared resource. Carefully classified images allowed researchers to train models, compare methods, and test progress on a common task. ImageNet helped make data availability and benchmark evaluation central to the deep-learning story.

34. Q: What was AlexNet’s contribution to the 2012 ImageNet competition?
A: Show answer
AlexNet demonstrated that a large convolutional neural network trained with substantial computing resources could sharply improve image-classification performance. Its result helped change expectations about neural networks in computer vision and encouraged researchers to investigate deep architectures. The achievement reflected the interaction of network design, labeled data, GPU computation, and training techniques, not merely an increase in layers.

35. Q: How do common benchmarks help machine-learning research?
A: Show answer
Benchmarks provide shared tasks, datasets, metrics, and evaluation procedures that allow researchers to compare systems. They can make progress visible, support reproducibility, and help identify weaknesses. However, a benchmark may not reflect real-world conditions, and performance can be affected by data leakage, narrow objectives, or over-optimization for the metric. Benchmark results should therefore be interpreted alongside task-specific evaluation.
Study instruction:
- "Design a benchmark review checklist that examines dataset provenance, metrics, test separation, reproducibility, and real-world relevance."
Key point:
- Benchmarks enable comparisons but can be imperfect proxies for real performance.
- A model should be evaluated beyond a single benchmark when deployment stakes warrant it.
Source links:
- NeurIPS 2012: ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks
36. Q: Why is machine learning especially useful when an explicit recipe is difficult to construct?
A: Show answer
For tasks such as image recognition, translation, or speech processing, the variation and context are difficult to enumerate through rules. Learning methods can infer useful patterns from examples, allowing the system to handle inputs that differ from the training cases. This does not mean the model understands the task in the same way a human does, nor that it will work reliably on every novel input. It means a learned representation can be more practical than exhaustive hand-coded rules.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a task with complex variation and explain why a learned model may be more feasible than a manually specified recipe."
Key point:
- Learning can address tasks where rules are difficult to enumerate.
- Learned capability must still be tested for generalization and failure.

- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Artificial Intelligence
37. Q: Why can a deep neural network be difficult to explain?
A: Show answer
A network’s learned expertise is distributed across many numerical parameters and representations. It may produce a prediction without providing a transparent sequence of rules that a person can inspect. Some explanation methods can approximate or visualize aspects of a model’s behavior, but they do not automatically reveal a complete causal account of why the model made a decision. The chapter contrasts neural networks with systems whose explicit rules can sometimes be traced more directly.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a rule-based explanation with a neural-network explanation and identify the limitations of each approach."
Key point:
- Neural-network parameters can encode complex behavior that is not directly interpretable.
- Explanation tools may be informative without providing a complete causal explanation.

38. Q: Why is opacity a concern in high-stakes AI applications?
A: Show answer
When a model affects medical diagnosis, lending, employment, or safety, users and affected people may need to understand the basis of decisions, assess errors, challenge outcomes, and verify accountability. An opaque model can make those processes harder, especially when predictions are uncertain or biased. Deployment should therefore include testing, documentation, oversight, appropriate explanation, escalation, and a clear decision responsibility rather than relying on a prediction alone.
Study instruction:
- "For one high-stakes use, identify who needs an explanation, what evidence they need, and how a decision can be reviewed or appealed."
Key point:
- High-stakes decisions require accountability and review beyond prediction accuracy.
- Opacity increases the importance of governance and human oversight.

- Systematic Review: Bias and Unfairness in Machine Learning Models
39. Q: What are adversarial examples in machine learning?
A: Show answer
Adversarial examples are inputs altered in a way that causes a model to make an incorrect prediction, sometimes through changes that are difficult for a human to notice. The chapter’s panda-versus-gibbon illustration shows how a subtle image modification can mislead a neural network. Adversarial vulnerability demonstrates that strong performance on ordinary test data does not guarantee robustness against deliberately manipulated or unusual inputs.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the adversarial-example concept and distinguish it from an ordinary random error."
Key point:
- A model can be fooled by carefully chosen input changes.
- Robustness is a distinct property from performance on clean data.
Source links:
- ACM Computing Surveys: Interpreting Adversarial Examples in Deep Learning
40. Q: Why does adversarial robustness matter when a model is used outside a research benchmark?
A: Show answer
Real-world systems encounter changing conditions, imperfect sensors, unusual inputs, and sometimes deliberate manipulation. An adversarial failure may cause a model to misclassify an object, misinterpret a sign, or make an unsafe recommendation. Testing should therefore include relevant threats, input variations, monitoring, fallback behavior, and procedures for responding to failures. The chapter warns that deep learning should not be used in sensitive applications without understanding these weaknesses.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a threat-oriented test plan for a model that processes images in a real environment."
Key point:
- Robustness testing should reflect the intended operating environment and threats.
- Safety-critical use requires fallback mechanisms and monitoring.
Source links:
- ACM Computing Surveys: Interpreting Adversarial Examples in Deep Learning
41. Q: How can a subtle image perturbation cause a neural network to misclassify an object?
A: Show answer
A model’s decision depends on the learned representation and decision boundary. An adversarial perturbation may alter features or activation patterns enough to move an input across that boundary, even when the change appears negligible to a human observer. The underlying mechanisms are an active research area, and the chapter uses the example to demonstrate a practical vulnerability rather than claiming a single universal explanation. Robustness tests must consider both digital and physical inputs.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the model-level consequence of a small input change and explain why the human visual impression may not predict the model’s response."
Key point:
- Small perturbations can change a model’s classification.
- Adversarial vulnerability is not necessarily visible to human observers.
Source links:
- ACM Computing Surveys: Interpreting Adversarial Examples in Deep Learning
42. Q: What is the difference between an adversarial example and ordinary distribution shift?
A: Show answer
An adversarial example is typically crafted to cause a model failure, often by manipulating an input in a targeted or constrained way. Distribution shift occurs when the data encountered during deployment differs from the data used in training, whether or not anyone is manipulating it. Both can reduce performance, and the categories can overlap. A robust evaluation should test deliberate threats and naturally occurring changes such as lighting, weather, population differences, or new operating conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Give one adversarial example and one unintentional distribution-shift example, then explain how their tests might differ."
Key point:
- Adversarial manipulation and distribution shift are related but distinct risks.
- Evaluation should cover deliberate and naturally occurring changes.
Source links:
- ACM Computing Surveys: Interpreting Adversarial Examples in Deep Learning
43. Q: Why can adversarial changes to road signs be dangerous for autonomous vehicles?
A: Show answer
A sign alteration that is visually subtle to a human may be interpreted incorrectly by a neural-network perception system. If that interpretation influences vehicle behavior, the failure can affect safety rather than simply produce an incorrect label. The chapter uses this example to show that robustness is not a cosmetic property. Systems should be tested under physical-world conditions, sensor variation, adversarial threats, and realistic operational constraints, with safety mechanisms that do not depend on one model output.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a sign-recognition failure pathway and propose independent safety checks or fallback actions."
Key point:
- Perception errors can have safety consequences when they drive physical actions.
- Robustness must be evaluated under realistic physical conditions.
Source links:
- ACM Computing Surveys: Interpreting Adversarial Examples in Deep Learning
44. Q: What is DeepMind’s significance in the chapter’s history of deep learning?
A: Show answer
DeepMind is presented as a high-profile research organization whose work exemplified the rise of deep learning. Its Atari agent and AlphaGo systems demonstrated learning capabilities that attracted both scientific and public attention. The chapter does not treat these achievements as proof of general intelligence; instead, they show how specialized learning systems can reach impressive performance when trained with data, computation, and carefully designed objectives.

- "Identify DeepMind’s major examples in the chapter and distinguish their achievements from the broader goal of general intelligence."

45. Q: How did DeepMind’s Atari agent learn to play games?
A: Show answer
The agent received screen images, game scores, and a set of possible actions, then learned through repeated interaction and reward feedback. It was not supplied with detailed game-specific rules or hand-designed visual features for each game. The approach combined deep learning with reinforcement learning, allowing the system to learn representations and action preferences from its experience. This demonstrated a striking way to learn behavior from raw sensory input and feedback.
Study instruction:
- "Map the Atari agent’s observations, actions, rewards, and learning loop; explain what information it was not given."

46. Q: What made DeepMind’s Atari learning approach different from a hand-coded game-playing program?
A: Show answer
A traditional game-playing program may encode rules, object locations, strategies, or search procedures supplied by developers. DeepMind’s Atari agent learned from pixels, score feedback, and available actions, without access to the emulator’s internal state or game-specific feature descriptions. The distinction is important because the system had to learn representations and strategies through experience rather than relying on an explicit knowledge model of each game.

47. Q: How did reinforcement learning help DeepMind’s Atari system improve?
A: Show answer
The agent played repeatedly, observed outcomes, and used reward signals to update the behavior associated with actions and game states. Over time, the learned policy became more effective at maximizing score. The approach requires balancing experimentation with exploiting actions that appear valuable, and it can be difficult when feedback is delayed. The chapter’s examples show that useful strategies can emerge without being explicitly specified by developers.

The result demonstrated that a deep reinforcement-learning approach could learn strong behavior across varied game tasks using a common learning framework. It did not show that one trained model could automatically transfer all its learned knowledge to every game or that the system possessed general intelligence; task coverage and evaluation protocol matter.

49. Q: What strategy did DeepMind’s Atari agent discover in Breakout?
A: Show answer
After learning to keep the ball in play and break bricks, the agent discovered that drilling a hole through the side of the brick wall could let the ball bounce above the wall and destroy bricks efficiently. This behavior was not explicitly prescribed by the developers. It illustrates how optimization through experience can uncover an effective strategy that may be surprising to people, while still being tied to the game’s reward and environment.

50. Q: Why was Go a difficult target for AI compared with chess?
A: Show answer
Go has a large board and a high branching factor, which makes the number of possible positions and sequences of moves enormous. Evaluating a board position is also difficult because the value of a move may emerge from interactions across the board and over many turns. Traditional search methods that work well in smaller or more constrained games were insufficient on their own. The chapter explains why learning-based evaluation and search became important in Go.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the board sizes and branching factors of Go and chess, then explain how the search-space difference affects planning."
Key point:
- Go’s large search space makes exhaustive search impractical.
- Position evaluation and selective search are central to strong Go play.
Source links:
- Nature: Mastering the game of Go with deep neural networks and tree search
51. Q: What are the policy and value networks used in AlphaGo?
A: Show answer
The policy network estimates or recommends promising moves from a board position, while the value network estimates how good a position is in terms of likely game outcome. Together, they help direct search toward promising choices and provide evaluations without relying solely on random simulations or handcrafted rules. The networks were trained using human expert games and reinforcement learning, combining learned patterns with search.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the distinct roles of the policy and value networks and how they can support search."
Key point:
- Policy networks guide move selection; value networks assess positions.
- Learned evaluation supports more focused search.
Source links:
- Nature: Mastering the game of Go with deep neural networks and tree search
52. Q: What is Monte Carlo tree search, and why did it help AlphaGo?
A: Show answer
Monte Carlo tree search explores a game’s decision tree selectively, using repeated simulations or estimates to evaluate promising branches. It balances exploration of less-visited options with exploitation of branches that appear valuable. AlphaGo combined this search process with policy and value networks, allowing it to focus computation on likely strong moves and estimate outcomes more effectively than a purely brute-force approach.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a decision tree and explain how selective exploration can allocate more computation to promising branches."
Key point:
- Monte Carlo tree search allocates search selectively rather than exploring every possible move.
- AlphaGo combined search with learned policy and value estimates.
Source links:
- Nature: Mastering the game of Go with deep neural networks and tree search
53. Q: What did AlphaGo’s victory over Fan Hui demonstrate?
A: Show answer
AlphaGo defeated European Go champion Fan Hui in a five-game match, marking a major achievement for computer Go. The result showed that the combination of deep neural networks, learning from human games and self-play, and Monte Carlo tree search could reach professional-level performance. It was a landmark in specialized AI performance, but it remained a result in a constrained game domain rather than evidence of general-purpose intelligence.

AlphaGo defeated Lee Sedol four games to one in the 2016 match, an outcome that surprised many observers. The event demonstrated the strength of the system and showed that machine learning and search could produce moves that challenged human experts. The chapter also cautions against anthropomorphizing the program: its performance reflected optimization and learned representations, not necessarily human-like motives or understanding.

A: Show answer
AlphaGo Zero learned through self-play and reinforcement learning without relying on human expert-game data as its training source. Its network learned to predict moves and outcomes from games it generated, and the improved network strengthened the search process, which in turn generated better training experience. The approach demonstrated that a system could achieve superhuman performance in Go by learning from the game’s rules and repeated self-play rather than human demonstrations.

61. Q: What does grounding mean in the chapter’s discussion of human understanding?
A: Show answer
Grounding connects symbols, language, or representations to experience and the world in which they are used. A human interpretation of an image can draw on memory, social context, physical experience, and knowledge of events. The chapter argues that a deep-learning system trained to map inputs to outputs may lack this richer grounding. Grounding is therefore relevant to understanding, not merely to producing a statistically plausible description.
Study instruction:
- "Use a familiar image or phrase to show how background experience changes interpretation beyond literal pattern recognition."
Key point:
- Grounding links representations to experience and context.
- Language or image mappings alone may not capture the full basis of human interpretation.

The example illustrates why a translation task is not simply a word-to-word mapping and why background knowledge can affect interpretation.

70. Q: What risks can arise when machine-learning systems are deployed without examining their training data?
A: Show answer
A system may reproduce biases, learn irrelevant correlations, perform poorly on underrepresented groups, or fail when the deployment population differs from its training data. Labels may also reflect past decisions rather than a neutral ground truth. Data review should examine provenance, sampling, missingness, quality, sensitive variables, and downstream effects. Performance metrics alone may conceal unequal errors or harms.
Study instruction:
- "Design a data audit for a model used in a decision-making context, including group coverage and potential proxy variables."
Key point:
- Training data can encode historical or structural bias.
- Data auditing should examine coverage, provenance, labels, and downstream effects.

71. Q: How should a machine-learning model be evaluated before being used in a sensitive application?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should test accuracy and relevant error types, robustness, data representativeness, calibration, fairness, usability, and performance under realistic conditions. A model should be compared with appropriate baselines and reviewed for failure modes that could cause harm. Deployment decisions should consider whether the model’s limitations are acceptable, how users will be informed, and what controls or fallback procedures will be available. Evaluation is an ongoing process, not a one-time test.
Study instruction:
- "Create an evaluation matrix for a sensitive model covering performance, fairness, robustness, explainability, and operational controls."
Key point:
- Evaluation should be multidimensional and tied to the real use case.
- Monitoring and controls remain necessary after deployment.

72. Q: Why is human oversight important when deep learning is used for consequential decisions?
A: Show answer
Human oversight can provide contextual judgment, review unusual cases, challenge model outputs, and take responsibility for decisions that the model cannot adequately explain or validate. However, oversight is only meaningful when reviewers have authority, information, competence, and time to intervene. A nominal human-in-the-loop process may simply rubber-stamp outputs. Governance should therefore specify escalation, independent review, documentation, and accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Specify the responsibilities and escalation authority of a human reviewer for a consequential model decision."
Key point:
- Human oversight must be substantive and supported by authority and information.
- Reviewers should be able to challenge and override model outputs.

73. Q: What is the difference between a model’s performance and its reliability in deployment?
A: Show answer
Performance often refers to results on defined evaluation metrics and datasets. Reliability concerns whether the system continues to behave appropriately across the conditions, users, inputs, and changes encountered in actual operation. A high score on a benchmark may not capture robustness, fairness, security, calibration, or operational failure. Reliability requires broader testing, monitoring, and controls tied to the deployment context.
Study instruction:
- "Identify metrics that measure performance and separate them from reliability checks such as drift monitoring, robustness tests, and incident review."
Key point:
- Benchmark performance is only one part of deployment reliability.
- Reliability requires testing and monitoring across real operating conditions.
Source links:
- ACM Computing Surveys: Interpreting Adversarial Examples in Deep Learning
- Systematic Review: Bias and Unfairness in Machine Learning Models
74. Q: How can organizations monitor a deep-learning system after deployment?
A: Show answer
Organizations should track input and output distributions, performance where labels become available, error patterns, group-level impacts, drift, incidents, and user feedback. Monitoring should include thresholds and escalation procedures, and the model should be re-evaluated when data, objectives, or operating conditions change. Version control, audit trails, and rollback capability help ensure that problems can be investigated and corrected. Monitoring must be planned before deployment rather than added after failures.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a monitoring plan with indicators, responsible roles, review frequency, escalation triggers, and rollback criteria."
Key point:
- Deployment monitoring detects changes and emerging failures.
- Monitoring requires defined ownership and corrective procedures.
