Table 7
AI Today: Scientific Discovery, Health Care, and Autonomous Vehicles
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 6, “AI Today,” about the expanding role of artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that advances in deep learning have moved AI from a largely specialist research activity into a technology with practical applications across science, health care, transport, commerce, agriculture, entertainment, and other fields. Its examples show several different capabilities: reconstructing scientific images from incomplete measurements, generating synthetic images, predicting protein structures, analyzing health data, interpreting medical images, and controlling vehicles. The chapter is not simply a celebration of technical progress. It also examines the limits of systems operating in complex environments, the consequences of inaccurate or overconfident use, and the need to keep human judgment and accountability in the loop. Its central message is that AI can extend human capability, but its benefits depend on appropriate data, reliable evaluation, sound deployment, and safeguards.
Study instruction:
- Create a concept map linking the chapter’s scientific, medical, and transport examples to the AI capability each one demonstrates. Add the main benefit and principal risk beside every example.
Key point:
- AI is moving into many real-world domains because learning systems can extract useful patterns from data.
- Technical capability must be matched with evaluation, governance, and human responsibility.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
2. Q: Why does the chapter describe deep learning as opening the floodgates for practical AI applications?
A: Show answer
Deep learning made it possible to learn complex representations from large collections of examples rather than relying only on manually specified rules. This is especially valuable for data such as images, signals, and other high-dimensional inputs, where useful features can be difficult to design by hand. The chapter connects this progress with the wider availability of data, powerful processors, connected devices, and cloud infrastructure. These conditions allow organizations to train models, deploy them in products, and collect further data from real use. Deep learning did not make every problem solvable, nor did it remove the need for domain expertise. It changed the range of tasks for which machine learning could be attempted at useful scale.
Study instruction:
- Explain how data, computing power, model architecture, and deployment infrastructure interact. For one application, identify which of these factors is the main constraint.
Key point:
- Deep learning learns complex patterns from examples, especially in images and other unstructured data.
- Progress depends on data and infrastructure as well as algorithms.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework Development
3. Q: How do the chapter’s examples illustrate different types of AI capability?
A: Show answer
The black-hole image example concerns reconstruction and interpretation of scientific measurements. Generative adversarial networks create new synthetic images. AlphaFold predicts biological structures from sequence and related information. Wearable-health systems analyze continuous sensor streams to identify patterns that may signal a health event. Medical-imaging systems classify or segment scans. Autonomous vehicles combine perception, prediction, planning, and control to act in a changing physical environment. These tasks should not be treated as interchangeable. A model that generates a plausible image is not necessarily a reliable diagnostic tool, and a system that classifies a static scan does not automatically have the ability to operate safely in an open-world environment.
Study instruction:
- Classify each named application as reconstruction, generation, prediction, classification, or action. Note where a system combines more than one capability.
Key point:
- AI applications differ in what they infer, generate, or do.
- Performance in one task does not establish competence in another.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
4. Q: How did AI-related algorithms contribute to the first image of the black hole in galaxy M87?
A: Show answer
The Event Horizon Telescope linked radio observatories around the world so that they functioned as an Earth-sized virtual telescope. The measurements were sparse and did not form an ordinary photograph that could simply be assembled pixel by pixel. Imaging algorithms were therefore needed to reconstruct an image consistent with the recorded measurements and with reasonable assumptions about the source. The chapter describes this as an example of AI helping to predict or infer missing image information. The scientific result depended on the telescope network, calibration, data processing, imaging methods, and collaboration among researchers. It should not be simplified into the claim that an AI system independently photographed a black hole.
Study instruction:
- Describe the chain from radio observations to a reconstructed image. Distinguish measured data from algorithmically inferred image structure.
Key point:
- The image was reconstructed from measurements made by a global radio-telescope network.
- Algorithms supported scientific inference; they did not replace observation or validation.
Source links:
- NAOJ: Astronomers Capture First Image of a Black Hole
- Event Horizon Telescope: Science and Image Reconstruction
5. Q: Why is image reconstruction different from ordinary image enhancement?
A: Show answer
Image reconstruction attempts to infer a plausible underlying scene from incomplete or indirect measurements. In the Event Horizon Telescope example, the observations came from a distributed radio array with limited sampling of the source’s spatial information. An algorithm must find image structures that are compatible with those measurements and with the assumptions built into the reconstruction method. Ordinary enhancement may adjust contrast, sharpen edges, or reduce noise in an already formed image. Reconstruction is more fundamental because it estimates what the image could have been before the instrument and sampling process obscured or omitted information. The result must therefore be tested for stability and sensitivity to assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Compare a reconstruction task with a simple sharpening filter. Identify the observations, assumptions, and validation tests required for each.
Key point:
- Reconstruction infers an underlying scene from incomplete measurements.
- A plausible-looking result is not sufficient evidence that an inferred feature is real.
Source links:
- Event Horizon Telescope: Science and Image Reconstruction
6. Q: Why did independent imaging teams matter in the Event Horizon Telescope project?
A: Show answer
Independent teams provide a check against a result being an artifact of one algorithm, one parameter choice, or one analyst’s expectations. The National Astronomical Observatory of Japan describes how multiple teams used different imaging approaches and obtained closely similar results, while researchers varied input parameters to test whether important image features remained stable. Agreement across genuinely different methods does not prove every detail beyond doubt, but it strengthens confidence when the methods are grounded in the same observations and their limitations are understood. This is a general lesson for AI-assisted science: validation should examine robustness, alternative explanations, and the sensitivity of conclusions to modelling choices.
Study instruction:
- Design a validation plan for an AI-assisted scientific image. Include independent methods, parameter sensitivity, and comparison with the original measurements.
Key point:
- Independent methods can reveal whether a result depends excessively on one modelling choice.
- Robustness testing strengthens, but does not make infallible, a scientific inference.
Source links:
- NAOJ: Astronomers Capture First Image of a Black Hole
- Event Horizon Telescope: Science and Image Reconstruction
7. Q: What is a generative adversarial network, and how does it create synthetic images?
A: Show answer
A generative adversarial network, or GAN, contains two neural networks trained in competition. The generator produces synthetic examples, while the discriminator attempts to distinguish generated examples from real training data. The generator is updated to make its outputs more convincing, and the discriminator is updated to become better at detecting generated samples. This adversarial process can produce highly realistic images when training is successful. The chapter uses synthetic human faces to illustrate how convincing generated images can appear. A GAN does not retrieve a hidden real person from a database simply because an output looks realistic; it learns statistical structure from training data and generates a new sample.
Study instruction:
- Draw the generator–discriminator training loop. Explain what feedback each network receives and why the two networks must improve in relation to one another.
Key point:
- The generator creates samples and the discriminator evaluates whether they resemble real data.
- A convincing synthetic image is not evidence that the depicted person exists.
Source links:
- Ian Goodfellow: NIPS Tutorial on Generative Adversarial Networks
8. Q: What practical opportunities and risks arise when AI can generate realistic but fictional people?
A: Show answer
Synthetic faces can support visual effects, game design, simulation, privacy-preserving demonstrations, and the creation of fictional characters. The same capability can also be used to fabricate evidence, impersonate people, create non-consensual imagery, or manipulate public understanding. The risk is not limited to whether an image can be detected as fake. A fabricated image may spread before verification, and viewers may make decisions based on its apparent authenticity. Responsible use requires clear disclosure, permission where a real person’s likeness is involved, provenance records, secure handling of source material, and procedures for reporting misuse. Organizations should consider both the content and the context in which generated media is distributed.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short policy for synthetic portraits covering consent, disclosure, provenance, approval, retention, and response to suspected impersonation.
Key point:
- Generative capability creates both creative value and opportunities for deception.
- Provenance, consent, and clear disclosure are essential safeguards.
Source links:
- Ian Goodfellow: NIPS Tutorial on Generative Adversarial Networks
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
9. Q: What is the protein-folding problem addressed by AlphaFold?
A: Show answer
Proteins are chains of amino acids that fold into three-dimensional structures. Their shapes influence how they function and interact with other molecules. Predicting a protein’s structure from its sequence is difficult because many possible configurations and interactions affect the final shape. The chapter presents AlphaFold as a machine-learning system that made major progress on this problem, potentially helping researchers investigate biological mechanisms and disease. Google DeepMind’s historical account records AlphaFold’s first-place performance at CASP13 in 2018 and the much stronger AlphaFold2 result at CASP14 in 2020. A predicted structure is a research resource, not a complete explanation of a protein’s biological role.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a protein’s three-dimensional structure matters. Distinguish a predicted structure from experimental evidence about function or biological effect.
Key point:
- Protein structure helps determine molecular interactions and biological function.
- A structure prediction supports research but does not by itself prove a clinical treatment.
Source links:
- Google DeepMind: AlphaFold
10. Q: How did AlphaFold change the practical possibilities for biological research?
A: Show answer
Before high-accuracy prediction became available, determining an individual protein structure could require substantial laboratory effort, time, and expense. AlphaFold made it possible to obtain useful structural predictions much faster for many proteins, helping researchers form hypotheses about function, binding, and biological pathways. DeepMind reports that AlphaFold2 was recognized at CASP14 as a major solution to the protein-folding problem and that later versions expanded the range of molecular interactions that could be modelled. The value is therefore not simply speed. It is the ability to prioritize experiments, explore previously difficult targets, and connect structural information with other biological evidence. Experimental validation and specialist interpretation remain important.
Study instruction:
- Choose a protein-related research question and show how a predicted structure could guide the next experiment without replacing it.
Key point:
- AI predictions can accelerate hypothesis generation and research prioritization.
- Predicted structure, experimental confirmation, and clinical efficacy are different kinds of evidence.
Source links:
- Google DeepMind: AlphaFold
11. Q: Why does a successful AI prediction not automatically establish a medical treatment?
A: Show answer
A prediction addresses a defined computational task, such as estimating a protein structure or identifying an image pattern. A treatment requires additional evidence about biological mechanisms, safety, dosage, effectiveness, patient differences, and outcomes. Even an accurate structural prediction may not identify the best intervention, show that a drug will work in humans, or establish that benefits outweigh harms. Clinical translation requires laboratory research, appropriately designed trials, regulatory review where applicable, and monitoring after deployment. The chapter presents AlphaFold as an important step in understanding proteins, not as a system that independently cures disease. This distinction prevents technical achievement from being overstated as a clinical result.
Study instruction:
- Trace the evidence pathway from a model prediction to a possible treatment. Identify at least three stages where additional evidence is required.
Key point:
- Prediction is one step in a longer scientific and clinical process.
- Do not confuse computational accuracy with demonstrated patient benefit.
Source links:
- Google DeepMind: AlphaFold
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
12. Q: Why does the chapter identify health care as an important area for AI development?
A: Show answer
Health care faces pressure from ageing populations, the growing range of treatable conditions, expensive equipment and services, and shortages of trained professionals. These pressures make better use of clinical information and scarce expertise attractive. AI may help by supporting image interpretation, identifying patterns in large datasets, monitoring patients between appointments, and reducing repetitive administrative work. However, the chapter does not establish that AI can solve health-care financing or workforce shortages by itself. The technology can also introduce costs for integration, validation, cybersecurity, training, oversight, and maintenance. Its value must be assessed against the actual clinical and operational problem it is intended to address.
Study instruction:
- For a health-care use case, identify the problem, the resource constraint, the proposed AI contribution, and the costs or risks that remain after implementation.
Key point:
- AI may improve the use of information and scarce clinical expertise.
- It is not a stand-alone solution to the structural costs of health care.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
13. Q: How do modern machine-learning applications differ from earlier medical expert systems such as MYCIN?
A: Show answer
MYCIN was a rule-based expert system that used encoded medical knowledge to support diagnosis of blood infections. Its performance attracted attention, but many early expert systems remained confined to research settings. Modern machine-learning systems often learn statistical patterns from large datasets, such as medical images, rather than relying exclusively on rules written by specialists. They also benefit from improved computing, digital records, connected devices, and more mature deployment infrastructure. The distinction is not that rules have disappeared: clinical systems may combine learned models with explicit rules, workflows, and professional judgment. Both approaches require careful validation and a clear account of what the system can and cannot decide.
Study instruction:
- Compare a rule-based expert system with a model trained on labelled medical images. Identify the source of knowledge, the update process, and the main validation challenge.
Key point:
- Expert systems encode rules; machine learning estimates patterns from data.
- Neither approach is safe merely because it performs well in a demonstration.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
14. Q: What is personal health management, and how can wearable devices support it?
A: Show answer
Personal health management uses information about an individual’s activity and physiology to help monitor health over time. Wearables such as smartwatches and fitness trackers can collect repeated measurements, including heart rate, movement, and in some devices temperature or electrocardiogram signals. A connected phone or cloud service can aggregate these readings and analyze patterns. The chapter describes the possibility of providing feedback, identifying unusual signals, or prompting a person to seek assessment. The usefulness depends on sensor quality, user behavior, model validation, and the clinical meaning of the signal. A consumer device’s wellness feature should not automatically be treated as a diagnostic service.
Study instruction:
- List the measurements available from a wearable you know. For each one, state what it can indicate, what it cannot establish, and when professional assessment is needed.
Key point:
- Wearables create repeated streams of personal physiological and behavioral data.
- Monitoring and diagnosis are different functions.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
15. Q: How does a wearable-health AI system turn sensor readings into a useful alert?
A: Show answer
A typical pipeline begins with sensors collecting raw signals, such as optical pulse measurements or accelerometer data. The device or a connected service cleans and segments the data, extracts relevant features, and applies a rule or trained model to identify a pattern. The system may then display a trend, issue an alert, or recommend a follow-up action. Each stage can introduce error: a sensor may be poorly positioned, motion may distort a reading, missing data may bias a trend, or the model may be unsuitable for the user. A useful alert therefore needs a defined intended purpose, tested thresholds, understandable messaging, and a route for the user to obtain appropriate help.
Study instruction:
- Draw the data pipeline from sensor to alert. Mark where noise, missing readings, incorrect assumptions, and false alarms can enter.
Key point:
- A health alert is the result of a multi-stage data and decision process.
- Reliability depends on the complete system, not only the prediction model.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
16. Q: How might wearable data help identify a possible heart rhythm problem such as atrial fibrillation?
A: Show answer
A wearable may collect pulse patterns or an electrocardiogram signal that can reveal irregularities warranting further assessment. An algorithm can screen the signal for patterns associated with atrial fibrillation and prompt the user to seek clinical review. The chapter discusses this as a potential way to detect a problem earlier than a person might otherwise notice symptoms. However, a consumer alert is not equivalent to a full clinical diagnosis. Signal quality, the device’s intended use, the user’s characteristics, and the confirmatory pathway all matter. A missed alert does not rule out disease, and an alert should be interpreted according to the device’s approved or validated purpose.
Study instruction:
- Write a patient-facing explanation of what an irregular-rhythm alert means, including what it does not prove and what follow-up should be considered.
Key point:
- Wearables can screen for signals that may warrant medical assessment.
- A device alert must be interpreted within its validated intended use.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
17. Q: How could a smartphone accelerometer help detect a fall, and what limitations should be expected?
A: Show answer
An accelerometer measures changes in movement and orientation. A fall-detection system can look for a sequence such as sudden acceleration, a rapid change in orientation, and a period of relative stillness. If the pattern meets a threshold, the device may ask whether the user needs help or notify a designated contact. The same pattern can occur during ordinary activities, and some falls may not produce the expected signal. Device location, whether the person carries it, battery state, connectivity, and user response all affect reliability. A fall detector should therefore be treated as one layer of support rather than a guarantee that every emergency will be recognized.
Study instruction:
- Create a simple fall-detection failure analysis covering false positives, missed events, device availability, connectivity, and escalation.
Key point:
- Movement sensors can identify patterns consistent with a fall.
- No single sensor-based alert guarantees emergency detection.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
18. Q: What does the chapter suggest about detecting early signs of dementia from smartphone behavior?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes preliminary research suggesting that changes in how a person uses a smartphone might reveal behavioral patterns associated with cognitive decline. Potential signals could include changes in routine, movement, interaction speed, or other usage patterns. The idea is that passive data might reveal changes before they are obvious to other people. The chapter presents this as early-stage research, not an established diagnostic method. A behavioral pattern may have many explanations, including changes in health, environment, device use, or personal circumstances. Any such system would require carefully designed studies, informed consent, protection against stigma, and clinical confirmation before it could influence care.
Study instruction:
- Separate the chapter’s research possibility from a clinically validated diagnostic claim. Identify confounding factors that could change smartphone behavior.
Key point:
- Digital behavior may provide research signals about changes in daily functioning.
- Preliminary correlations must not be presented as a dementia diagnosis.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
19. Q: Why is wearable health information especially sensitive from a privacy perspective?
A: Show answer
Wearables can collect intimate information continuously rather than during a single clinical visit. A record may reveal sleep, activity, heart rhythms, location-linked routines, and changes in behavior. When combined over time, these signals can expose health conditions or personal circumstances that the user did not intend to disclose. Risk increases when data are transferred to multiple services, retained indefinitely, used for purposes unrelated to care, or shared with employers, insurers, advertisers, or other parties. Privacy protection therefore requires more than a consent screen. It should include data minimization, purpose limitation, access controls, retention rules, security safeguards, transparency, and meaningful choices about secondary use.
Study instruction:
- Map the flow of wearable data from device to app, cloud provider, health professional, and any third party. Identify what each recipient needs and how access should be controlled.
Key point:
- Continuous physiological data can reveal highly personal information.
- Collect only what is necessary and control secondary use and disclosure.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- WHO: Six Principles for AI in Health
20. Q: What ethical concerns arise when insurers use wearable activity data to determine premiums?
A: Show answer
Activity-based insurance can reward behavior that an insurer considers lower risk, but it can also shift the burden of health, disability, work conditions, and social circumstances onto individuals. A person may be unable to meet a target because of illness, disability, caring responsibilities, unsafe neighborhoods, or limited access to exercise facilities. Data may also be inaccurate or incomplete, and users may not understand how a score affects the price of coverage. The chapter raises concern about a future in which access to insurance or public benefits depends on continuous monitoring. A fair design must examine proportionality, consent, discrimination, appeal rights, data accuracy, and whether participation is genuinely voluntary.
Study instruction:
- Assess an activity-based premium proposal using fairness, accessibility, data quality, transparency, and the ability to challenge an adverse decision.
Key point:
- Behavior-linked pricing can create unequal effects even when the data are technically accurate.
- Consent and fairness require attention to real-world choice and circumstances.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- WHO: Six Principles for AI in Health
21. Q: Why might making health-care access conditional on continuous monitoring threaten human autonomy?
A: Show answer
A monitoring requirement can become coercive when people must surrender intimate data to obtain an essential service or public benefit. A person may formally agree but have no realistic alternative. The arrangement can also pressure individuals to meet standardized targets that do not reflect disability, chronic illness, age, work patterns, or cultural circumstances. The chapter uses a hypothetical national health-care condition to illustrate the risk of turning a supportive technology into a mechanism of surveillance and behavioral control. Ethical governance should preserve human agency, protect confidentiality, explain how information is used, and avoid making access to essential care depend on unnecessary monitoring.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish voluntary wellness participation from a condition imposed on access to an essential service. Identify the safeguards needed in each case.
Key point:
- A nominal choice may not be meaningful when essential services are at stake.
- Health AI should protect autonomy, privacy, and equitable access.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- WHO: Six Principles for AI in Health
22. Q: How does machine learning analyze medical images such as X-rays, ultrasound scans, and retinal images?
A: Show answer
A supervised image model is commonly trained using examples that have been labelled by qualified reviewers. The model learns statistical patterns associated with the labels and then produces a score, classification, or highlighted region for new images. The chapter describes systems trained on normal and abnormal images and gives examples involving eye scans, tumors, and heart imaging. A model’s output depends on the quality of the images, the consistency of the labels, the clinical question, and whether the training data represent the population in which it will be used. Image analysis may help prioritize or support interpretation, but the clinical workflow must define who reviews the result and how uncertainty is handled.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a supervised-learning workflow for a medical-image task, from image collection and expert labelling through training, testing, and clinical review.
Key point:
- Medical-image models learn patterns from labelled examples.
- The intended clinical use and review process determine how an output should be acted upon.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
23. Q: What is the difference between image segmentation and image classification in a medical AI system?
A: Show answer
Image segmentation identifies and labels regions or structures within an image, such as boundaries, tissue types, or anatomical components. Classification assigns a label or score to an image or region, such as whether a particular abnormality is present. The chapter’s Moorfields example describes two networks: one to segment eye scans and another to support diagnosis. These tasks are related but not identical. Segmentation can help the diagnostic model focus on relevant structures, while classification interprets patterns in the processed image. Each task needs its own evaluation criteria, reference labels, and error analysis. A strong segmentation result does not automatically guarantee a correct diagnosis.
Study instruction:
- Compare the output of a segmentation model with that of a classification model. Specify one suitable performance measure for each and explain why the measures differ.
Key point:
- Segmentation marks where structures are; classification estimates what a finding represents.
- Each stage must be validated for its own intended purpose.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
24. Q: Why did the Moorfields eye-scan example use separate neural networks for segmentation and diagnosis?
A: Show answer
The first network was used to identify components of the eye scan, creating a structured representation of the image. The second network used scan examples to assess whether disease or abnormality was present. Separating these tasks can make the workflow more manageable because anatomical localization and clinical classification pose different learning problems. It can also allow errors to be investigated by stage: a diagnosis may fail because the relevant region was not segmented correctly, or because the diagnostic model interpreted the region incorrectly. The chapter reports that the system’s experimental performance was at or above human-expert levels, but such a result must be understood in the context of the trial design and the task being measured.
Study instruction:
- Build a two-stage error tree for an eye-scan system. Identify how a segmentation error could propagate into a diagnostic decision.
Key point:
- A multi-stage pipeline can divide a complex task into specialized subtasks.
- Overall performance depends on the interaction and validation of every stage.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
25. Q: How should an organization evaluate the performance of an AI system that detects abnormalities in medical images?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should begin with a precise intended use: which disease, image type, patient group, setting, and decision is the system meant to support? The test set should be separate from training data and should represent the intended clinical population. Reviewers should examine sensitivity, specificity, false-positive and false-negative rates, calibration, subgroup performance, and the consequences of errors in the actual workflow. Results should be compared with appropriate clinical standards and, where relevant, with human performance under realistic conditions. A high average score can conceal poor performance for a subgroup or an uncommon presentation. Evaluation must continue after deployment because equipment, populations, and practice can change.
Study instruction:
- Design a validation checklist for a medical-image model. Include intended use, independent testing, subgroup analysis, error costs, and post-deployment monitoring.
Key point:
- A model must be evaluated for the clinical decision it is intended to support.
- Average accuracy alone is not enough to establish clinical safety or usefulness.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
26. Q: Why are false positives and false negatives both important in automated diagnosis?
A: Show answer
A false positive occurs when a system flags an abnormality that is not present; a false negative occurs when it misses an abnormality that is present. False positives can cause anxiety, additional tests, unnecessary treatment, and increased workload. False negatives can delay diagnosis or treatment and create a false sense of reassurance. The relative consequences depend on the condition, the prevalence in the population, and the next step in the care pathway. Thresholds should therefore be chosen in light of clinical priorities rather than a single abstract accuracy score. Human review, confirmatory testing, and clear communication can reduce the risk of treating a model output as a final answer.
Study instruction:
- For one screening scenario, describe the likely consequences of a false positive and a false negative. Explain how the clinical pathway changes the preferred threshold.
Key point:
- Different error types create different harms.
- Thresholds should reflect the clinical context and the consequences of each error.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
27. Q: How can biased or unrepresentative training data affect a medical AI system?
A: Show answer
A model may perform poorly when the training data do not reflect the age, sex, ethnicity, disease stage, equipment, or care setting of the people who later use it. Differences in image quality or clinical practice can also become unintended shortcuts that the model learns. A system may appear accurate in a hospital where it was developed but fail at another site with different equipment or patient characteristics. The result can be unequal access to accurate diagnosis or a higher rate of missed conditions for some groups. Mitigation requires representative data where feasible, subgroup testing, documentation of limitations, clinician oversight, and monitoring for performance differences after deployment.
Study instruction:
- Identify at least four sources of dataset shift in a medical-imaging project. Explain how each could be detected before or after deployment.
Key point:
- Training data shape what a model learns and whose cases it handles well.
- Fairness requires evaluation across relevant populations and care settings.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
28. Q: Why is ongoing monitoring necessary after a medical AI system has been deployed?
A: Show answer
A model’s operating environment can change. New scanners, different patient populations, altered referral patterns, updated clinical protocols, or changes in data collection may reduce the relevance of the original training and validation results. A model may also be modified by its supplier or updated through a controlled process. Monitoring should track input quality, alert rates, errors, subgroup performance, user overrides, adverse events, and changes in workflow. The FDA emphasizes lifecycle management for AI-enabled medical devices, including development, validation, deployment, monitoring, maintenance, and modification. Organizations need a named owner, escalation thresholds, incident reporting, and a process to suspend or roll back a system when safety is uncertain.
Study instruction:
- Draft a post-deployment monitoring plan with indicators, review frequency, responsible roles, escalation triggers, and a suspension procedure.
Key point:
- Clinical performance can change when data and workflows change.
- Deployment requires ongoing governance, not a one-time approval.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
29. Q: Why does the chapter argue that AI should augment health-care professionals rather than simply replace them?
A: Show answer
AI can analyze defined data tasks quickly and consistently, but health care involves much more than interpreting a scan or assigning a risk score. Clinicians understand symptoms, history, social circumstances, patient preferences, treatment feasibility, and the consequences of different choices. They also communicate uncertainty, obtain consent, coordinate care, and respond to unexpected developments. The chapter argues that AI can free professionals from some repetitive work and provide an additional analytical perspective, allowing more attention to difficult clinical and human tasks. Augmentation is not automatically safe, however: clinicians need training, clear responsibility, access to the underlying evidence, and the ability to question or override an output.
Study instruction:
- Map a clinical workflow and mark which tasks may be supported by AI and which require professional judgment, communication, or accountability.
Key point:
- AI can strengthen selected clinical tasks without replacing the whole professional role.
- Human oversight is meaningful only when staff can understand and challenge the system.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
30. Q: Why is human judgment not an infallible benchmark against which AI should be compared?
A: Show answer
Human professionals can become tired, distracted, emotionally affected, or influenced by cognitive and social biases. Their performance can vary with workload, experience, and the quality of available information. AI systems may offer consistency and detect patterns that are difficult for a person to notice, but they can also reproduce bias, fail outside their training conditions, or produce confident errors. The appropriate comparison is therefore not “human perfect” versus “machine perfect.” It is a comparison of complete decision processes under realistic conditions, including the model, the professional, the available evidence, the workflow, and the consequences of error. The goal is to combine strengths while controlling weaknesses.
Study instruction:
- Choose a diagnostic task and compare human-only, AI-only, and human-plus-AI approaches. Identify the error risks and safeguards in each.
Key point:
- Humans and AI have different strengths and failure modes.
- Evaluate the combined decision process rather than idealizing either participant.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
31. Q: How can AI expand access to health expertise in communities with few specialists?
A: Show answer
AI-supported tools may help clinicians in underserved areas interpret images, prioritize cases, monitor patients remotely, or obtain decision support where specialist services are scarce. This could be valuable where the realistic alternative is delayed care or no specialist assessment. Yet access benefits depend on electricity, connectivity, device availability, language support, local training, maintenance, and a referral pathway for cases that need human expertise. A system that performs well in a well-resourced hospital may not transfer safely to a rural clinic. Equitable deployment therefore requires local validation, community involvement, accessible design, and investment in the health system around the model.
Study instruction:
- Assess a proposed AI diagnostic service for a remote clinic. Identify the infrastructure, training, referral, language, and maintenance requirements that must be funded.
Key point:
- AI may extend scarce expertise when supported by a functioning care pathway.
- Technology alone cannot remove structural barriers to health access.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- WHO: Six Principles for AI in Health
32. Q: What governance principles should guide the use of AI in health care?
A: Show answer
The World Health Organization emphasizes that AI for health should protect human autonomy, promote well-being and safety, serve the public interest, be transparent and explainable, support responsibility and accountability, and promote inclusiveness and equity. These principles translate into practical controls: clear intended use, informed consent where required, privacy and security protections, meaningful human oversight, evidence of safety and effectiveness, documentation of limitations, and mechanisms to challenge or report harmful outcomes. Governance should include patients and health workers rather than treating deployment as a purely technical procurement decision. Legal and regulatory obligations differ by jurisdiction and device category, so organizations must check applicable local requirements.
Study instruction:
- Turn the WHO principles into a governance checklist for a hospital considering an AI-enabled diagnostic device.
Key point:
- Health AI governance combines safety, autonomy, transparency, accountability, and equity.
- Ethical principles must be translated into operational controls and local compliance checks.
Source links:
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- WHO: Six Principles for AI in Health
33. Q: What should a hospital check before procuring an AI-enabled diagnostic product?
A: Show answer
The hospital should define the clinical problem and intended use before comparing products. It should examine evidence from independent testing, the patient groups and settings represented, known limitations, regulatory status in the relevant jurisdiction, data requirements, interoperability, cybersecurity, privacy terms, and the supplier’s maintenance and update process. The contract should clarify responsibility for incidents, model changes, service interruptions, data access, audit rights, and end-of-life support. The hospital should also plan staff training, workflow redesign, human review, monitoring, and a safe fallback if the system becomes unavailable. Procurement is therefore a clinical-governance decision as well as a technology purchase.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a procurement due-diligence checklist that separates clinical evidence, regulatory status, information security, contractual terms, and operational readiness.
Key point:
- A product’s technical performance is only one part of procurement suitability.
- The organization must be able to govern the system throughout its operational life.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
34. Q: What problem are autonomous or driverless cars intended to solve?
A: Show answer
The chapter begins with the human cost of road crashes and argues that automated driving may reduce risks associated with distraction, fatigue, impaired driving, and human error. It also identifies potential benefits such as smoother traffic flow, more efficient energy use, and new mobility services. These are potential benefits, not guaranteed outcomes. An automated system can introduce new failure modes involving sensors, software, cybersecurity, human supervision, and interactions with other road users. Whether a vehicle is safer must be demonstrated in the conditions in which it operates, using suitable evidence and independent oversight. The goal is not simply to remove a person from the driving seat but to manage the complete driving task safely.
Study instruction:
- List the intended safety and efficiency benefits of autonomous vehicles. For each benefit, identify one new risk that automation could introduce.
Key point:
- Automation aims to improve safety and transport efficiency.
- Benefits must be demonstrated against the risks of the complete system.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
35. Q: What are the main functional components of an autonomous-driving system?
A: Show answer
An autonomous-driving system typically needs to perceive the road environment, estimate its own location and motion, predict how other road users may behave, plan a safe route or maneuver, and control steering, acceleration, and braking. These functions operate in a continuous loop. Sensor data are interpreted to create an estimate of the current scene; the system then considers possible future states and selects an action. Safety monitoring must detect when the system cannot continue within its operating conditions. A failure in one component can affect the others: an object missed during perception may never be considered during planning, while an inaccurate location estimate can make an otherwise sound maneuver unsafe.
Study instruction:
- Draw the perception–localization–prediction–planning–control loop. Add one possible failure at each stage and the safeguard that should detect it.
Key point:
- Driving automation is a coordinated perception and action system.
- Safety depends on the interfaces and failure handling between components.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
36. Q: Why is perception a fundamental challenge for driverless cars?
A: Show answer
A vehicle must determine where it is, what objects surround it, how those objects are moving, and which parts of the scene matter for safe driving. Roads contain changing light, weather, road markings, unusual objects, occlusion, construction, and behavior that may violate normal expectations. The chapter identifies perception as the central technical problem because an automated car cannot plan safely if it misunderstands the scene. Modern machine learning helps interpret camera, radar, lidar, and other sensor data, but no sensor or model is perfect. The system must estimate uncertainty, use complementary information where possible, and enter a safe state when it cannot reliably interpret the environment.
Study instruction:
- Create a perception risk register covering lighting, weather, occlusion, unusual objects, roadworks, sensor faults, and uncertain classification.
Key point:
- Safe planning depends on an accurate and timely understanding of the environment.
- Uncertainty must be detected and managed rather than hidden by a confident output.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
37. Q: How can sensor fusion improve an autonomous vehicle’s understanding of its surroundings?
A: Show answer
Different sensors observe the environment in different ways. Cameras provide rich visual information, radar can estimate range and relative speed, lidar can provide detailed distance measurements, and GPS or other localization systems help estimate position. Combining these inputs can compensate for some weaknesses of individual sensors. However, fusion creates its own challenges: sensors may be misaligned, have different update rates, fail in different weather conditions, or disagree about an object. The system must assess data quality and uncertainty rather than assuming that more sensors automatically mean greater safety. Redundancy is useful only when the combined design can detect and handle common-mode failures.
Study instruction:
- Compare camera, radar, lidar, and localization data for a vehicle approaching a pedestrian in poor weather. Identify what each sensor contributes and where it may fail.
Key point:
- Sensor fusion combines complementary information about the driving environment.
- More sensors do not eliminate the need for calibration, fault detection, and uncertainty management.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
38. Q: What was the significance of the PROMETHEUS project in the history of autonomous vehicles?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes PROMETHEUS as a major European research program that ran from 1987 to 1995 and demonstrated a vehicle travelling between Munich and Odense and back in 1995. Human intervention was still needed, so the demonstration was not a fully autonomous commercial car. Its importance was that it showed what could be achieved with the computing and sensing technology of the period and helped generate innovations that later influenced driver-assistance systems. It also illustrated the long development path from controlled experiments to reliable everyday operation. A successful demonstration establishes feasibility under particular conditions; it does not establish universal road readiness.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the PROMETHEUS milestone and distinguish its proof-of-concept achievement from the requirements of a commercial autonomous vehicle.
Key point:
- PROMETHEUS demonstrated meaningful progress but still required human intervention.
- A research milestone is not the same as a fully deployable product.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
39. Q: What did the 2004 and 2005 DARPA Grand Challenges reveal about the pace of autonomous-driving progress?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the 2004 challenge as a difficult test in which none of the finalists completed more than a small portion of the course. In 2005, several vehicles completed the desert route, and Stanford’s Stanley won after travelling the course in just under seven hours. The contrast demonstrated rapid progress in perception, navigation, planning, and vehicle control over a short period. It also shows why carefully designed competitions can accelerate research: teams face a common objective and measurable conditions. Nevertheless, a desert course is not the same as daily urban driving, where pedestrians, traffic rules, unpredictable interactions, and rare events create additional complexity.
Study instruction:
- Compare the 2004 and 2005 challenge conditions and outcomes. Explain why the improvement was important but did not prove that urban driving had been solved.
Key point:
- The challenges accelerated technical progress through measurable competition.
- Success in a constrained course does not establish safe performance in every road environment.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
40. Q: Why was the 2007 DARPA Urban Challenge a different test from the earlier desert competitions?
A: Show answer
The 2007 Urban Challenge placed vehicles in a more complex environment involving intersections, parking, traffic jams, and road rules. The system had to do more than navigate from a start point to a destination across open terrain. It had to interpret road structures, interact with other vehicles, negotiate shared space, and make decisions that remained lawful and predictable. This highlighted the social and interactive nature of driving. A vehicle can be technically capable of steering and braking yet still be unsafe if it cannot anticipate other road users or communicate its intentions through normal driving behavior.
Study instruction:
- Identify five additional capabilities required for urban driving compared with a desert route. Include interaction, rules, and negotiation of shared road space.
Key point:
- Urban driving requires reasoning about other road users and traffic conventions.
- Navigation accuracy alone is not enough for safe operation in cities.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
41. Q: What do the six SAE driving-automation levels describe?
A: Show answer
The six-level framework distinguishes how much of the driving task is performed by the system and who remains responsible for monitoring the environment and responding to failures. Level 0 has no driving automation, although warnings or emergency interventions may exist. Level 1 assists with either steering or speed control. Level 2 can assist with both steering and speed but the human driver must continuously monitor. Level 3 performs the driving task under defined conditions while a human must be available to take over when requested. Level 4 performs the task within a limited operational area without requiring a human driver to supervise. Level 5 is designed to operate across all conditions and roadways without a human driver. The levels describe capability and responsibility, not a simple ranking of product quality.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the six levels by writing who monitors the road and who must respond when the system reaches its limits at each level.
Key point:
- Automation levels distinguish the system’s role from the human’s role.
- The operational conditions and fallback responsibility matter as much as the label.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
42. Q: How should Level 1 and Level 2 driver assistance be distinguished?
A: Show answer
At Level 1, the system provides continuous assistance with either steering or acceleration and braking, while the driver remains responsible for the driving task and must monitor the environment. At Level 2, the system can assist with both steering and speed control at the same time, but the driver still has to supervise continuously and be ready to intervene. Neither level means that the vehicle is self-driving. The distinction matters because people may infer too much from a vehicle’s ability to control multiple functions. The human monitoring obligation remains central at both levels.
Study instruction:
- Use two examples of driver-assistance features and classify them as Level 1 or Level 2. State explicitly what the driver must continue to do.
Key point:
- Level 2 adds combined steering and speed assistance, not independent driving responsibility.
- The driver remains engaged and attentive at Levels 1 and 2.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
43. Q: What changes when a vehicle moves from Level 2 to Level 3 automation?
A: Show answer
At Level 2, the human driver must continuously monitor the driving environment while the system assists with steering and speed. At Level 3, the automated system performs the driving task within its defined conditions, and the human is expected to be available to take over when the system requests it. This transition creates a demanding handover problem. The person may have been disengaged from active driving and may need time to understand the situation. The system must provide clear warnings and sufficient transition time, while the driver must understand the conditions under which the system can operate. Legal requirements and permitted uses vary by jurisdiction and vehicle system.
Study instruction:
- Write a handover scenario in which a Level 3 vehicle requests intervention. Identify the information the system must provide and the time-critical actions the human may need to take.
Key point:
- Level 3 changes the monitoring role but retains a human fallback requirement.
- Safe handover depends on clear system limits and realistic human response capability.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
44. Q: How do Level 4 and Level 5 automation differ?
A: Show answer
Level 4 systems can perform the driving task without a human driver within a limited operational design domain, such as specified locations, routes, or conditions. Outside that domain, the system must not assume it can continue normally; it needs an appropriate fallback strategy. Level 5 is intended to perform the driving task across all conditions and roadways without a human driver. The chapter describes Level 5 as the fully driverless ideal, but current NHTSA material makes clear that Level 4 and Level 5 technologies are not available for ordinary consumer purchase in the United States. A limited-area Level 4 service should not be advertised or understood as universally capable.
Study instruction:
- Compare a geofenced driverless shuttle with a hypothetical all-road Level 5 car. Identify the operational limits and fallback responsibilities of each.
Key point:
- Level 4 is bounded by a defined operating area or conditions.
- Level 5 implies universal driving capability and is a much stronger claim.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
45. Q: Why can the name of an automated-driving feature create unsafe expectations?
A: Show answer
A product name may suggest a higher level of autonomy than the system actually provides. The chapter uses Tesla Autopilot as an example of a feature that it regarded as Level 2, while the manufacturer emphasized that drivers had to remain attentive and ready to intervene. If marketing, media coverage, or informal conversation leads users to believe that a system can manage every driving situation, they may stop monitoring the road. The resulting risk is not only a technical failure; it is a mismatch between system capability and human expectation. Clear naming, warnings, training, monitoring, and enforcement of intended use are therefore safety controls.
Study instruction:
- Review the language used to describe a driver-assistance product. Rewrite any claim that could cause a user to mistake assistance for full autonomy.
Key point:
- Public understanding must match the system’s actual automation level.
- Misleading expectations can cause overreliance and reduce human supervision.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NTSB: Uber Automated Vehicle Crash Investigation
46. Q: What lesson did the 2016 Tesla Autopilot fatal crash illustrate in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a fatal crash in which the vehicle’s sensors and software failed to recognize a white truck against a bright sky, while the driver was relying on a system that still required attention and supervision. The broader lesson is that an automated feature may fail in a situation that is unusual for its training or sensing assumptions. A system’s ability to handle many routine miles does not mean it can safely handle every road configuration. The incident also illustrates why the driver’s role must be communicated clearly and why system designers should consider foreseeable misuse, monitoring limitations, and the consequences of a missed object.
Study instruction:
- Analyze the crash as a human–machine interaction problem. Separate the perception failure, the driver’s expected role, and the design safeguards that could reduce risk.
Key point:
- A system can perform well in routine conditions and still fail in an unusual scene.
- Driver supervision requirements must be realistic and consistently reinforced.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
47. Q: What did the NTSB investigation of the 2018 Uber test-vehicle crash find?
A: Show answer
The NTSB investigation states that the automated system detected the pedestrian several seconds before impact but did not accurately classify her or predict her path. The system design also prevented emergency braking for collision mitigation and relied on the safety operator to intervene. The NTSB identified the operator’s visual distraction as the probable cause and cited contributing factors that included inadequate safety risk assessment, ineffective oversight, automation complacency, and weaknesses in the testing organization’s safety culture. This is more complex than a simple claim that the vehicle’s AI alone caused the crash. It demonstrates that software, human supervision, organizational controls, and public-road testing arrangements interact in a safety-critical system.
Study instruction:
- Read the NTSB findings and build a cause-and-contributor diagram. Separate immediate events, system design, operator behavior, organizational controls, and regulatory oversight.
Key point:
- The Tempe crash involved interacting technical, human, and organizational failures.
- Safety responsibility cannot be reduced to the model’s prediction accuracy alone.
Source links:
- NTSB: Uber Automated Vehicle Crash Investigation
48. Q: What is automation complacency, and why is it dangerous in partially automated vehicles?
A: Show answer
Automation complacency occurs when a person trusts an automated system so much that they reduce attention, monitoring, or readiness to intervene. It is particularly dangerous when the system can handle routine situations for long periods but still requires human supervision. The operator may become bored, distracted, or overconfident and may not regain situational awareness quickly enough when a rare event occurs. The NTSB’s investigation of the Tempe crash identified inadequate mechanisms for addressing operator automation complacency as a contributing factor. Effective safeguards should therefore include system design that monitors engagement, realistic training, operational limits, fatigue management, and organizational supervision.
Study instruction:
- Identify how long periods of successful automation can change operator attention. Propose controls at the system, training, and management levels.
Key point:
- Routine success can encourage reduced vigilance when supervision is still required.
- Automation complacency must be addressed through design and organizational controls.
Source links:
- NTSB: Uber Automated Vehicle Crash Investigation
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
49. Q: Why is the safety-driver role difficult to perform during a Level 2 or test-vehicle operation?
A: Show answer
A safety driver may be expected to monitor a vehicle that performs many routine tasks automatically while remaining ready to take over immediately. This is cognitively difficult because the person receives fewer active driving cues and may not maintain a full mental model of the road. The role becomes even harder when the system’s limits are unclear, the handover is abrupt, or organizational practices normalize distraction. A safety-driver program needs clear duties, training, active monitoring, limits on distraction, escalation rules, and enough staff to manage fatigue. If the system requires a person to supervise continuously, the operation must be designed around the real demands of sustained human attention.
Study instruction:
- Draft a safety-driver job aid covering attention, permitted activities, handover response, fatigue, incident reporting, and stop-work authority.
Key point:
- A human fallback is not an effective safeguard unless the person can realistically perform it.
- Monitoring duties must be supported by training, design, and supervision.
Source links:
- NTSB: Uber Automated Vehicle Crash Investigation
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
50. Q: What is an autonomous-vehicle disengagement, and what can disengagement reports tell us?
A: Show answer
A disengagement is generally an event in which a human safety driver takes control from the automated system or the system stops operating in autonomous mode. Such reports can provide information about testing mileage, the conditions in which intervention occurred, and the system’s operational limitations. The chapter uses California reports as an indicator of how often test vehicles needed human intervention. However, a disengagement is not necessarily a crash or imminent collision, and the absence of a disengagement does not prove that a journey was safe. Definitions, reporting rules, operating conditions, and company practices can differ, so the data should be interpreted carefully.
Study instruction:
- Define disengagement for a test program and list the contextual fields needed to interpret each event, including location, weather, scenario, cause, and operator action.
Key point:
- Disengagement data provide one view of system limitations during testing.
- They are not a direct substitute for crash rates or comparable safety evidence.
Source links:
- California DMV: Autonomous Vehicle Test Miles and Disengagement Reports
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
51. Q: Why should disengagement rates not be used as a simple league table of autonomous-vehicle safety?
A: Show answer
A rate depends on what counts as a disengagement, which miles are included, the conditions in which vehicles operate, and how cautiously a company chooses to intervene. One operator may disengage early as a precaution, while another may use a different threshold. A vehicle tested on a mapped, low-speed route is not directly comparable with one operating in complex urban traffic. California’s reporting materials explain the scope and limits of the published testing data, and the DMV cautions that reports are not designed for simple company-to-company comparisons. A meaningful analysis needs context, exposure, incident severity, independent review, and consistent definitions.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short critique of a headline ranking autonomous-vehicle companies by disengagement rate. Identify the missing information needed before drawing a safety conclusion.
Key point:
- Metrics require consistent definitions and comparable operating conditions.
- Disengagement frequency alone cannot establish which system is safest.
Source links:
- California DMV: Autonomous Vehicle Test Miles and Disengagement Reports
52. Q: How should autonomous-driving safety be measured more comprehensively than by counting miles?
A: Show answer
Mileage provides exposure information, but safety evaluation should also examine the types of scenarios encountered, the severity of incidents, near misses, disengagements, emergency interventions, system faults, and performance across weather, road types, and vulnerable road users. Testing should include rare but foreseeable situations and independent assessment of the system’s response. Simulated testing can explore large numbers of scenarios, but simulation assumptions must be validated against real-world behavior. Public-road testing also needs incident reporting, safety-driver oversight, and clear stop criteria. The relevant question is not simply how far the vehicle travelled, but whether it operated safely within a defined domain and how it behaved when conditions changed.
Study instruction:
- Design a balanced AV safety dashboard. Include exposure, event severity, scenario coverage, disengagement context, and post-incident corrective action.
Key point:
- Mileage is an exposure measure, not a complete safety outcome.
- Safety evidence must include scenario diversity, severity, and independent review.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- California DMV: Autonomous Vehicle Test Miles and Disengagement Reports
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
53. Q: What is the long-tail problem in autonomous driving?
A: Show answer
Most driving situations are routine, but a small number of unusual events can have serious consequences. Examples include unexpected roadworks, unusual objects, unusual pedestrian behavior, emergency vehicles, damaged infrastructure, or combinations of weather and visibility conditions. These rare situations form a long tail: they may be too uncommon to appear frequently in training data but remain important for safety. A model may perform extremely well on familiar cases yet fail when confronted with a new combination of conditions. Human drivers can sometimes draw on broad life experience and improvisation, while an AI system may have limited ability to reason beyond its training and design assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Create five rare-but-plausible road scenarios that a test dataset might underrepresent. Explain how simulation, targeted data collection, and safe fallback behavior could address them.
Key point:
- Rare events can dominate safety risk even when routine performance is excellent.
- Testing must deliberately cover unusual conditions and combinations of hazards.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
54. Q: Why is generalization especially difficult for autonomous vehicles?
A: Show answer
Generalization means performing reliably on situations that differ from the examples used in training and testing. Road environments vary across countries, weather, road markings, vehicle types, construction practices, and human behavior. A system may learn correlations that work in one region but fail elsewhere. It may also encounter objects or events that were absent from its training data. Because driving decisions can cause immediate physical harm, the system must detect when it is outside its validated operating domain and respond conservatively. Generalization should be tested through diverse data, scenario-based evaluation, monitored deployment, and explicit limits rather than assumed from a high score on a narrow benchmark.
Study instruction:
- Choose a driving environment different from the one used to train a model. List the distribution shifts that could affect perception and planning.
Key point:
- A model’s performance in one environment does not guarantee performance elsewhere.
- Operational limits and safe fallback behavior are essential when generalization is uncertain.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
55. Q: What makes mixed traffic, with human-driven and autonomous vehicles sharing roads, difficult?
A: Show answer
Human drivers do not always behave predictably or follow traffic rules exactly, while automated vehicles may follow programmed rules in ways that seem unusual to people. Each group must interpret the other’s actions at intersections, merges, roundabouts, and congested roads. An autonomous vehicle may hesitate where a human driver expects a quick response, or a human may misread a cautious maneuver. These mismatches can create confusion even if each vehicle follows its own operating logic. Mixed traffic therefore requires systems to anticipate human behavior, communicate intentions through familiar driving cues, and operate conservatively when another road user behaves unexpectedly.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a merging scenario involving one autonomous vehicle and several human drivers. Identify the assumptions each participant might make and the safest interaction strategy.
Key point:
- Mixed traffic creates interaction risks between different driving behaviors.
- Predictability and communication are safety properties, not merely matters of convenience.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
56. Q: Why might autonomous vehicles be introduced first in mines, factories, ports, or other constrained environments?
A: Show answer
Constrained environments have more predictable routes, fewer unrelated road users, controlled access, and operating conditions that can be defined in advance. A mine truck may operate on known haul roads; a port vehicle may move within a managed facility; a factory vehicle may follow mapped routes with designated crossings. These conditions reduce the range of situations the system must handle and make it easier to supervise and maintain. The chapter uses such settings to explain why practical automation may expand through specific niches before becoming widespread on public roads. The limitation is that success in a controlled domain cannot be assumed to transfer to unrestricted traffic.
Study instruction:
- Select one constrained environment and define its operational design domain, including routes, speed, weather, access controls, human presence, and emergency procedures.
Key point:
- Constrained domains reduce environmental complexity and make validation more manageable.
- A successful niche deployment does not prove universal autonomous capability.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
57. Q: What is an operational design domain, and why should it be defined before deployment?
A: Show answer
An operational design domain describes the conditions under which an automated system is designed to operate, such as road types, geographic area, speed range, weather, lighting, traffic conditions, and infrastructure. Defining the domain makes the system’s claims testable and helps users understand when it should or should not be engaged. The system must detect when conditions are outside the domain and transition to a safe state or an appropriate fallback. Without a clear domain, operators may use the technology in situations for which it has not been validated. The domain should be documented, communicated, monitored, and revised only through controlled evidence-based change.
Study instruction:
- Write an operational design domain for a low-speed campus shuttle. Include at least six environmental or operational limits and a response for each limit being exceeded.
Key point:
- An explicit operating domain bounds the system’s safety claim.
- Use outside validated conditions requires a safe response, not optimistic extrapolation.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
58. Q: How could low-speed driverless taxi pods or shuttles provide an early public use case?
A: Show answer
A low-speed shuttle operating on a mapped route can avoid some of the complexity of unrestricted driving. The route may have known stops, limited speeds, designated crossings, and a controlled service area. These constraints can make the service useful for campuses, business districts, airports, or other defined settings. Yet a mapped route still contains pedestrians, cyclists, unexpected obstacles, weather changes, and emergency situations. The service needs clear rules about where it operates, how passengers request help, what happens when the vehicle stops, and how the operator responds to incidents. Low speed reduces some risks but does not eliminate the need for safety engineering.
Study instruction:
- Design a pilot for a low-speed shuttle. Specify route boundaries, speed limits, passenger support, pedestrian interaction, emergency response, and service suspension criteria.
Key point:
- A limited route can make early deployment more controllable.
- Low-speed services still require robust interaction and emergency procedures.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
59. Q: What are the potential benefits and limitations of dedicated autonomous-vehicle lanes?
A: Show answer
Dedicated lanes could reduce unpredictable interactions between human-driven and automated vehicles and provide a more controlled environment for testing or service. They might also be equipped with sensors, markings, or communication infrastructure that helps vehicles localize and coordinate. However, creating separate lanes requires road space, public investment, enforcement, and decisions about which users receive priority. Vehicles still need to enter and exit the lane safely, respond to obstructions, and handle emergencies. A dedicated lane may improve reliability within a defined corridor, but it does not solve every problem of autonomous driving or guarantee that the rest of the transport network benefits.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a proposed autonomous lane using safety, capacity, accessibility, cost, enforcement, emergency access, and effects on other road users.
Key point:
- Dedicated infrastructure can reduce some interaction uncertainty.
- Its public value depends on wider transport and equity considerations.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
60. Q: Could driverless taxis make private car ownership less attractive?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests that inexpensive driverless taxis could provide mobility without requiring individuals to own and maintain a vehicle. If vehicles are available on demand, shared fleets may reduce the need for private parking and could improve access for people who cannot drive. The outcome is not automatic. Costs depend on vehicle utilization, maintenance, insurance, energy, cleaning, remote assistance, fleet management, and regulation. Cheap rides could also increase total vehicle travel or replace walking, cycling, and public transport. The effect on congestion, emissions, and ownership therefore depends on pricing, land use, service design, and how the new service interacts with existing transport options.
Study instruction:
- Build a simple comparison of private ownership and a driverless taxi service. Include utilization, fleet costs, waiting time, empty travel, congestion, and public-transport effects.
Key point:
- Shared autonomous services could change the economics of car ownership.
- Net transport benefits depend on utilization and effects on total travel demand.

61. Q: Why is the transition to fully autonomous transport likely to occur gradually rather than all at once?
A: Show answer
The chapter expects adoption to begin in settings where routes and conditions can be controlled, then expand as technology, evidence, infrastructure, and public acceptance improve. A gradual approach allows developers and regulators to learn from real operations while limiting exposure to situations the system cannot yet manage. Different regions may adopt at different speeds because road design, legal frameworks, weather, vehicle fleets, and public expectations vary. The transition also requires changes in emergency response, insurance, maintenance, traffic management, and workforce skills. A technical breakthrough is only one part of a large socio-technical change.
Study instruction:
- Create a staged deployment roadmap from a private test site to a public service. Define evidence and approval gates between stages.
Key point:
- Autonomous mobility requires coordinated technical, regulatory, and social change.
- Progress should be staged around evidence and controlled expansion.

62. Q: How do the risks of AI in health care differ from the risks of AI in autonomous vehicles?
A: Show answer
Both domains involve high-stakes decisions, but the timing and form of harm differ. A medical model may produce a missed diagnosis, an unnecessary test, or a misleading risk score that affects care over time. An autonomous vehicle can make a physical control error within seconds, potentially causing a crash. Health AI therefore needs clinical validation, privacy protection, informed consent, and clear professional oversight. Driving automation needs real-time perception, safe control, operational limits, fail-safe behavior, and rigorous road testing. Both require accountability, monitoring, cybersecurity, and transparent communication about limitations. The shared principle is to govern the whole system rather than focusing only on the model.
Study instruction:
- Create a two-column risk comparison for one medical AI tool and one autonomous vehicle. Identify common controls and domain-specific controls.
Key point:
- High-stakes AI requires controls tailored to the harm pathway and decision context.
- Model accuracy alone cannot establish system safety in either domain.
