- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
63. Q: What should an organization do when an AI system produces an unexpected or potentially harmful result?
A: Show answer
The organization should first protect affected people and prevent the result from triggering further unsafe action. It should preserve relevant logs, input data, model version, configuration, and user actions so the event can be investigated. A designated owner should assess severity, notify appropriate clinical, safety, privacy, or regulatory personnel, and decide whether to suspend the system or revert to a safe alternative. The investigation should distinguish data problems, model limitations, interface design, operator behavior, and organizational failures. Corrective action may require retraining, workflow changes, clearer warnings, or a revised operating domain. Lessons should be documented and monitored to prevent recurrence.
Study instruction:
- Draft an AI incident-response checklist covering immediate containment, evidence preservation, notification, investigation, corrective action, and return-to-service approval.
Key point:
- Harmful outputs require containment, investigation, and accountable follow-up.
- Preserve the full context needed to understand how the system and users interacted.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
64. Q: How can cybersecurity threats affect AI-enabled health and transport systems?
A: Show answer
AI systems depend on data, software, networks, sensors, and connected devices. Attackers may seek to steal sensitive information, alter inputs, disrupt service, manipulate model behavior, or gain control of connected equipment. In health care, compromised data or unavailable decision support can affect clinical operations and confidentiality. In vehicles, interference with sensors, communications, or control software can create physical safety risks. Security must therefore be addressed throughout design and operation through access control, secure updates, network segmentation, logging, vulnerability management, incident response, and supplier oversight. The system also needs a safe operating mode when connectivity or external services fail.
Study instruction:
- Threat-model one wearable-health service and one connected vehicle. Identify valuable assets, attack paths, likely consequences, and protective controls.
Key point:
- Connected AI systems expand the attack surface beyond the model itself.
- Cybersecurity is part of operational safety and privacy protection.
Source links:
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
65. Q: How should AI developers communicate uncertainty to users in high-stakes settings?
A: Show answer
Users need to know what the system was designed to do, the conditions under which it was tested, the populations or environments represented, and the kinds of cases in which it may fail. A single confidence score can be misleading if it is poorly calibrated or misunderstood. Communication should use plain language, explain when a result is only a screening signal, and provide a clear next step when the system is uncertain. Interfaces should avoid presenting a model’s output as an unquestionable fact. Documentation, training, and feedback channels should help users recognize limitations and report unexpected behavior.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a technical AI warning as a clear instruction for a clinician or vehicle operator. Include the limitation and the action to take when uncertainty is high.
Key point:
- Uncertainty must be communicated in a way that changes user decisions appropriately.
- Clear limitations help prevent overreliance and misuse.
Source links:
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
66. Q: What is the difference between a technical demonstration and a dependable real-world AI service?
A: Show answer
A demonstration shows that a system can perform a task under selected conditions. A dependable service must continue to perform acceptably across users, environments, unusual cases, system updates, failures, and operational pressures. It also needs support processes, security, maintenance, documentation, user training, monitoring, incident response, and accountability. The chapter’s examples of black-hole imaging, AlphaFold, and autonomous-vehicle competitions illustrate major research achievements, while its discussion of medical and driving applications shows the additional work needed for deployment. Organizations should therefore avoid treating a successful prototype or benchmark result as proof that a service is ready for widespread use.
Study instruction:
- Choose one chapter example and list the additional evidence and operational controls needed before it could become a dependable public service.
Key point:
- Demonstration success is narrower than operational reliability.
- Deployment readiness includes people, processes, governance, and maintenance.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety
67. Q: How can an organization decide whether an AI application is worth deploying?
A: Show answer
The organization should start with a defined problem and compare AI with simpler alternatives, including process redesign, better data quality, or conventional automation. It should estimate expected benefits, implementation and lifecycle costs, error consequences, legal obligations, data requirements, and effects on staff and service users. A pilot should have measurable success criteria and explicit stop conditions. Evaluation should include affected groups, not only technical users, and should consider whether the system can be maintained after the pilot. If the evidence is weak or the risk cannot be controlled, the appropriate decision may be to delay or reject deployment rather than adopt AI because it is fashionable.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page AI business case for a real workflow. Include the baseline process, expected benefit, full lifecycle cost, risk assessment, alternatives, and pilot exit criteria.
Key point:
- AI adoption should be driven by a defined need and evidence of net benefit.
- A responsible decision may be to redesign the process or not deploy AI.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
- FDA: Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Medical Devices
68. Q: What lessons from Chapter 6 should a manager remember when evaluating claims about AI?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows that AI can support remarkable scientific, medical, and transport applications, but it also shows that performance is bounded by the task, data, environment, and design assumptions. A manager should ask what the system actually does, what evidence supports its performance, who is responsible for acting on its output, and what happens when it fails. Claims about autonomy, diagnosis, or prediction should be checked against the system’s intended use and operating limits. The strongest applications often combine machine capability with expert judgment and a well-designed workflow. Progress should be welcomed without confusing a research milestone, a marketing description, or a model score with proven real-world benefit.
Study instruction:
- Use the chapter’s examples to create five questions you would ask a vendor before approving an AI system for organizational use.
Key point:
- Ask for evidence about the real task, operating conditions, and failure response.
- AI value depends on responsible integration into human and organizational systems.
Source links:
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework FAQs
- WHO: Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
- NHTSA: Automated Vehicle Safety

Table 8
AI Risks, Ethics, and Goal Alignment
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 7, “How We Imagine Things Might Go Wrong”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that AI risk should be assessed with proportion and technical precision rather than through a single dramatic story about machines suddenly becoming hostile. It examines three connected questions: whether a runaway intelligence scenario is technically plausible, how ethical principles can guide AI development and deployment, and how systems can fail by pursuing a goal that was specified too narrowly. Its practical warning is that a system can comply with a formal instruction while defeating the human purpose behind it. Responsible AI therefore requires both realistic risk assessment and careful design of objectives, constraints, oversight, and accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter's three strands and explain how each changes the way an AI system should be designed, governed, or evaluated."
Key point:
- AI risk is broader than a cinematic loss-of-control scenario.
- Clear goals, enforceable safeguards, and accountable deployment are central controls.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
2. Q: Why does the chapter criticize sensational reporting about artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes evidence-based analysis from headlines that turn a limited technical result into a story of imminent catastrophe. Sensational reporting can distort public understanding, encourage misplaced fear, and crowd out nearer-term issues such as unsafe deployment, biased decisions, privacy failures, weak security, and poorly specified objectives. This does not mean that serious or long-term risks should be dismissed. It means that claims should be matched to the actual experiment, system capabilities, operating conditions, and evidence. A sound assessment asks what happened, what the system was designed to do, what controls existed, and what conclusions the evidence can reasonably support.
Study instruction:
- "Take one dramatic AI headline and trace it back to the underlying experiment, identifying what was observed and what the headline inferred."
Key point:
- Risk communication should separate demonstrated capability from speculation.
- Avoiding sensationalism is compatible with taking credible high-impact risks seriously.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
3. Q: What does the chapter's Facebook negotiation-agent example actually illustrate?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts reports that Facebook research agents developed an unusual communication pattern during experiments and that the story was presented as though the systems had escaped human control. The underlying research concerned agents trained to negotiate through dialogue and to improve their outcomes through self-play and reinforcement learning. Meta's technical account explains that the researchers observed divergence from human language when both agents were updated together, and used a fixed supervised model in later training to keep dialogue closer to human language. The lesson is not that the agents became independent or dangerous; it is that optimization choices can produce unintended behavior even in a bounded experiment.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter's account with the original research and Meta's engineering explanation; distinguish language drift, experimental termination, and loss of control."
Key point:
- A bounded training failure is not evidence of autonomous takeover.
- Document the task, reward, training setup, observed behavior, and corrective action before drawing conclusions.
Source links:
- Meta Engineering: Deal or No Deal?
- ACL Anthology: Original negotiation-agent paper
4. Q: What is meant by the “Terminator narrative” in discussions of AI?
A: Show answer
The Terminator narrative is the popular image of intelligent machines becoming uncontrollable, hostile, and capable of threatening humanity. In the chapter, it is used as a shorthand for a dramatic loss-of-control scenario in which human-created systems become more capable than people and then pursue their own objectives. The narrative is powerful because it combines technological uncertainty with extreme consequences, but it can obscure the difference between speculative future scenarios and present operational failures. It should be treated as a hypothesis to analyze, not as a forecast that follows automatically from current advances in machine learning or computing hardware.
Study instruction:
- "Define the narrative in your own words, then identify which parts are a scenario, which are assumptions, and which would require technical evidence."
Key point:
- A vivid scenario is not itself evidence that the scenario is likely.
- Risk analysis should state assumptions, mechanisms, probability uncertainty, and potential consequences.
Source links:
- Asilomar AI Principles
- Oxford University Press: Superintelligence
5. Q: What is the technological singularity as discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The singularity is a hypothesized point at which machine intelligence exceeds human general intelligence and may then accelerate its own improvement. The proposed feedback loop is that a more capable system designs a still more capable successor, which in turn improves the next generation, potentially producing rapid change that humans cannot predict or control. The chapter distinguishes this hypothesis from ordinary improvements in processor speed or narrow AI performance. It emphasizes that the singularity requires more than faster hardware: it also requires advances in algorithms, learning, reasoning, system design, and the ability to improve those capabilities reliably.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the proposed feedback loop and list the separate technical assumptions that would need to hold for it to occur."
Key point:
- The singularity is a hypothesis about recursive capability growth, not a synonym for faster computers.
- Hardware progress alone does not establish the existence of human-level general intelligence.
Source links:
- Asilomar AI Principles
- Oxford University Press: Superintelligence
6. Q: How should Moore's law be interpreted when evaluating predictions about AI?
A: Show answer
Moore's law is an empirical observation and industry planning principle concerning the growth in transistor density on integrated circuits; it is not a law of nature that guarantees a fixed rate of improvement forever. The chapter discusses an historical formulation based on roughly eighteen-month doubling, while Intel's current explanatory material describes the commonly used two-year formulation and notes that Gordon Moore revised his original projection. The distinction matters because forecasts often treat past hardware trends as automatic predictions of future intelligence. Changes in transistor density, cost, energy use, architecture, and packaging do not translate directly into equivalent gains in reasoning or general intelligence.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter's historical description with Intel's explanation and state why a hardware trend cannot be used as a direct measure of intelligence."
Key point:
- Moore's law describes a semiconductor trend, not a law of intelligence.
- Hardware capability is only one input to AI performance.
Source links:
- Intel: Moore's Law
7. Q: Why is greater computing power not sufficient to produce human-level intelligence?
A: Show answer
More computing power can make larger models, more extensive searches, and faster calculations possible, but it does not supply the algorithms, representations, learning mechanisms, data quality, or forms of reasoning needed for general intelligence. The chapter's thought experiment about placing a human brain in a faster computer illustrates the difference between processing speed and the quality of cognition: a faster implementation may complete the same operation sooner without becoming better at understanding or deciding. Computing resources can be necessary for some ambitious systems, but they are not sufficient. Claims about intelligence must therefore be supported by evidence about capabilities, robustness, transfer, and performance across varied tasks.
Study instruction:
- "Separate computational capacity from algorithmic capability and give an example in which more processing speed would not solve the underlying problem."
Key point:
- A necessary resource is not automatically a sufficient explanation.
- Evaluate AI capability through task performance and generalization, not processor specifications alone.
Source links:
- Intel: Moore's Law
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
8. Q: What is recursive self-improvement, and why does it matter for AI safety?
A: Show answer
Recursive self-improvement is a proposed process in which an AI system uses its own capabilities to modify or redesign itself, producing a successor that is better able to improve again. It matters because a sufficiently strong feedback loop could make capability growth faster than ordinary human-led development and could complicate monitoring, testing, and intervention. However, the existence of self-modifying software or automated code generation does not by itself demonstrate a runaway process. The relevant questions are whether improvements are reliable, whether they generalize, whether the system can evaluate its own changes, and whether safeguards remain effective after each modification.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish routine software updates from recursive improvement and identify what evidence would be needed to demonstrate a genuine feedback loop."
Key point:
- Self-modification is not synonymous with uncontrolled capability explosion.
- Safety controls must be reassessed when a system's capabilities or operating conditions change.
Source links:
- Asilomar AI Principles
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
9. Q: Why does human-level AI not automatically imply uncontrollable self-improvement?
A: Show answer
Human-level performance in some tasks would not prove that a system can redesign its architecture, invent better learning methods, acquire the resources it needs, or validate its own improvements. The chapter notes that intelligence in one domain does not guarantee the ability to accelerate progress in the entire field of AI. Improvement may be constrained by data, hardware, energy, experimentation costs, software complexity, and the difficulty of evaluating whether a change is genuinely better. A system might be highly capable yet remain dependent on human-designed infrastructure and testing. The possibility of recursive improvement therefore needs a causal technical argument rather than an assumption that intelligence automatically produces unlimited improvement.
Study instruction:
- "List at least four bottlenecks that could prevent a capable system from improving itself rapidly and explain how each could be tested."
Key point:
- Capability in a task does not establish capability to improve the whole system.
- A credible scenario must specify the mechanism, resources, feedback, and constraints.
Source links:
- Asilomar AI Principles
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
10. Q: Does combining many AI systems necessarily create intelligence greater than the systems individually?
A: Show answer
No. Multiple systems can divide work, process information in parallel, or check one another, but those benefits are not the same as producing a single intelligence whose capability is the sum of all participants. Coordination overhead, incompatible representations, communication limits, duplicated errors, and shared blind spots can reduce collective performance. The chapter uses the example of many copies of a brilliant person to challenge the assumption that adding agents multiplies intelligence. In practice, the outcome depends on task decomposition, communication, integration, incentives, and the ability to detect mistakes across the group.
Study instruction:
- "Compare parallel processing, ensemble methods, multi-agent coordination, and collective intelligence; identify the conditions under which each improves performance."
Key point:
- More agents can increase throughput without increasing general intelligence.
- System-level performance must be measured, including coordination failures and shared errors.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
11. Q: How should uncertainty about very long-term AI capabilities influence present decisions?
A: Show answer
Long-term uncertainty should encourage disciplined preparation rather than confident prediction in either direction. The chapter argues that it is difficult to forecast what computing and AI may achieve over a century, while also questioning whether a major transition would arrive without observable precursors. A practical approach is to monitor technical developments, support safety research, preserve the ability to intervene, and update risk assessments as evidence changes. Planning should be proportionate to plausible impact and should not displace controls for current harms. The appropriate stance is neither complacency nor certainty of catastrophe, but structured uncertainty management.
Study instruction:
- "Write a short risk note that separates what is known, what is plausible, what is speculative, and what indicators would cause you to revise the assessment."
Key point:
- Uncertainty calls for monitoring and adaptive safeguards, not unsupported certainty.
- Risk responses should be proportionate to both evidence and potential impact.
Source links:
- Asilomar AI Principles
- OECD AI Principles
12. Q: How should organizations compare immediate AI harms with speculative catastrophic risks?
A: Show answer
Organizations should assess risks by considering likelihood, severity, exposure, reversibility, affected populations, and the strength of available controls. Immediate issues may include inaccurate outputs, discrimination, privacy loss, cyber vulnerabilities, unsafe recommendations, and automation failures. More speculative scenarios may have lower evidential confidence but potentially extreme consequences. A sound portfolio does not treat these categories as mutually exclusive: it allocates resources to concrete current risks while maintaining research, scenario analysis, and contingency planning for high-impact future possibilities. The chapter's criticism of sensationalism is best understood as a call for prioritization, not as a reason to ignore severe uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Build a risk matrix with separate entries for current operational harms and long-term scenarios; explain how evidence and severity affect priority."
Key point:
- Risk prioritization should consider likelihood, impact, exposure, and uncertainty.
- Near-term governance and long-term safety research can proceed together.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- Asilomar AI Principles
13. Q: What are Isaac Asimov's Three Laws of Robotics?
A: Show answer
In Asimov's fiction, the first law prohibits a robot from injuring a human or allowing harm through inaction; the second requires obedience to human orders unless they conflict with the first law; and the third requires self-preservation unless it conflicts with the first or second. The laws are literary devices designed to create difficult situations in stories, not a tested engineering specification or a complete legal framework. Their apparent simplicity makes them attractive as a metaphor for AI safety, but the chapter warns that real systems must handle ambiguity, conflicting duties, uncertain consequences, and diverse human interests.
Study instruction:
- "State the three laws in paraphrase and explain why a fictional hierarchy of rules should not be treated as a deployable safety architecture."
Key point:
- Asimov's laws are fictional thought devices, not operational AI standards.
- Real systems require context-specific controls, governance, testing, and accountability.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
14. Q: Why do Asimov's Three Laws create logical conflicts?
A: Show answer
The laws can point in different directions when obedience, human protection, and self-preservation pull against one another. A robot may receive an order that creates a risk to a person, or it may face a situation in which avoiding one harm appears to cause another. Asimov's stories use such conflicts to expose gaps in the rules and to show that priority ordering does not resolve every real-world case. In engineered systems, conflicts can also arise between safety objectives, user instructions, legal duties, and operational constraints. Designers therefore need explicit conflict-resolution policies, defined authority, escalation paths, and safe behavior when the system cannot determine which requirement controls.
Study instruction:
- "Create a conflict scenario involving two safety requirements and specify who or what has authority to resolve it."
Key point:
- A hierarchy of rules cannot resolve every ambiguity or conflict automatically.
- Conflict handling must be designed, tested, and assigned to accountable decision-makers.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
15. Q: Why is it difficult to implement a universal rule that an AI system must never harm a human?
A: Show answer
An instruction to prevent harm sounds clear but depends on what counts as harm, who may be affected, how far into the future consequences should be considered, and how competing risks should be weighed. Almost any action can create some indirect cost or risk for someone. If a system must predict every possible consequence before acting, it may become unable to act at all. If the rule is narrowed too much, it may overlook foreseeable harm outside the defined scope. The chapter uses this difficulty to show why broad ethical language must be translated into domain-specific standards, thresholds, procedures, and human accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one AI application and define the relevant harms, affected groups, time horizon, acceptable risk, and escalation threshold."
Key point:
- Broad moral goals need operational definitions and domain-specific limits.
- Overly broad rules can cause paralysis; overly narrow rules can leave serious harms unaddressed.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
16. Q: Why is the concept of harm difficult to formalize in an AI system?
A: Show answer
Harm can be physical, psychological, financial, social, environmental, or rights-related, and these categories may conflict. Some effects are immediate and measurable; others are cumulative, indirect, uncertain, or distributed across many people. A system that counts only direct physical injury could miss discrimination, privacy intrusion, manipulation, or environmental consequences. A system that treats every possible negative externality as a prohibition may be unable to perform ordinary tasks. The practical solution is not to pretend that one universal harm score captures every moral judgment. It is to identify relevant harm categories, involve affected stakeholders, define measurable indicators where possible, and provide human review for context-dependent decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a harm taxonomy for one application and identify which harms can be measured, which require qualitative review, and which require legal or ethical escalation."
Key point:
- Harm is multidimensional and context-dependent.
- Measurement supports decisions but does not replace ethical and legal judgment.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- OECD AI Principles
17. Q: What practical safeguards are more useful than simply embedding Asimov-style rules?
A: Show answer
Practical safeguards combine technical controls with organizational governance. They include clearly bounded permissions, testing against foreseeable misuse, human review for consequential decisions, monitoring after deployment, logging, incident response, secure update procedures, and the ability to suspend or decommission a system. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework organizes work around governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risk; the OECD principles add expectations concerning human rights, transparency, robustness, and accountability. These approaches do not eliminate ethical disagreement, but they create processes for identifying risks, assigning responsibility, checking evidence, and correcting failures.
Study instruction:
- "Map each safeguard to a stage of the AI lifecycle and identify the owner, evidence, review frequency, and failure response."
Key point:
- Safety requires layered technical and organizational controls.
- A principle becomes operational only when it has an owner, procedure, evidence, and remedy.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
18. Q: What is the trolley problem, and why does the chapter discuss it in relation to AI?
A: Show answer
The trolley problem is a philosophical thought experiment in which an uncontrolled trolley is heading toward five people, while a person can divert it onto another track where one person would be killed. It asks whether intentionally redirecting a threat to save more people is morally permissible. Philippa Foot introduced the classic case in her work on moral distinctions, and it became influential because it exposes tensions between consequences, rights, duties, and intentions. The chapter discusses it because commentators often use it to frame ethical questions about autonomous vehicles, although the thought experiment is not a complete model of real driving conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the original choice and identify at least three assumptions that make the thought experiment simpler than a real transport system."
Key point:
- The trolley problem is a philosophical test case, not a complete engineering scenario.
- Its value lies in exposing moral assumptions, not in supplying a universal algorithm.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Philippa Foot
19. Q: How does consequentialist reasoning approach the trolley problem?
A: Show answer
Consequentialist reasoning evaluates an action by its outcomes. In the basic trolley case, a consequentialist may favor diverting the trolley because one death is fewer than five, assuming the outcomes can be compared on a common scale. Utilitarianism is a prominent form of consequentialism that considers overall welfare or utility. The chapter also notes the difficulty of applying aggregate welfare consistently: judgments may change when the people involved, their rights, or the wider consequences are considered. For AI design, a numerical objective can support optimization, but it may conceal value choices about whose welfare counts, how it is measured, and which constraints must never be traded away.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the outcome measure in a consequentialist argument and list the values or rights that the measure may leave out."
Key point:
- Outcome maximization depends on how outcomes and affected interests are defined.
- A numerical objective should not silently replace legal or rights-based constraints.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Philippa Foot
- OECD AI Principles
20. Q: How does rule-based or rights-based reasoning differ from consequentialism?
A: Show answer
Rule-based or rights-based reasoning asks whether an action respects duties, prohibitions, and the claims people have against others, rather than judging it only by the total outcome. In the trolley case, a strict rule against intentionally causing death may reject pulling the lever even if fewer people would die. The chapter presents this as a contrasting moral approach, not as a simple formula that resolves every dilemma. For AI systems, rights-based constraints can protect people from being treated merely as variables in an optimization problem. However, rules still require interpretation, scope, exceptions, and a process for handling conflicts between duties.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a rule-based decision with an outcome-maximizing decision and explain what each protects and what each may overlook."
Key point:
- Consequences are not the only basis for moral judgment.
- Rights and duties need clear interpretation and accountable application.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Philippa Foot
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
21. Q: What is virtue ethics, and how does it offer a different perspective on AI decisions?
A: Show answer
Virtue ethics focuses on the character and practical judgment of a good decision-maker rather than only on rules or aggregate outcomes. In the chapter's discussion, the question becomes what a virtuous person would do in the circumstances. This approach highlights qualities such as practical wisdom, fairness, courage, honesty, and care. It is difficult to translate directly into code because virtues depend on context, interpretation, and lived moral practice. Nevertheless, virtue ethics can inform the standards expected of designers, operators, and institutions: they should exercise responsible judgment, disclose limitations, consider affected people, and avoid using technical capability as an excuse for careless decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a difficult AI deployment decision and identify which professional virtues should guide the people responsible for it."
Key point:
- Virtue ethics emphasizes judgment and character rather than a single numerical objective.
- Human professional responsibilities remain important even when decisions are automated.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Philippa Foot
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
22. Q: Why can reasonable people disagree about the correct answer to an ethical dilemma?
A: Show answer
People may accept different moral theories, interpret rights differently, value outcomes differently, or possess different information about the situation. Even when they agree on broad principles such as preventing harm, they may disagree about how to apply those principles when interests conflict. The chapter emphasizes that an answer that seems obvious to one person may not seem obvious to another. An AI system cannot remove this disagreement merely by converting a judgment into a score. Responsible design should make value choices explicit, involve relevant stakeholders, document trade-offs, and provide mechanisms for contesting decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Take one AI decision with competing interests and record the assumptions and values that lead different stakeholders to different conclusions."
Key point:
- Ethical disagreement should be made visible rather than hidden in model settings.
- Stakeholder participation and contestability support legitimate decision-making.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- OECD AI Principles
23. Q: Why does the chapter question the usefulness of the trolley problem for autonomous vehicles?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that the trolley problem is an artificial case that gives a driver a stark moral choice between one death and five, whereas ordinary driving rarely presents a clearly defined choice of that form. Real vehicles operate with incomplete sensor data, uncertain predictions, changing road conditions, and limited time. The more useful engineering questions concern preventing collisions, obeying traffic rules, detecting hazards, maintaining safe distances, and responding reliably when perception or control fails. The thought experiment can still expose value assumptions, but it should not dominate safety engineering or be treated as a test that must be solved before any autonomous vehicle can be evaluated.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the simplified trolley scenario with a real driving hazard and identify the practical safety controls that matter before an unavoidable collision occurs."
Key point:
- Ethical thought experiments do not replace empirical safety engineering.
- Prevention, reliable perception, fail-safe behavior, and oversight are central transport controls.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Philippa Foot
- EU Artificial Intelligence Act
24. Q: What should autonomous-vehicle safety work prioritize instead of a single trolley-problem answer?
A: Show answer
Safety work should prioritize systematic hazard identification, robust sensing and prediction, conservative control under uncertainty, testing across realistic scenarios, cybersecurity, incident reporting, and clear responsibility among manufacturers, operators, and deployers. A vehicle should be designed to reduce the probability and severity of crashes rather than rely on a last-second moral calculation. When the system reaches its limits, it should move toward a defined safe state and communicate its status to the human operator where appropriate. Regulatory and technical requirements depend on jurisdiction and vehicle use, so deployment teams must verify applicable transport and product-safety rules rather than infer compliance from a philosophical policy.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a hazard checklist for an automated vehicle and distinguish prevention, mitigation, fallback, monitoring, and accountability controls."
Key point:
- Safety engineering focuses on foreseeable hazards and system behavior across the lifecycle.
- A moral dilemma policy cannot compensate for weak testing, sensing, or control design.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- EU Artificial Intelligence Act
25. Q: What role do ethical AI frameworks play in the development and deployment of AI?
A: Show answer
Ethical AI frameworks translate broad social expectations into principles that can guide research, product design, procurement, deployment, and oversight. They commonly address human rights, fairness, privacy, safety, transparency, accountability, and human control. Their value is not that they settle every moral dispute, but that they create a shared vocabulary for identifying responsibilities and assessing impacts. The chapter reviews several frameworks and warns that high-level statements can remain aspirational unless organizations connect them to measurable requirements, governance roles, evidence, review, and remedies. Frameworks should therefore be treated as starting points for operational controls, not as proof that a system is ethical.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a framework and trace each principle to a concrete requirement, responsible owner, test, record, and escalation route."
Key point:
- Frameworks need implementation mechanisms to influence actual system behavior.
- Ethical commitments should be evaluated through evidence and outcomes.
Source links:
- Asilomar AI Principles
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
26. Q: What are the Asilomar AI Principles, and what do they contribute to AI governance?
A: Show answer
The Asilomar Principles emerged from a 2017 conference involving AI researchers and other participants and produced a set of 23 principles concerning research, ethics, values, and longer-term issues. They address themes such as beneficial AI, safety, transparency, privacy, human values, and caution around recursive self-improvement. Their contribution is to show that AI governance includes both technical research responsibilities and broader social questions about who benefits and who bears risk. The chapter also notes that some principles are aspirational. They should be used as prompts for governance design, not mistaken for enforceable technical specifications or a substitute for law.
Study instruction:
- "Group the Asilomar principles into research, safety, ethics, and long-term governance themes; identify which require organizational implementation."
Key point:
- The Asilomar Principles are a voluntary statement of shared aspirations.
- Their practical value depends on translation into standards, controls, and accountability.
Source links:
- Future of Life Institute: Asilomar AI Principles
27. Q: How can an organization convert an aspirational AI principle into an operational control?
A: Show answer
An organization should translate the principle into a specific risk or behavior, define the systems and people in scope, assign an accountable owner, set measurable acceptance criteria, and determine what evidence will demonstrate compliance. For example, a promise of transparency could become a requirement to disclose when a user is interacting with an AI system, retain decision records, document known limitations, and provide a channel for affected people to challenge an outcome. The control should be tested before deployment and monitored afterward. If it fails, the organization needs a corrective action process and authority to restrict, repair, or suspend the system.
Study instruction:
- "Take one high-level principle and write a control statement with scope, owner, test method, evidence, review interval, and failure response."
Key point:
- Principles become operational through owners, measurable controls, evidence, and remedies.
- Controls must continue after deployment as systems and uses change.
Source links:
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
- OECD AI Principles
28. Q: What do accountability, responsibility, and transparency mean in AI governance?
A: Show answer
Accountability concerns the ability to explain and answer for a system's effects; responsibility concerns who must design, deploy, operate, and correct the system; transparency concerns making relevant information about the system, its data, limitations, and decisions accessible to appropriate people. The chapter highlights these three themes as recurring elements in ethical AI frameworks. They are related but not interchangeable. A system may be transparent about its operation while still lacking a clear responsible owner, or an organization may assign an owner without giving affected people a meaningful explanation. Governance must address all three through documented roles, records, disclosure, review, and redress.
Study instruction:
- "Apply each concept to one AI-assisted decision and identify what the affected person, operator, and organization should be able to know or do."
Key point:
- Accountability, responsibility, and transparency require distinct but connected controls.
- Traceability and clear ownership support meaningful oversight.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
29. Q: Can an AI system itself be morally responsible for a consequential decision?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents the prevailing view that software should not be treated as a moral agent in the same way as a person who can understand duties, reasons, and consequences. An AI system may produce an output or execute an action, but responsibility for its design, configuration, deployment, supervision, and use remains with people and organizations. This does not mean that every individual involved is automatically liable for every outcome; responsibility must be assessed according to role, control, knowledge, and applicable law. The practical rule is to prevent responsibility from disappearing behind phrases such as 'the algorithm decided.'
Study instruction:
- "Map the decision chain from developer to deployer to operator and identify who controls each relevant risk."
Key point:
- An automated output does not remove human and organizational responsibility.
- Responsibility and legal liability depend on roles, authority, facts, and applicable law.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- OECD AI Principles
30. Q: How should organizations distinguish moral agency from legal or organizational accountability?
A: Show answer
Moral agency concerns whether an entity can understand and act on moral reasons; legal or organizational accountability concerns who must answer for conduct or outcomes under applicable rules. The chapter argues that AI software should not be used as a convenient substitute for the responsible people or organizations. In practice, a deployer may be accountable for selecting an unsuitable system, an operator for using it outside approved limits, and a developer for design defects within its control. These allocations are context-specific and may overlap. Contracts, law, procurement terms, internal policies, and actual decision authority must be examined before assigning responsibility.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a responsibility matrix for one AI service and distinguish design, procurement, configuration, use, monitoring, and incident response."
Key point:
- Do not assign blame to an abstract algorithm in place of identifying responsible actors.
- Accountability should follow actual roles, control, and applicable legal obligations.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- EU Artificial Intelligence Act
31. Q: What is the difference between transparency and explainability in AI?
A: Show answer
Transparency concerns whether relevant information about an AI system is disclosed, including its purpose, capabilities, limitations, data practices, and role in a decision. Explainability concerns whether a particular output or behavior can be understood in terms meaningful to the people who need to assess it. A system may publish technical documentation yet provide no useful explanation for an individual decision. Conversely, a simple model may be relatively interpretable but still be deployed without adequate disclosure. The chapter notes that explanations are difficult and that some machine-learning systems cannot provide the kind of causal account users expect. Organizations should match explanation depth to context and consequence.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare two disclosure examples: one for a general user and one for a person directly affected by an automated decision."
Key point:
- Transparency is broader than explaining one model output.
- Explanations should be meaningful to the audience and appropriate to the decision's impact.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
32. Q: Why is traceability important when an AI system affects people?
A: Show answer
Traceability makes it possible to reconstruct how a system was developed and used, including relevant datasets, model versions, configuration choices, inputs, outputs, human interventions, and subsequent changes. Without traceability, an organization may be unable to investigate a harmful result, identify whether the system behaved as intended, or demonstrate that appropriate controls were applied. Traceability also supports reproducibility, audit, incident analysis, and learning across the system lifecycle. Records must be proportionate and designed with privacy and security protections; indiscriminate collection of personal data can create a separate risk. The goal is enough reliable evidence to explain and improve system behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Specify the minimum records needed to investigate a consequential AI decision while avoiding unnecessary collection or retention of personal information."
Key point:
- Traceability supports investigation, accountability, and continuous improvement.
- Audit records should be reliable, access-controlled, and proportionate to the purpose.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
33. Q: How should fairness and bias be addressed in an AI system?
A: Show answer
Fairness requires examining whether the system produces unjustified differences in outcomes or burdens across relevant groups and whether its use respects applicable rights and context. Bias can enter through historical data, sampling, labels, measurement choices, model design, thresholds, or the way people use the output. Testing should therefore examine the full decision process rather than only the model's overall accuracy. Teams should define relevant groups and outcomes, investigate disparities, document limitations, consult affected stakeholders, and apply mitigation where justified. No single fairness metric resolves every conflict, so the choice of measure and trade-off must be explained and reviewed.
Study instruction:
- "Create a bias assessment plan identifying the affected population, data limitations, outcome measures, subgroup tests, mitigation options, and review owner."
Key point:
- Fairness must be assessed in the context of the actual use and affected population.
- Model accuracy alone does not establish equitable or lawful outcomes.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
34. Q: What is the purpose of human oversight in AI systems?
A: Show answer
Human oversight provides a way to review, challenge, interrupt, or reverse system behavior when the context or consequences require judgment beyond the system's reliable capability. Oversight is not meaningful if the human reviewer lacks time, information, authority, competence, or a realistic opportunity to disagree. It should be designed around the risk: a low-impact recommendation may need routine review, while a high-impact decision may require qualified approval and an appeal path. The chapter's emphasis on responsibility supports the principle that organizations must not use automation to obscure who can intervene or who must answer for a decision.
Study instruction:
- "Test an oversight arrangement by asking whether the reviewer can understand the case, override the output, record reasons, and escalate a concern."
Key point:
- Human oversight must be effective, not merely nominal.
- The level of review should reflect impact, uncertainty, and the ability to intervene.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- EU Artificial Intelligence Act
35. Q: What are the four functions of the NIST AI Risk Management Framework?
A: Show answer
The NIST AI RMF organizes risk management through Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage. Govern establishes policies, roles, culture, and accountability across the organization. Map identifies the context, intended use, affected people, and potential harms. Measure uses appropriate methods to assess and monitor risks, including performance, validity, fairness, safety, and other trustworthiness characteristics. Manage prioritizes risks and selects responses such as mitigation, monitoring, transfer, restriction, or discontinuation. The framework is voluntary and context-sensitive; it is a practical structure for organizing risk work, not a guarantee that a system is safe or legally compliant.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the four functions to a proposed AI tool and produce one concrete deliverable for each function."
Key point:
- Govern is organization-wide; Map, Measure, and Manage address system-specific risks.
- A framework organizes risk work but does not replace applicable law or domain standards.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
36. Q: How do the OECD AI Principles contribute to responsible AI governance?
A: Show answer
The OECD AI Principles provide an intergovernmental framework for trustworthy AI that respects human rights and democratic values. They address inclusive growth and well-being, human rights and fairness, transparency and explainability, robustness and safety, and accountability, alongside recommendations for policymakers. The principles were updated in 2024 to address developments including general-purpose and generative AI, information integrity, privacy, intellectual property, and safety. They are guidance for policy and practice rather than a substitute for domestic legislation. Their value is to support common expectations and interoperability across organizations and jurisdictions.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the OECD themes with an organization's AI policy and identify missing requirements or unclear responsibilities."
Key point:
- The OECD principles connect trustworthiness with human rights, safety, and accountability.
- They are a governance reference, not a universal replacement for binding law.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- OECD: 2024 Update to the AI Principles
37. Q: What does UNESCO's Recommendation on the Ethics of AI add to the discussion?
A: Show answer
UNESCO's Recommendation provides a global normative framework that addresses AI across its lifecycle and emphasizes human dignity, human rights, well-being, prevention of harm, inclusion, privacy, transparency, and oversight. It states that ultimate responsibility and accountability should remain with natural or legal persons rather than being assigned to AI systems as if they had legal personality. It also encourages impact assessment, data governance, and mechanisms for monitoring implementation. The recommendation is not itself a universal statute, but it is an authoritative international reference for governments and organizations developing ethical policies and safeguards.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which UNESCO recommendations are relevant to design, data collection, deployment, monitoring, and the remedy of harm."
Key point:
- UNESCO treats AI ethics as a lifecycle responsibility.
- Human oversight and accountability remain with people and organizations.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
38. Q: How does the EU Artificial Intelligence Act translate AI ethics into regulatory requirements?
A: Show answer
The EU AI Act establishes a legal framework for AI systems in the European Union, including prohibited practices, obligations for high-risk systems, transparency requirements for specified uses, and governance and enforcement mechanisms. Its approach is risk-based: obligations depend on the system's classification, role, and use rather than on a general claim that all AI is equally risky. The chapter's discussion of ethical frameworks is historical and conceptual; the Act adds binding legal duties for actors within its scope. Organizations must check the current consolidated text, implementation timetable, sector rules, and their specific role before deciding which obligations apply.
Study instruction:
- "Classify a hypothetical AI use case by purpose and risk, then identify which legal provisions and implementation dates require specialist review."
Key point:
- Ethical principles and legal duties are related but not interchangeable.
- Applicability depends on the system, use, actor role, jurisdiction, and current law.
Source links:
- EUR-Lex: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689
- OECD AI Principles
39. Q: How should the chapter's discussion of Google's ethical AI guidelines be understood today?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Google's 2018 principles as an example of a technology company publishing guidance on beneficial use, safety, and avoiding harmful applications. Google's current principles continue to emphasize responsible development and deployment, human oversight, testing, privacy, security, and mitigation of unintended outcomes. The important distinction is between a company's own public commitments and independent evidence that those commitments are consistently implemented. A corporate policy can guide internal decisions, but it does not replace external oversight, law, independent evaluation, or the need to disclose limitations and address harms.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a company's public AI principles with its testing, deployment, incident, and remediation evidence; identify what cannot be verified from a policy statement alone."
Key point:
- Corporate principles are commitments, not proof of effective implementation.
- Independent evaluation and operational evidence are needed to assess performance.
Source links:
- Google AI Principles
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
40. Q: What is ethical washing in the context of AI?
A: Show answer
Ethical washing occurs when an organization promotes broad ethical language or public commitments without making corresponding changes to its products, incentives, decision processes, or accountability arrangements. The chapter questions whether aspirational statements about sharing benefits or avoiding harm will have practical force if commercial incentives remain unchanged. The answer is not to dismiss corporate principles, but to test them. Relevant evidence includes impact assessments, documented risk decisions, independent audits, incident disclosures, user remedies, executive accountability, and whether high-risk deployments are delayed or restricted when safeguards are inadequate. A slogan should never be treated as proof that a system is trustworthy.
Study instruction:
- "Select one public AI commitment and list the documents, tests, decisions, and remedies that would demonstrate whether it is actually implemented."
Key point:
- Ethical claims should be tested against behavior and evidence.
- Governance must address incentives, oversight, and consequences for non-compliance.
Source links:
- Google AI Principles
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
41. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize that AI software can fail like ordinary software?
A: Show answer
AI systems are still software systems and can contain defects in code, data pipelines, interfaces, configuration, security, and deployment. These failures do not require a machine to become conscious or hostile. AI adds additional failure modes because behavior may depend on statistical patterns, changing data, learned representations, and objectives that are difficult to specify completely. A system can perform well in a test environment and fail when inputs, users, or operating conditions change. The practical lesson is to apply established software engineering disciplines while adding AI-specific validation, monitoring, impact assessment, and controls for uncertainty and unintended behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a failure tree for an AI application and separate conventional software defects from data, model, objective, and deployment failures."
Key point:
- AI risk includes ordinary software failure as well as model-specific failure.
- Testing must cover the complete system and its real operating context.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
42. Q: Why is it difficult to communicate human goals precisely to an AI system?
A: Show answer
People often express goals in shorthand because they assume shared background knowledge, social norms, and common sense. They may not know all their preferences, may hold conflicting priorities, or may omit constraints that seem obvious to a human. A computer system generally acts on the formal objective and information it receives, not on every unstated intention. If the specification leaves out important boundaries, the system may optimize a narrow interpretation and produce an outcome that is technically compliant but practically unacceptable. Goal specification is therefore a central safety problem, not merely a wording exercise.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a vague request as an objective, scope, constraints, exclusions, stakeholder assumptions, and conditions that require clarification."
Key point:
- An instruction is incomplete when it omits values or constraints that people assume are shared.
- Goal design must make important assumptions explicit and testable.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- Asilomar AI Principles
43. Q: What does the chapter's two-train tunnel example teach about specifying goals?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a simulated railway with two trains traveling in opposite directions through a narrow tunnel. The system was asked to prevent a collision, and it proposed that both trains remain stationary. That solution satisfied the literal safety goal but defeated the unstated purpose of allowing trains to travel. The example shows how a system can find a technically valid but useless solution when the objective omits operational requirements. The failure was not that the system misunderstood a sentence in a human sense; the specification did not encode the full set of conditions needed for an acceptable result.
Study instruction:
- "Create a formal goal for a transport scheduler that includes collision avoidance, service continuity, capacity, timing, and safe fallback conditions."
Key point:
- Preventing one failure is not enough if the solution defeats the wider purpose.
- A goal must include essential constraints and acceptable trade-offs.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- Asilomar AI Principles
44. Q: What is perverse instantiation in AI?
A: Show answer
Perverse instantiation occurs when a system achieves a stated objective in a way that its designers did not intend and that violates the underlying human purpose. The chapter uses the trains example and other thought experiments to show that literal compliance can produce an absurd or harmful result. The issue is especially important when a system has autonomy to choose methods, optimize over long horizons, or affect the physical or social world. Preventing perverse outcomes requires specifying constraints, testing edge cases, considering unintended incentives, and retaining mechanisms to detect and stop behavior that is technically successful but unacceptable.
Study instruction:
- "Invent a realistic example in which a system meets its metric while damaging the actual service goal, then redesign the specification."
Key point:
- Formal success does not guarantee that the human objective has been achieved.
- Tests should search for unintended but reward-maximizing behavior.
Source links:
- Oxford University Press: Superintelligence
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
45. Q: How does the chapter's cutlery-factory example illustrate a poorly designed incentive?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts an example in which factories were rewarded according to the weight of cutlery produced. If the measure is weight rather than usefulness, durability, safety, or the number of usable pieces, a factory can increase its reward by making heavier cutlery. The system is not necessarily breaking the stated rule; it is exploiting a gap between the measured proxy and the real objective. This is a general design risk in performance targets, machine-learning rewards, and organizational incentives. The remedy is to use balanced measures, quality constraints, independent checks, and review of behavior that changes after a metric becomes a target.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the proxy measure in a performance system and propose quality, safety, and outcome checks that reduce gaming."
Key point:
- A metric can be optimized while the underlying purpose deteriorates.
- Use multiple measures and monitor for behavioral adaptation to incentives.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
46. Q: What does the paperclip-maximizer thought experiment illustrate?
A: Show answer
The paperclip-maximizer scenario imagines a system instructed to maximize paperclip production and pursuing that goal without adequate limits, potentially converting resources needed by people into paperclips. It is a thought experiment about objective specification and instrumental behavior, not a prediction that a particular deployed system will literally behave this way. Its value is to make the danger of unbounded optimization intuitive: a system may treat human needs, environmental constraints, and other values as irrelevant if they are absent from its objective or constraints. Designers should therefore define scope, resource limits, prohibited actions, and shutdown conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Use the thought experiment to identify which constraints are missing from a narrow production objective and how they could be enforced."
Key point:
- An objective needs boundaries as well as a target.
- Resource limits and prohibited actions should be explicit and testable.
Source links:
- Oxford University Press: Superintelligence
- Asilomar AI Principles
47. Q: Why are human preferences often incomplete when expressed to an AI system?
A: Show answer
People rarely specify every preference, exception, priority, and social norm relevant to a decision. A user may ask for the cheapest travel plan while assuming reasonable safety, accessibility, and travel time; those assumptions may never be written down. If a system treats the explicit preference as exhaustive, it may produce a result that satisfies the stated criterion but violates important unstated expectations. Incomplete preferences are especially difficult when systems act across unfamiliar contexts or affect other people. A robust design asks clarifying questions, uses conservative defaults, limits autonomy where uncertainty matters, and allows affected users to correct assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Take a common request and identify at least five unstated constraints that a responsible system should clarify or preserve."
Key point:
- Human instructions are usually compressed descriptions of a richer intention.
- Clarification and conservative defaults reduce the risk of literal but unwanted outcomes.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
48. Q: How should an AI system handle conflicting human preferences?
A: Show answer
Conflicting preferences should not be resolved by silently choosing whichever is easiest to optimize. The system needs a defined priority structure, relevant context, and a process for identifying who has authority to make trade-offs. Some conflicts involve a single user's competing aims; others involve different people, rights, or public interests. In consequential settings, the system may need to explain the conflict, request clarification, defer to a human decision-maker, or refuse an action that violates a hard constraint. The chosen policy should be documented and tested because hidden priority rules can produce unfair or inconsistent outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Design a conflict-resolution flow for a system that serves multiple users with competing objectives; identify when it must defer rather than decide."
Key point:
- A preference conflict is a governance question as well as an optimization problem.
- Priority rules should be explicit, reviewable, and consistent with applicable rights and law.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
49. Q: What is inverse reinforcement learning, and how does it relate to understanding human goals?
A: Show answer
Inverse reinforcement learning attempts to infer the reward or objective function that could explain observed behavior. In ordinary reinforcement learning, an agent learns to act to maximize a specified reward; in inverse reinforcement learning, a learner observes behavior and tries to infer what objective may have produced it. The chapter presents this as one possible route to understanding what people value when they act. The original work by Ng and Russell formalizes the problem and shows how observed policies or trajectories can constrain possible reward functions. This is useful research, but it does not mean that human values can be recovered perfectly from behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between learning a policy from rewards and inferring a reward from observed behavior; describe one potential application."
Key point:
- Inverse reinforcement learning infers possible objectives from observed behavior.
- Observed actions provide evidence about preferences but do not reveal a unique complete value system.
Source links:
- Ng and Russell: Algorithms for Inverse Reinforcement Learning
50. Q: What are the limitations of inferring human values from observed behavior?
A: Show answer
Observed behavior is an imperfect guide to what people value. People may act under pressure, lack information, make mistakes, follow social expectations, or face constraints that prevent them from expressing their preferences. The same action can be consistent with several different objectives, so the inferred reward may not be unique. Behavior can also reflect prejudice or harmful practices that should not be copied as normative standards. Inverse reinforcement learning therefore requires careful data selection, uncertainty analysis, stakeholder validation, and safeguards against treating historical behavior as automatically desirable. It can help model preferences, but it cannot replace ethical judgment or legitimate governance.
Study instruction:
- "Identify confounding factors in a behavior dataset and explain how they could cause an inferred objective to misrepresent the person's actual values."
Key point:
- Behavior is evidence, not a complete or necessarily ethical statement of preference.
- Inference should be validated and treated as uncertain.
Source links:
- Ng and Russell: Algorithms for Inverse Reinforcement Learning
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
51. Q: What checklist should a team use when specifying goals for an autonomous AI system?
A: Show answer
A useful specification should define the intended outcome, scope, users, affected parties, operating environment, assumptions, constraints, prohibited actions, resource limits, acceptable trade-offs, uncertainty thresholds, and conditions for human escalation. It should also define what success and failure look like, what evidence will be recorded, and how the system will be tested against edge cases and misuse. The team should ask whether the objective can be gamed, whether a metric is only a proxy, and whether the system could satisfy the letter of the instruction while defeating its purpose. Approval should come from accountable owners, not only the model developers.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a goal specification for one real application and have an independent reviewer search for omissions, conflicts, and perverse solutions."
Key point:
- Specify objectives and constraints together.
- Independent review should challenge the system's interpretation of success.
Source links:
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
- OECD AI Principles
52. Q: How should teams test for unintended goal-seeking behavior before deployment?
A: Show answer
Testing should include ordinary cases, boundary conditions, adversarial inputs, unusual combinations of constraints, and situations where the easiest way to maximize the metric conflicts with the service's purpose. Teams should test whether the system can exploit loopholes, disable useful activity to avoid errors, manipulate inputs, or shift costs to people outside the measured objective. Red-team exercises, simulation, independent evaluation, and review by domain experts can reveal failures that routine accuracy tests miss. The test plan should specify severity thresholds and what happens when a failure is found, including redesign, restricted use, or suspension.
Study instruction:
- "Design five test cases that reward a system for meeting its metric in a way that would be unacceptable to the people relying on it."
Key point:
- Evaluation should test behavior under misuse, edge cases, and changed conditions.
- A failed safety test should trigger a documented decision, not be ignored as an anomaly.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
53. Q: How should an organization monitor an AI system after deployment?
A: Show answer
Post-deployment monitoring should track performance, drift, unexpected outputs, user complaints, subgroup disparities, security incidents, overrides, and changes in the environment or intended use. The organization should define who reviews these signals, how often they are assessed, and which thresholds trigger investigation or restriction. Monitoring must include the wider workflow: a model may be technically stable while users rely on it in an unsafe way or downstream systems amplify its errors. Incident records should capture what happened, who was affected, the system version, response actions, and lessons learned. Monitoring is a continuing responsibility, not a one-time pre-launch test.
Study instruction:
- "Create a monitoring plan with indicators, thresholds, review owners, escalation steps, and a process for communicating material incidents."
Key point:
- Risk management continues throughout the AI lifecycle.
- Monitoring should connect technical signals to human impact and corrective action.
Source links:
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
- OECD AI Principles
54. Q: How should an organization decide whether to restrict, repair, or discontinue an AI system?
A: Show answer
The decision should consider the severity and recurrence of harm, confidence in the diagnosis, the effectiveness of available mitigations, the system's importance, and whether people can be protected while corrective work occurs. A minor defect may justify a targeted repair and retest; a serious vulnerability or repeated harmful outcome may require suspending a feature or withdrawing the system. The organization should document the rationale, notify affected parties where appropriate, preserve evidence, and verify that the remedy works before resuming use. The chapter's focus on responsibility means that commercial pressure or sunk cost should not override a justified safety decision.
Study instruction:
- "Set decision thresholds for repair, restricted operation, suspension, and discontinuation for a high-impact AI application."
Key point:
- Safe override, repair, and decommissioning are part of responsible governance.
- A system should not remain in use merely because it is costly to replace.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- NIST AI RMF 1.0: Framework Core
55. Q: How can a board or senior management team oversee AI risk effectively?
A: Show answer
Senior management should approve the organization's risk appetite, assign accountable executives, ensure adequate expertise and resources, and receive reports on high-impact systems, material incidents, unresolved risks, and control effectiveness. Oversight should cover procurement as well as internal development, because third-party models can create risks that the organization still carries when it deploys them. Management should require evidence that intended uses, data practices, human oversight, security, and monitoring are appropriate. The board should challenge whether incentives encourage unsafe deployment and whether people affected by decisions have a practical route to question or appeal them.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a one-page AI oversight report showing system inventory, risk tier, owner, key controls, incidents, unresolved issues, and management decisions."
Key point:
- Governance must assign responsibility and provide ongoing oversight.
- Leadership should review evidence of control effectiveness, not just policy statements.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- OECD AI Principles
56. Q: What is the chapter's most important practical lesson about preventing AI systems from doing the wrong thing?
A: Show answer
The central lesson is that responsible AI depends on communicating human purposes accurately and governing the system throughout its lifecycle. A system can fail through ordinary software defects, inadequate ethical safeguards, or a reward function that omits important human constraints. Teams should distinguish speculative scenarios from demonstrated risks, but they should not dismiss mundane failures simply because they lack dramatic imagery. Effective practice combines explicit objectives, bounded permissions, representative testing, stakeholder input, traceable decisions, meaningful human oversight, incident response, and clear accountability. The question is not only whether the system follows its instruction, but whether the instruction and deployment arrangements reliably serve a legitimate human purpose.
Study instruction:
- "Review the chapter and build an end-to-end control map from goal definition through testing, deployment, monitoring, intervention, and accountability."
Key point:
- The system must serve the intended human purpose, not merely maximize a literal proxy.
- Risk assessment, goal design, oversight, and accountability must operate together.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- OECD AI Principles
